Fluid mechanics

Use momentum and energy balances to predict pipe losses, boundary-layer drag, work in a pump and the flow limits of a compressible nozzle.

Each volume develops its arguments in sequence, with worked problems and links to experiments.

  • Momentum, motion and transport in fluids

    Derive a flow from its conservation laws and boundary conditions. Quantify how length scales and material properties decide which approximation can predict a measurement.

Advanced study across the subject

Each sequence connects the course to detailed chapters in neighbouring subjects. The complete course PDF includes their derivations, examples and exercises.

  1. Energy, entropy and mechanical response

    Close a steady-flow energy balance, then determine which pressure or temperature change reveals an irreversible process. Connect a film force to a moving bearing.

    Follow control-volume reasoning onto a grid. Compare diffusion, interface fluxes and advective remapping. Check how a travelling perturbation moves at different resolutions.

How the course fits together

  1. Chapters 1–4

    First specify how fluid moves and how pressure supports a stationary column. Apply momentum and mechanical-energy balances to a control volume. The force on a bend and the head lost in a pipe require different terms.

  2. Chapters 5–8

    Resolve viscous shear in a duct, then use similarity to identify equivalent flows. Vorticity tracks local rotation. At a wall, balancing inertia with viscous diffusion predicts the growth of a thin boundary layer.

  3. Chapters 9–12

    Integrate pressure and shear over a surface to obtain drag or lift. Couple pressure losses across networks and rotating machines, then include density changes to study compressible flow.

Chapter 1 · Velocity fields, flow rates and conservation of mass

Read local acceleration from a velocity field and use an integral mass balance to connect inlet, outlet and storage.

From first principles

Derive the continuity equation from conservation of mass

Choose a fixed region of space containing fluid of density \(\rho(\mathbf x,t)\) and velocity \(\mathbf u(\mathbf x,t)\). Mass crosses a small boundary element \(dA\) with outward normal \(\mathbf n\) at the signed rate \(\rho\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n\,dA\). A positive flux removes mass. With no sources of mass inside, the rate of change of the enclosed mass must balance the net outflow.

\[\frac d{dt}\int_V\rho\,dV+\oint_{\partial V}\rho\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n\,dA=0 .\]

For a stationary control volume the time derivative can be taken inside the integral. The divergence theorem changes the boundary integral to a volume integral. Since the resulting balance holds for every sufficiently small volume in a smooth flow, its integrand must vanish.

\[\partial_t\rho+\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u)=0,\]
\[\frac{D\rho}{Dt}+\rho\,\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0 .\]

Here \(D/Dt=\partial_t+\mathbf u\cdot\nabla\) follows a moving parcel. Constant parcel density therefore gives

\[\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0.\]

In a steady single-inlet, single-outlet incompressible duct, integration reduces this statement to \(A_1\bar u_1=A_2\bar u_2\).

Background · Material acceleration
\[D\mathbf u/Dt=\partial_t\mathbf u+(\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u\]

A fluid parcel can accelerate as it follows a steady but spatially varying velocity. A probe fixed in space measures only the local time history.

Derivation and applications

Distinguish a parcel trajectory from an instantaneous streamline

Dye introduced into an unsteady flow need not follow a line drawn tangent to one velocity snapshot. To calculate the difference, consider a spatially uniform planar velocity field with horizontal component \(u=U>0\) and vertical component \(v=at\), where \(a\) is constant. Such a field is divergence-free because neither component varies with position. Neglect diffusion and assume a neutrally buoyant tracer. A parcel released at the origin at time \(t_0\) obeys the two ordinary differential equations

\[\dot x=U,\]
\[\dot y=at,\]
\[x=U(t-t_0),\]
\[y=\tfrac12a(t^2-t_0^2).\]

Eliminate the elapsed time to find the parcel path. The first term reflects vertical velocity at release and the quadratic term accumulates the subsequent acceleration. In contrast, an instantaneous streamline uses the velocity direction at one frozen time \(t_s\) for all its points.

\[y_{\rm path}(x)=\frac{at_0}{U}x+\frac{a}{2U^2}x^2,\]
\[\left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right|_{\rm stream}=\frac{at_s}{U}.\]

Continuous injection at the origin generates a third observable. At a fixed observing time \(t_s\), different coloured parcels have different release times \(t_0=t_s-x/U\). Substitution into the position solution gives the locus of all visible dye at that instant, called the streakline.

\[y_{\rm streak}(x;t_s)=\frac{at_s}{U}x-\frac{a}{2U^2}x^2 .\]

The streakline is tangent to the instantaneous velocity at the injector but curves away from that direction farther downstream. In the steady limit \(a=0\) all three constructions produce horizontal lines.

Worked example

Compare a pulse of dye with a velocity image

Take \(U=0.400\) m/s and \(a=0.100\) m/s². A parcel released at \(t_0=0\) reaches \(x=0.800\) m at 2.00 s and has risen \(\tfrac12(0.100)(2.00)^2=0.200\) m. The streamline through the injector at 2.00 s has slope 0.500, reaching 0.400 m at the same horizontal coordinate. It represents the current velocity field, which changed while the pulse was travelling.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Recover acceleration from a continuous dye trace

In another experiment \(U=0.500\) m/s. At \(t_s=3.00\) s, continuously injected dye at \(x=0.500\) m lies 0.250 m above the source. That parcel is 1.00 s old, so its release time was 2.00 s. From \(y=a(3^2-2^2)/2\) one finds \(a=0.100\) m/s². The local velocity slope at observation is then 0.600, independently measurable from a short-exposure particle image.

Look at a point or follow a parcel

At a contraction in an otherwise steady pipe, a parcel of water speeds up. A probe fixed at the throat reports a constant speed throughout the experiment. Both observations are compatible because a field can vary along the path even when it has no explicit time dependence. Eulerian measurements describe velocity at a place. Following a marked parcel gives its changing position and velocity.

Write the velocity field as \(\mathbf u(\mathbf x,t)\). During \(dt\) the parcel advances through \(d\mathbf x=\mathbf u\,dt\). The chain rule along that trajectory gives

\[\mathbf a=\partial_t\mathbf u+(\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u.\]

The first term is local acceleration and the second is convective acceleration. In steady one-dimensional flow \(a_x=u\,du/dx\). A streamline is tangent to the instantaneous velocity. In a steady flow pathlines and streamlines coincide, whereas in an unsteady field the curve traced by a parcel can differ from a single snapshot’s streamlines.

Background · Can a sequence of photographs identify the acceleration of one parcel?

A fixed camera that records velocity at one pixel measures an Eulerian history. A particle may enter a different pixel long before the next frame. For the planar velocity field \(\mathbf u=(a x,-a y)\) with constant \(a\), all fixed pixels record an unchanging field. A parcel released on the positive \(x\) axis instead obeys \(x(t)=x_0e^{at}\).

\[\frac{D\mathbf u}{Dt}=\partial_t\mathbf u+(\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u=(a^2x,a^2y).\]

At \(a=2~\mathrm{s}^{-1}\) and \(x=0.10\) m on the axis, the downstream acceleration is 0.40 m/s². The parcel moves into faster neighbouring fluid. Estimating acceleration from an image pair therefore requires either tracking the same particle or combining the local time difference with spatial velocity gradients.

Count the mass crossing a surface

Enclose part of a fluid system in a fixed control volume. A small surface element with outward normal \(\mathbf n\) passes volume \((\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n)dA\,dt\) in time \(dt\). Multiply by density and add over the whole boundary. Normal inflow is negative, reducing the net outward flux. Conservation of mass is

\[\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\rm CV}\rho\,dV+\int_{\rm CS}\rho\,\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n\,dA=0.\]

In steady operation the mass flow rate entering all inlets equals that leaving all outlets. A one-inlet, one-outlet reduction with uniform cross-sectional density gives

\[\rho_1 A_1\bar u_1=\rho_2 A_2\bar u_2.\]

The mean velocity is the area integral of the normal component divided by area. A centreline speed is seldom that mean. For variable density the volume rate can change along a duct while mass rate remains constant.

Accelerate water in a contraction

Water of density 1000 kg/m³ enters a straight circular nozzle 60 mm in diameter with a mean speed of 1.50 m/s. The outlet is 30 mm in diameter. First use the inlet area to find the volume and mass passing per second.

\[\begin{aligned} Q&=\bar u_1\frac{\pi D_1^2}{4} =4.24\times10^{-3}~\mathrm{m^3/s},\\ \dot m&=\rho Q=4.24~\mathrm{kg/s}. \end{aligned}\]

For steady incompressible flow the same volume per second must pass through an outlet of one quarter the inlet area. The mean speed increases accordingly.

\[\bar u_2=\bar u_1\left(\frac{D_1}{D_2}\right)^2 =1.50\left(\frac{60}{30}\right)^2~\mathrm{m/s}=6.00~\mathrm{m/s}.\]

These speeds determine the change in streamwise momentum flux if both profiles are nearly uniform. The pressure and wall forces responsible for that change require a momentum balance.

Suppose along a centreline segment of length 0.30 m the speed is approximately linear from 1.50 to 6.0 m/s. Its gradient is 15 s⁻¹. At a local speed of 3.0 m/s the convective acceleration is 45 m/s². The transit time through the segment is

\[\int dx/u=\ln(6/1.5)/15=0.0924\,\mathrm{s}.\]

Dividing distance by the arithmetic mean of the two speeds gives a different answer because a particle spends a larger fraction of its time in the slower region.

Let the control volume fill

A vented tank receives 4 L/s and initially delivers 2 L/s through a controlled outlet. In a constant horizontal tank section of 0.50 m² the water level rises at \((0.004-0.002)/0.50=0.0040\) m/s. The storage term remains essential even if both inlet and outlet flow meters have steady readings. A free-surface tank can therefore act as an independent calibration: the measured volume increase over a timed interval should equal the integrated difference between inflow and outflow. If the outlet is driven by the changing hydrostatic head, its rate will cease to be constant and the balance becomes a differential equation for the depth.

Check continuity directly in a velocity field

A planar test field \(u=ax,\ v=-ay\) with constant \(a\) stretches a fluid square in one direction and shortens it in the other. Since \(\partial_xu+\partial_yv=a-a=0\), its area is preserved locally. A particle released at \(x_0,y_0\) follows \(x=x_0e^{at},\ y=y_0e^{-at}\); the product \(xy\) remains constant. This explicit trajectory shows how an incompressible parcel can deform and accelerate even when the velocity at every fixed point is independent of time.

Count mass through a control surface that moves

A flow meter on a translating piston does not see the same volume rate as a stationary meter at the same instant. Start with a material system, whose total mass is constant. Over a short time \(dt\), a patch of control surface of area \(dA\) moves a distance \(\mathbf v_s\cdot\mathbf n\,dt\) in its outward normal direction. Fluid moves a distance \(\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf n\,dt\) in that direction. The volume swept through the moving patch is the difference times \(dA\). Converting this volume into mass gives the moving-control-volume form of continuity:

\[\frac{d}{dt}\int_{CV(t)}\rho\,dV+\int_{CS(t)}\rho(\mathbf v-\mathbf v_s)\cdot\mathbf n\,dA=0 .\]

The storage term differentiates the mass inside the actual changing volume. A boundary following a parcel has zero relative normal velocity there, even when the parcel and the boundary move quickly in the laboratory. On an impermeable wall this no-penetration condition applies to its normal movement; the mass balance alone imposes no constraint on tangential slip. Setting \(\mathbf v_s=0\) recovers the fixed-volume formula. Making the entire surface material removes the flux term and recovers conservation of the system mass.

For constant density, differentiating the geometric volume and using the outward convention gives

\[dV/dt=\int\mathbf v_s\cdot\mathbf n\,dA.\]

Substitution shows how the familiar \(\int\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf n\,dA=0\) can hold for an instantaneous surface even while its enclosed volume is growing. Relative flux and volume storage must be paired consistently.

Interpret a flow reading on a moving piston

A horizontal cylinder of area \(0.010\,\mathrm{m^2}\) has an inlet in its fixed left end and a small outlet passing through a piston at its right end. The piston moves to the right at 0.040 m/s while the cylinder stays full of incompressible water. A fixed inlet meter measures \(0.00070\,\mathrm{m^3/s}\). The volume occupied by water grows at \(0.010(0.040)=0.00040\,\mathrm{m^3/s}\). Therefore the relative outlet flux through the piston is \(0.00070-0.00040=0.00030\,\mathrm{m^3/s}\), or 0.30 L/s.

The outlet area is \(2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2}\) and flow exits to the right. Its mean velocity relative to the piston is 1.50 m/s, while its laboratory velocity at the moving outlet is

\[1.50+0.040=1.54\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

Multiplying that laboratory velocity directly by the outlet area gives 0.308 L/s, too high for the outlet flow measured by an instrument carried on the piston. If the piston moved to the right at 0.070 m/s, the entire inlet rate would fill the new volume and relative outlet discharge would be zero. These conclusions assume no leakage past the piston rim and that the outlet area is part of the moving boundary; a fixed downstream collection nozzle would require its own control surface and storage terms.

A downstream weighing tank that travels with the piston would collect 0.30 kg per second at water density 1000 kg/m³. This is the discharge relative to that apparatus. The distinction is measurable and does not depend on any pressure-loss assumption for the outlet.

Can a steady flow accelerate a fluid particle?

Let the cross-sectional area in a straight nozzle be \(A(x)/A_0=1-a x/L\). In an ideal steady quasi-one-dimensional incompressible flow, continuity gives

\[u=U_0/(1-a x/L).\]

The material acceleration is \(u\,du/dx\) although every stationary probe measures a constant velocity.

Chapter 2 · Hydrostatic pressure and forces on submerged surfaces

Derive pressure from a body-force balance and locate the resultant force on a vertical gate.

From first principles

Determine gate force by integrating hydrostatic pressure

Consider a liquid at rest, with constant density \(\rho\) under uniform gravity \(g\). Take depth \(h\) positive downwards from its free surface. A small horizontal fluid slice of area \(A\) and thickness \(dh\) has downward weight \(\rho gA\,dh\). Equilibrium requires the increase in bottom pressure force to equal that weight.

\[[p(h+dh)-p(h)]A=\rho gA\,dh,\]
\[p(h)-p(0)=\rho gh .\]

For a vertical plane gate with the same atmospheric pressure on its dry side, only this gauge pressure contributes to the net force. Its resultant follows by integrating \(\rho gh\) over the wetted area. Its line of action must also reproduce the pressure moment about the surface level.

\[F=\rho g\int_A h\,dA=\rho gA\bar h,\]
\[h_RF=\rho g\int_A h^2\,dA .\]

Writing \(\int h^2dA=I_G+A\bar h^2\) gives

\[h_R=\bar h+I_G/(A\bar h),\]

where \(I_G\) is the second area moment about the horizontal centroidal axis. The greater pressure at depth places the resultant below the centroid.

Derivation and applications

Find the horizontal and vertical forces on a curved gate

A curved gate generally feels both a horizontal and a vertical force from hydrostatic pressure. Consider a circular quarter-arc of radius \(R\) and width \(b\) perpendicular to the page. Place the arc centre at the free surface and choose \(x\) rightwards, \(z\) vertically downwards. The arc is \(x=R\cos\theta\), \(z=R\sin\theta\) for \(0\leq\theta\leq\pi/2\). Water occupies the circular sector on the centre side of the gate. Uniform atmospheric pressure on the other side cancels, and the liquid is stationary at constant density \(\rho\).

\[p=\rho gR\sin\theta,\]
\[dA=bR\,d\theta,\]
\[d\mathbf F=p\,dA\,(\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_x+\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_z).\]

Integrating the horizontal component involves \(\sin\theta\cos\theta\). The resulting force equals the pressure force on the vertical projection of the arc, of height \(R\) and centroid depth \(R/2\).

\[F_x=\rho gbR^2\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin\theta\cos\theta\,d\theta=\frac12\rho gbR^2.\]

The vertical component instead integrates \(\sin^2\theta\). It equals the weight of the imaginary water above the arc up to the free surface, which in this geometry is precisely the water sector itself.

\[F_z=\rho gbR^2\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^2\theta\,d\theta=\frac{\pi}{4}\rho gbR^2.\]

Every elemental force lies along an arc radius, so the resultant line passes through the circle centre. Its magnitude follows from vector addition and its direction makes angle \(\arctan(\pi/2)\) below horizontal. The sign of the vertical reaction changes if the fluid bears on the opposite side.

Worked example

Size the reaction on a laboratory quadrant

With \(R=0.400\) m, \(b=0.600\) m and \(\rho=1000\) kg/m³, the common factor is

\[\rho gbR^2=942\,\mathrm{N}.\]

The horizontal and downward forces are 471 N and 740 N respectively. The resultant is

\[\sqrt{471^2+740^2}=877\,\mathrm{N}.\]

A bearing at the centre can react that force with no hydrostatic torque about its own axis.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Infer liquid density from the vertical component

A separate quarter gate has radius 0.300 m and width 0.500 m. After subtracting gate weight, its measured downward fluid force is 295 N. Inversion gives \(\rho=4F_z/(\pi gbR^2)=851\) kg/m³. The expected horizontal reaction magnitude is

\[2F_z/\pi=188\,\mathrm{N}.\]

This ratio tests the geometry without any independent density calibration.

Locate the force on an inclined circular viewing window

A circular window is installed below the water level in a laboratory tank. Knowing its total normal load is enough for one force balance. Sizing its hinge reaction also requires the moment of that load. The pressure increases with vertical depth, while the force elements must be integrated across an inclined surface. Distinguish distance down the window from depth below the surface before constructing the moment.

Let the window have radius \(a\), centre depth \(h_c\) and inclination \(\theta\) above the horizontal. Define \(s\) along its steepest downslope direction, measured from the disc centre. A point at \(s\) has vertical depth \(h_c+s\sin\theta\). With an atmospheric gas on the dry side, the differential normal force is \(\rho g(h_c+s\sin\theta)\,dA\). Since the first area moment \(\int s\,dA\) vanishes by symmetry, the resultant is simply \(\rho gh_c A\). Thus two windows of equal area at the same centre depth carry the same normal force even if their inclinations differ, provided both remain completely submerged.

To get the location of the force, take moments around the horizontal diameter through the window centre. The constant contribution to pressure gives zero moment about this axis, while the part proportional to \(s\) contributes \(\rho g\sin\theta\int s^2dA\). A circular disc has \(A=\pi a^2\) and diametral second moment \(I=\pi a^4/4\), which gives

\[s_{\mathrm{cp}}=\frac{\rho g\sin\theta\,I}{\rho gh_cA} =\frac{a^2\sin\theta}{4h_c}.\]

For \(a=0.18\) m, \(h_c=0.42\) m, \(\theta=60^\circ\) and \(\rho=998\) kg/m³, the force is 418.55 N and acts 16.70 mm down the window from its centre. Its vertical depth is only \(16.70\sin60^\circ=14.46\) mm below the centre depth. A load strip of thickness \(ds\) has width \(2\sqrt{a^2-s^2}\). The notebook integrates the strip forces and their first moments independently to check the circular formula.

For a hinge along the upper tangent to the disc, the resultant lever arm is \(a+s_{\rm cp}\) measured down the window. Multiplying the default force by \(0.180+0.01670\) m gives approximately 82.3 N m. Treating the force as though it acted at the centre would predict only 75.3 N m. The difference of 7.0 N m equals the moment of the pressure gradient about the centre diameter. The hinge axis must lie in the plane of the window for this simple lever arm to apply. Changing to a vertical hinge introduces a different area moment and, for this symmetric disc, a different distribution of opening torque.

What changes when the window is lowered until its centre depth doubles at the same tilt?

Work through the solution

The normal force doubles and the downslope offset halves, to about 8.35 mm. Their product, the moment about the horizontal diameter through the disc centre, stays fixed because it depends only on the pressure gradient and the geometry. Rotate a fully submerged window to horizontal in the notebook and the offset goes to zero. If a window crosses the free surface, the wetted area and its centroid change; continuing to integrate the full disc would introduce fictitious negative gauge loads on dry strips.

Balance a stationary fluid element

A diver experiences pressure from all directions, while a sluice gate feels a net force mainly from one side. Pressure is a scalar state variable. The force on a surface element is directed opposite its outward normal and has magnitude \(p\,dA\). The net force on a real gate also depends on what presses on its back face. For a fluid at rest there is no viscous shear. Take a thin horizontal slab of area \(A\) and thickness \(dz\), with \(z\) upward. Its vertical force balance is \(p(z)A-p(z+dz)A-\rho gA\,dz=0\), or \(dp/dz=-\rho g\).

For constant density this integrates to \(p=p_s+\rho gh\) at depth \(h\) below a free surface of pressure \(p_s\). A vented water surface has atmospheric absolute pressure. Gauge pressure subtracts that same atmospheric pressure at every point. It is convenient for net forces when the other face is exposed to the same atmosphere. A sealed gas space with appreciable excess pressure adds a uniform load component to the hydrostatic triangle.

Background · Which way does a liquid surface tilt in an accelerating tank?

Consider a tank translating horizontally at uniform acceleration \(a\) to the right. After sloshing has subsided, the liquid is stationary relative to the tank. In that frame each parcel has an effective body acceleration \(-a\mathbf e_x-g\mathbf e_z\). Along a free surface at uniform atmospheric pressure the pressure gradient has no tangential component.

\[dp=-\rho a\,dx-\rho g\,dz=0,\]
\[\frac{dz}{dx}=-\frac{a}{g}.\]

The free surface falls to the right. A tank 0.80 m long accelerating at 1.2 m/s² exhibits a height difference of about 98 mm between the ends, provided neither end spills or dries out. This argument uses the uniform translating frame; a tank that is still sloshing has additional relative accelerations.

Use two separate integrals

For a flat vertical gate, add horizontal pressure forces to get

\[F=\int_A p_g\,dA,\]

where \(p_g\) is the pressure difference across it. To find the force’s depth \(h_R\), match its moment:

\[Fh_R=\int_A h p_g\,dA.\]

With a vented free surface on one side and air on the other, this gives

\[F=\rho gA\bar h,\]
\[h_R=\int_A h^2dA/(A\bar h).\]

Decomposing the second moment into centroidal and parallel-axis pieces gives

\[h_R=\bar h+\frac{I_G}{A\bar h}.\]

Here \(I_G\) is the area’s second moment about its horizontal centroidal axis. Its SI unit is m⁴. The second term is positive: lower parts of a vertical gate carry more force per unit area, moving the resultant below the area centroid.

Calculate hinge moment before actuator force

A 1.20 m wide rectangular gate extends from the water surface to depth 1.80 m. Its horizontal hinge is at the surface. At the bottom the gauge pressure is \(1000(9.81)(1.80)=17.7\) kPa. The triangular pressure profile integrates to \(F=\rho g bH^2/2=19.1\) kN. The resultant lies at \(2H/3=1.20\) m beneath the hinge, producing a moment of 22.9 kN·m. A horizontal actuator at the bottom must pull with magnitude \(22.9/1.80=12.7\) kN to balance this water moment. This excludes gate weight and friction at the hinge.

If the whole gate is submerged another metre with both ends still separated from the same air pressure on the back, its pressure diagram gains a uniform rectangle. That additional resultant acts at the gate centroid. It increases hinge moment, while the centre of pressure of the total load moves closer to the centroid measured relative to the gate. Simply adding one metre to the original lever arm would confuse a depth measured from the surface with a moment arm measured from the hinge.

Include stratification and buoyancy

In layered immiscible liquids, integrate \(\rho g\) through each layer. Pressure remains continuous across a flat interface when capillary effects are negligible, while its depth gradient changes. For a closed submerged object the resultant pressure force is upward with magnitude equal to the displaced fluid’s weight. To see this, replace the body by the fluid that would occupy its volume. In static equilibrium surface pressure must balance that fluid’s weight; the same surface pressure acts on the original object. Buoyancy concerns the net force on a closed surface. It cannot replace a hinge-moment calculation on an open gate.

Read a two-fluid differential manometer

Two water taps at the same elevation connect to opposite legs of a manometer containing a heavier immiscible liquid of density 2500 kg/m³. If the heavy-fluid interfaces differ in height by 40 mm, following a hydrostatic path gives a pressure difference \((2500-1000)g(0.040)=589\,\mathrm{Pa}\). The tap above the lower heavy-fluid interface has the larger pressure. The water columns matter even though both taps sit at the same elevation.

Derive a restoring moment for a floating body

Equality of weight and buoyancy establishes vertical equilibrium of a floating body, but does not determine whether a small roll is restored. Consider a long prismatic pontoon with vertical sides floating at rest in a liquid of uniform density. Let the displaced volume be \(\nabla\), its centroid the centre of buoyancy \(B\), and the centre of gravity \(G\) on the upright symmetry axis. For a small heel angle \(\theta\), a strip of waterplane area \(dA\) at transverse coordinate \(y\) gains a submerged slice of volume approximately \(y\theta\,dA\) on one side and loses one on the opposite side.

The first moment of these changed wedges shifts the centre of buoyancy sideways by \(\Delta y_B=\theta\int y^2dA/\nabla=\theta I_{\rm wp}/\nabla\). Thus the line of buoyancy in the tilted configuration intersects the original symmetry axis at a point \(M\), the transverse metacentre, located \(BM=I_{\rm wp}/\nabla\) above \(B\) to first order. The initial righting lever is \(GM\sin\theta\simeq GM\,\theta\), and the corresponding restoring moment magnitude is \(WGM\theta\) when \(GM>0\).

The waterplane second moment here is taken about the longitudinal heel axis; it is not the structural bending second moment of the vessel wall. A wide waterplane can increase stability without changing the weight because its outer strips contribute strongly to \(I_{\rm wp}\). A centre of gravity above the centre of buoyancy can still be stable if it remains below the metacentre.

Measure a pontoon’s centre of gravity by shifting a weight

A rectangular pontoon 4.0 m long and 2.0 m wide floats with 0.40 m draft in freshwater. Its displaced volume is \(4(2)(0.4)=3.20\,\mathrm{m^3}\), so the total supported mass is 3200 kg. The centre of buoyancy lies 0.20 m above the keel. Its waterplane moment is \(4(2^3)/12=2.667\,\mathrm{m^4}\), giving \(BM=2.667/3.20=0.833\,\mathrm m\) and \(KM=1.033\,\mathrm m\) above the keel.

Shift a pre-existing 100 kg test weight transversely by 0.40 m without changing its height. A settled heel of 2.0° balances the imposed heeling moment against buoyancy. For small heel the more accurate inclination-test expression is

\[GM=md/(M_{\rm tot}\tan\theta)=100(0.40)/[3200\tan2^\circ]=0.358\,\mathrm m.\]

The inferred unshifted vertical centre of gravity is

\[KG=1.033-0.358=0.675\,\mathrm m.\]

Raising a 400 kg installed component by 0.80 m would raise the combined centre by \(400(0.80)/3200=0.100\) m and reduce initial \(GM\) to 0.258 m. A slack liquid surface inside the pontoon or partial emergence of its deck edge changes the small-heel model; either can invalidate an extrapolation of the measured initial restoring moment to large roll angles.

For the original centre of gravity the initial rotational stiffness is

\[WGM=3200(9.81)(0.358)=11.24\,\mathrm{kN\,m/rad}.\]

A loading estimate from a small known heeling moment can therefore serve as a separate check on the shifted-weight experiment. This stiffness is hydrostatic; rolling inertia and hydrodynamic damping are still required to predict a free-roll period or decay.

Place the hinge on a fully submerged vertical gate

A rectangular vertical gate is 2 m tall and of constant width. Integrate hydrostatic gauge pressure over its area. The centre of pressure is \(h_c+I_c/(A h_c)\), so the offset below the centroid shrinks as the gate is submerged more deeply.

Study the full derivation

Gravity, capillarity and instability at a moving interface · Momentum, motion and transport in fluids

A flat interface can carry waves restored by gravity and surface tension. If its two liquids or gases move at different speeds, a band of those waves can grow. Derive the ideal threshold, identify the most vulnerable wavelength at onset and distinguish growth of an instability from advection of an otherwise stable disturbance.

Problem sheet 1 · Mass balances and hydrostatic loading

Determine the correct area, depth and system boundary before evaluating the force or volume flow.

1. Audit a branching pipeline

A 100 mm pipe supplies water at mean speed 1.00 m/s. One 60 mm branch carries 2.50 L/s; the remaining water leaves through a 50 mm branch. (a) Find the inlet flow and both branch speeds. (b) If discharge from the smaller branch is actually 4.00 L/s, infer an accumulation or leak rate. (c) What observation distinguishes temporary storage from a persistent leak?

Close the volume budget

The inlet carries

\[0.25\pi(0.10)^2(1)=7.85\,\mathrm{L/s}.\]

The nominal branch speeds are

\[0.00250/[\pi(0.06)^2/4]=0.884\,\mathrm{m/s},\]
\[0.005354/[\pi(0.05)^2/4]=2.73\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

At the measured discharge the unaccounted rate is

\[7.85-2.50-4.00=1.35\,\mathrm{L/s}.\]

A rising storage level or an expanding system volume could temporarily account for it. At constant inventory a persistent imbalance points to another flow path or meter offset.

2. Locate pressure with two liquids

A vented tank has 0.40 m of oil of density 800 kg/m³ over 0.80 m of water. (a) Find gauge pressure at the interface and at the base. (b) Find the net horizontal force on a 0.50 m wide vertical wall segment extending only over the water depth. (c) Why is a single triangular pressure diagram across the water segment insufficient?

Add the uniform and triangular loads

Interface pressure is \(800(9.81)(0.40)=3.14\) kPa. At the base add \(1000(9.81)(0.80)=7.85\) kPa to obtain 10.99 kPa. Assuming atmospheric pressure on the back, the average pressure on the water segment is

\[(3.14+10.99)/2=7.06\,\mathrm{kPa}.\]

On 0.40 m² this gives 2.83 kN. Oil adds the uniform 3.14 kPa across the water segment. Using only the water triangle would omit 1.26 kN.

3. Move a gate hinge

A vertical gate is 1.0 m wide, extending from 0.50 to 1.50 m below a vented water surface. There is air behind it. (a) Find resultant force and its depth. (b) Find the water moment about a hinge at its lower edge. (c) Compare with a force applied at the centroid.

Match the force and its moment

The centroid depth is 1.0 m and the area is 1.0 m², giving 9.81 kN. The centroidal second moment is \(1(1)^3/12=0.0833\) m⁴. The resultant acts at \(1.0833\) m depth, with lower-hinge arm \(1.50-1.0833=0.4167\) m, giving 4.09 kN·m. Using the centroid would give 4.91 kN·m, too large because the greater pressure near the lower edge shifts the resultant towards that hinge.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Velocity fields, flow rates and conservation of mass; Hydrostatic pressure and forces on submerged surfaces. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Notebook practice · Locate the force on an inclined circular viewing window

Chapter 3 · Control-volume momentum and forces on fittings

Use the velocity vector, gauge pressure and a consistent force boundary to find the support load on a pipe bend.

From first principles

Relate a support reaction to momentum carried by a stream

Newton's second law applies to a fixed set of fluid particles: external force equals the time derivative of their momentum. In a pipe bend it is easier to observe a stationary control volume that particles continually enter and leave. The rate of change for the material particles consists of storage in that volume plus the net rate at which momentum crosses its boundary.

\[\sum\mathbf F=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\int_V\rho\mathbf u\,dV+\oint_{\partial V}\rho\mathbf u(\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n)\,dA .\]

The dot product determines whether mass crosses inwards or outwards, while the other velocity factor retains the direction of the momentum carried. For steady uniform inlet and outlet sections, the flux integral reduces to

\[\mathbf F_{\rm wall}+\mathbf F_{\rm pressure}+\mathbf W=\dot m(\mathbf V_2-\mathbf V_1).\]

Pressure pushes inward normal to each open section, so its contribution must be included before solving for the wall force. The force of the fluid on the bend is opposite to the wall's force on the fluid. Equal inlet and outlet speed can still give a substantial support load when their directions differ.

Derivation and applications

Derive the irreversible head loss across a sudden expansion

A sudden enlargement slows a liquid and partly restores static pressure. Consider steady incompressible flow from area \(A_1\) into area \(A_2>A_1\) in a horizontal pipe. Choose the downstream section after mixing has produced an approximately uniform velocity again. Approximate both station profiles as uniform, neglect wall shear over the short control volume and take the mean pressure on the annular shoulder to be the upstream pressure \(p_1\). Mass conservation makes the two mean speeds \(V_1,V_2\) proportional to inverse area.

\[Q=A_1V_1=A_2V_2,\]
\[p_1A_2-p_2A_2=\rho Q(V_2-V_1).\]

The inlet and shoulder together contribute \(p_1A_2\) to the axial pressure force. Rearranging the momentum balance gives a positive downstream pressure rise, despite the separated mixing region.

\[p_2-p_1=\rho V_2(V_1-V_2).\]

Insert that pressure rise into the mechanical energy balance. The difference between kinetic energy released and pressure recovered is the specific energy dissipated in mixing, expressed below as a head of flowing liquid.

\[h_L=\frac{V_1^2-V_2^2}{2g}-\frac{p_2-p_1}{\rho g}=\frac{(V_1-V_2)^2}{2g}.\]

This result tends to zero quadratically as the area change vanishes. A gradual diffuser would require a different shoulder-pressure and separation model. Pressure recovery can thus coexist with positive mechanical-energy loss and its measurement alone does not provide the loss.

Worked example

Audit an abrupt pipe enlargement

Water moving at 3.00 m/s enters a pipe section with three times the area. Its final speed is 1.00 m/s. Taking \(\rho=1000\) kg/m³, the pressure rises by \(1000(1.00)(2.00)=2.00\) kPa. The kinetic pressure falls by 4.00 kPa; the missing 2.00 kPa is a head loss of 0.204 m. For 6.00 L/s, the rate of mechanical energy dissipation is \(Q\rho g h_L=12.0\) W.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Recover the enlargement ratio from a head test

Another rig measures \(V_1=2.50\) m/s and \(h_L=0.0800\) m. Taking the positive speed drop yields

\[V_2=2.50-\sqrt{2(9.81)(0.0800)}=1.25\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

The inferred area ratio is \(A_2/A_1=V_1/V_2=2.00\) to the stated precision. The expected water pressure rise is about 1.56 kPa, a separately measurable check on the shoulder-pressure assumption.

Momentum crosses the boundary with the mass

A pipe bend can exert a substantial force on its supports even when its inlet and outlet speeds agree. Momentum has direction. Turning a stream requires force and gives the fitting an equal and opposite load. Enclose the fluid inside the fitting in a fixed control volume. The external forces include wall traction, pressure on the control surfaces and body forces such as gravity.

A surface element transports mass per second \(\rho(\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n)dA\). Multiply by the local velocity to obtain momentum per second. Newton’s second law applied to the control volume reads

\[\sum\mathbf F_{\rm ext}=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\rm CV}\rho\mathbf u\,dV+\int_{\rm CS}\rho\mathbf u(\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n)\,dA.\]

For a steady fitting with nearly uniform inlet and outlet velocities this reduces to a sum of outgoing \(\dot m\mathbf u\) minus incoming \(\dot m\mathbf u\). Use one fixed set of axes even as pipe directions change. Pressure always pushes into the control volume, whereas the flux carries the direction of the local velocity, including its sign on the chosen axis.

Include the end pressures explicitly

Take a horizontal bend turning water from the positive \(x\) direction to the positive \(y\) direction. The inlet and outlet areas are each 0.010 m² and \(Q=0.040\,\mathrm{m^3/s}\). Their speeds are both 4.0 m/s and \(\dot m=40\) kg/s. The net outward momentum flux vector is \((-160,+160)\) N. Let the gauge pressures be 90 kPa at inlet and 80 kPa at outlet. Pressure pushes +900 N in \(x\) through the inlet and −800 N in \(y\) through the outlet.

Let \(\mathbf W\) be the fitting’s wall force on the enclosed fluid, with external atmospheric effects accounted for consistently using gauge pressures. Then \(\mathbf W+(900,-800)=(-160,160)\) N, giving \(\mathbf W=(-1060,960)\) N. The fluid’s net gauge load on the fitting is the opposite, \((1060,-960)\) N, of magnitude 1.43 kN. Supports plus connected pipework must provide the opposite force for a stationary fitting, in addition to carrying the weight of hardware and fluid. The strongest contribution here stems from the pressure terms, despite the conspicuous turn in velocity.

Recover momentum from a nonuniform profile

A flow meter usually measures \(Q\), not \(\int u^2dA\). At a plane normal to a unidirectional profile define \(\beta=\int u^2dA/(A\bar u^2)\). Then the axial momentum rate is \(\beta\rho Q\bar u\). Uniform flow gives \(\beta=1\). For a developed laminar circular pipe \(u=2\bar u[1-(r/R)^2]\); inserting \(dA=2\pi r\,dr\) in the integral gives \(\beta=4/3\). The largest velocities carry a disproportionate share of momentum. If one bend cross-section admits strong swirl or backflow, resolve vector fluxes instead of applying a single profile coefficient.

Recognise an open-stream boundary

A free jet at atmospheric pressure strikes a fixed flat plate normal to its axis and turns into a thin sheet that leaves sideways. Neglecting rebound and friction far from the impact, the outgoing axial momentum flux is zero. For water flowing at 10 m/s through a 20 mm diameter nozzle, \(\dot m=3.14\) kg/s, so the axial force on the plate is 31.4 N. Both the incoming and outgoing free-stream control surfaces have zero gauge pressure. The plate force then follows entirely from the change of momentum. A nozzle bolted to a pressurised pipe would require different pressure forces even if it produced exactly the same free jet.

A jet catches a moving vane

Consider a single flat vane moving steadily in the jet direction at speed \(u\). In the vane frame the arriving volume rate is \(A(V-u)\) and the incoming streamwise relative velocity is \(V-u\). If the jet spreads tangentially across the face, the resulting normal force is \(\rho A(V-u)^2\). For water, \(A=2.0\,\mathrm{cm^2}\), \(V=10\) m/s and \(u=4\) m/s, this gives 7.2 N. Its mechanical power on the moving vane is \(Fu=28.8\,\mathrm W\). Using the stationary volume rate would count fluid parcels that the moving vane never catches.

Derive the two depths of a hydraulic jump

A hydraulic jump converts shallow fast open-channel flow into deeper slow flow over a short distance. Turbulent mixing dissipates mechanical energy, so applying an inviscid energy balance between the two uniform sections would miss its defining loss. Instead choose a control volume bounded by sections before and after the roller, the horizontal bed and the free surface. Approximate the velocity as uniform and the pressure distribution as hydrostatic at the two end sections. Atmospheric pressure cancels, and over the short reach assume the integral of bed friction is small compared with the pressure and momentum forces.

In a rectangular channel per unit width let the volume rate be \(q\), depths \(y_1,y_2\) and speeds \(v_i=q/y_i\). The horizontal pressure resultant per width is \(\rho g y_i^2/2\). Equating pressure force difference to momentum-flux change gives

\[\frac{y_1^2}{2}+\frac{q^2}{g y_1}=\frac{y_2^2}{2}+\frac{q^2}{g y_2}.\]

One solution is the unchanged depth. Factoring out \(y_2-y_1\) for the nontrivial solution gives

\[q^2=g y_1 y_2(y_1+y_2)/2.\]

With upstream Froude number \(Fr_1=v_1/\sqrt{g y_1}\), the downstream-to-upstream depth ratio becomes \([\sqrt{1+8Fr_1^2}-1]/2\). The deeper downstream branch occurs when \(Fr_1>1\). As \(Fr_1\to1\), the ratio tends to unity and the finite jump disappears.

Calculate the energy dissipated in a channel transition

A horizontal test channel carries water at \(y_1=0.20\,\mathrm m\) and \(v_1=4.0\,\mathrm{m/s}\). Its unit-width discharge is \(q=0.80\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\) and Froude number is

\[4/\sqrt{9.81(0.20)}=2.86.\]

The depth ratio is about 3.57, giving \(y_2=0.714\,\mathrm m\) and \(v_2=0.80/0.714=1.12\,\mathrm{m/s}\). The downstream Froude number is about 0.42. These are conjugate depths for the specified momentum balance; the tailwater has to support the downstream depth for a stationary jump to sit at the chosen location.

Using specific mechanical head \(E=y+v^2/(2g)\), the inlet and outlet give 1.015 and 0.778 m respectively. The loss is approximately 0.237 m of water, or \(1000(9.81)(0.80)(0.237)=1.86\,\mathrm{kW}\) dissipated per metre of channel width. Substitution of the momentum relation into the energy difference gives

\[\Delta E=(y_2-y_1)^3/(4y_1y_2),\]

which is positive for the physical jump and approaches zero rapidly for a weak jump. This is a useful cross-check on a measured depth pair. If measurements are taken within the roller, pressure may be nonhydrostatic and velocity markedly nonuniform; using those sections in the simple end-state formula can produce an apparent energy or momentum imbalance.

Another discrimination comes from comparing the two depths with an upstream energy curve. At \(q=0.80\) m²/s, a downstream depth giving exactly the upstream energy would have to satisfy \(y+0.03262/y^2=1.015\). The deeper root is about 0.981 m, appreciably deeper than the momentum-predicted 0.714 m. Pairing those equal-energy depths would require an additional horizontal force or a different channel geometry to satisfy momentum. A smooth reversible change over a channel hump can conserve specific head while changing velocity and depth; a jump on a flat bed instead conserves the appropriate momentum function while dissipating head. Laboratory measurements of both levels are sufficient to distinguish the two idealisations.

Resolve the momentum force on a turning guide

A steady free water jet of mass flow 6 kg/s enters a smooth stationary guide at 20 m/s and leaves with the same speed at the indicated angle. Inlet and exit are at ambient pressure. Neglect weight for this short control volume. Reaction on the guide is the negative of the fluid momentum change.

Chapter 4 · Bernoulli head, losses and pump duty

Build a mechanical-energy balance between two stations and distinguish a recoverable pressure change from irreversible loss.

From first principles

Derive mechanical head along a streamline

Take steady inviscid flow of constant-density liquid under gravity. Along a streamline use distance \(s\), speed \(V\) and elevation \(z\) positive upwards. Tangential acceleration of a parcel is \(V\,dV/ds\) because the parcel moves from one position to the next. Pressure gradient and gravity provide the tangential force per unit volume. Dividing their balance by density gives

\[V\frac{dV}{ds}=-\frac1\rho\frac{dp}{ds}-g\frac{dz}{ds}.\]

Each term is a derivative along the path. Integrating between two positions shows that their sum, written as energy per unit mass, is conserved under the stated assumptions.

\[\frac p\rho+\frac{V^2}{2}+gz=\text{constant along a streamline}.\]

Dividing by \(g\) converts all terms into lengths called heads. In a pipe system one measures added pump head \(H_p\), removed turbine head \(H_t\) and irreversible loss \(h_L\) to form a section-to-section engineering balance. For uniform profiles this reads

\[H_1+H_p=H_2+H_t+h_L,\]
\[H_j=p_j/(\rho g)+V_j^2/(2g)+z_j .\]

This distinguishes a reversible pressure drop associated with acceleration from a loss of total mechanical head.

Background · Head loss
\[h_L=\Delta p_{\rm loss}/(\rho g)\]

This is an irreversible loss expressed as a length. A static pressure change can also include reversible changes of speed or elevation.

Derivation and applications

Derive an ideal Venturi flow reading from two pressure taps

A narrowing can measure flow by reversibly trading pressure for speed. Take a horizontal Venturi meter carrying steady, incompressible liquid from a developed upstream section of area \(A_1\) into a throat of area \(A_2\). For an ideal first model assume uniform velocity at the two taps, negligible viscous loss between them, and no pump or turbine between stations. Pressure taps register static pressure. Define \(\beta=d_2/d_1\) for geometrically circular sections and use continuity to eliminate upstream speed.

\[V_1=\frac{A_2}{A_1}V_2=\beta^2V_2,\]
\[p_1-p_2=\frac{\rho}{2}(V_2^2-V_1^2).\]

The squared velocity is the source of the fourth power of diameter ratio. Substituting the continuity expression and multiplying throat speed by throat area gives the ideal meter law.

\[Q_{\rm id}=A_2\sqrt{\frac{2(p_1-p_2)}{\rho(1-\beta^4)}} .\]

In an installed meter a calibrated discharge coefficient \(C_d\) relates actual flow to this model. At fixed geometry, density and coefficient, logarithmic differentiation produces a useful uncertainty relation for pressure-transducer readings.

\[Q=C_d Q_{\rm id},\]
\[\frac{dQ}{Q}=\frac12\frac{d(\Delta p)}{\Delta p},\]
\[\Delta p=p_1-p_2.\]

The pressure difference between upstream tap and throat is larger than the permanent loss measured after the diffuser. Estimating pump power from the meter differential would therefore include recoverable pressure. At very small flow, a fixed pressure offset has a particularly large relative effect.

Worked example

Translate a pressure reading into discharge

A water meter has \(d_1=80.0\) mm, \(d_2=40.0\) mm, \(\rho=1000\) kg/m³ and \(C_d=0.980\). A 3.00 kPa differential corresponds to ideal throat speed \(\sqrt{6/0.9375}=2.53\) m/s. Actual discharge is

\[0.980\pi(0.0200)^2(2.53)=3.12\,\mathrm{L/s}.\]

A 30 Pa differential error represents one percent of the measured drop, giving approximately 0.5 percent flow error.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Calibrate a second meter against a collection tank

A meter with \(\beta=0.600\) and \(A_2=600\) mm² gives 2.00 kPa differential for a liquid of density 800 kg/m³. Its ideal flow is

\[0.000600\sqrt{4000/[800(1-0.6^4)]}=1.44\,\mathrm{L/s}.\]

Collecting 14.0 L in 10.0 s indicates 1.40 L/s, hence \(C_d=0.974\) after using unrounded intermediate values. Repeating at other flow rates tests whether one constant coefficient is appropriate.

Integrate the acceleration along a streamline

A nozzle can lower static pressure while increasing speed. A valve can also lower pressure, yet recover little useful speed downstream. To separate the mechanisms, start from a steady, inviscid streamline in constant-density flow. Project Newton’s equation along its tangent \(s\), with gravity acting downward. The acceleration in that direction is \(u\,du/ds\), giving \(\rho u\,du=-dp-\rho g\,dz\). Integration between points on that streamline gives \(p/\rho+u^2/2+gz=\) constant.

The three terms have units J/kg. Dividing by \(g\) measures the same energies as equivalent lengths of fluid column, called pressure head, velocity head and elevation head. A rise in speed at constant elevation can be paid for by a drop in static pressure even in lossless flow. Bringing such a stream to rest along a short, nearly lossless streamline would convert velocity head back into stagnation pressure. An irreversible loss reduces that recoverable total.

Add distributed losses and machinery

For a steady incompressible stream between stations with approximately uniform profiles, a pump supplies head \(H_p\) and a turbine extracts head \(H_t\). Viscous losses \(h_L\) appear as mechanical energy converted into internal energy. With endpoints numbered in the flow direction the energy balance is

\[\frac{p_1}{\rho g}+\frac{\bar u_1^2}{2g}+z_1+H_p=\frac{p_2}{\rho g}+\frac{\bar u_2^2}{2g}+z_2+H_t+h_L .\]

For a noticeably nonuniform profile, multiply each velocity-head term by the kinetic-energy correction \(\alpha=\int_Au^3dA/(A\bar u^3)\). It differs from the momentum correction because energy flux weights the cube of velocity. Long straight pipes are commonly modelled with the Darcy loss \(f_D(L/D)\bar u^2/(2g)\) and fittings with \(K\bar u^2/(2g)\). State which pipe diameter supplies the reference velocity for each coefficient.

Lift water between open tanks

Two large vented reservoirs differ in elevation by 8.0 m. Water flows at \(Q=0.010\,\mathrm{m^3/s}\) through 40 m of 80 mm diameter pipe. Use a supplied Darcy friction factor 0.025 and total fitting coefficient 3.0, including entrance and exit losses. Both free surfaces have atmospheric pressure and negligible velocity. The pipe area is \(5.03\times10^{-3}\) m², giving mean speed 1.99 m/s and velocity head 0.202 m. Friction and fittings dissipate \([0.025(40/0.080)+3.0]0.202=3.13\) m. The pump therefore supplies 11.13 m head.

The hydraulic power is \(\rho gQH_p=1.09\) kW for \(\rho=1000\) kg/m³. A pump with efficiency 0.70 at this duty requires 1.56 kW of shaft power. Elevation work alone would predict 0.785 kW hydraulic, and electrical input would also require the motor and drive efficiencies. These differences reflect which boundary is used for the energy balance.

Find the intersection with the pump characteristic

If coefficients are treated as fixed, the system head here has the form \(H_{\rm sys}=8+BQ^2\). Doubling the flow quadruples the losses, not the elevation rise. A real pump produces a head that depends on flow rate and rotational speed. Its steady operating point is where available head equals required head. Partially closing a valve increases \(B\); it does not directly prescribe a particular flow. Readjusting pump speed changes its characteristic instead. In a real design recheck friction factor and efficiency at the new rate. At low Reynolds number, where laminar friction factor varies inversely with speed, the distributed loss becomes linear rather than quadratic in flow.

Check the absolute pressure on a pump inlet

A pump inlet sits 3.0 m above an open water reservoir. At a particular duty the suction-line speed is 2.0 m/s and the loss between reservoir and inlet is 0.60 m. Atmospheric pressure is 101 kPa. Applying the mechanical energy balance upstream of the impeller gives absolute inlet pressure \(101000-1000(9.81)(3.0+0.60)-1000(2.0)^2/2=63.7\,\mathrm{kPa}\). If the supplied vapour pressure is 2.3 kPa, this tapping remains above it. Pressure may fall further inside the impeller. Evaluating cavitation margin requires the pump’s suction requirement at the actual duty and an absolute pressure reference; a negative gauge pressure at the tapping alone does not establish cavitation.

Locate the critical depth from a constrained energy minimum

In open-channel flow the atmosphere fixes the free-surface pressure, while the depth and speed can both adjust. Consider a rectangular channel of constant width with slowly changing bed elevation \(z_b\). Define the discharge per unit width \(q=vy\) and use the bed as the local elevation datum. With nearly hydrostatic pressure and approximately uniform section velocity, the mechanical head relative to the bed is \(E(y)=y+q^2/(2gy^2)\). This specific energy is finite at a given depth but diverges both for a very shallow stream at fixed discharge and for a very deep stream.

At fixed \(q\), differentiate with respect to \(y\): \(dE/dy=1-q^2/(gy^3)\). The stationary depth is \(y_c=(q^2/g)^{1/3}\), its Froude number is one, and the second derivative \(3q^2/(gy^4)\) is positive. The minimum value is

\[E_c=3y_c/2.\]

For any specific energy above this minimum two positive depths are possible. One is deep and slow and the other shallow and fast. One must know the upstream flow regime and the physical control to choose a branch.

Across a smooth low hump with negligible loss the total head \(z_b+E\) remains constant. Raising the bed reduces the specific energy available at the crest. On the deep branch depth decreases towards critical, whereas on the shallow branch depth increases towards it. These opposite trends follow from the sign of \(dE/dy\). Neither branch can pass below \(E_c\) at the prescribed discharge.

Predict when a raised bed changes the upstream water level

Water enters a wide rectangular flume at \(y=0.80\,\mathrm m\) and \(v=1.0\,\mathrm{m/s}\), giving \(q=0.80\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\) and upstream specific energy \(E_1=0.8510\,\mathrm m\). The critical depth is \((0.64/9.81)^{1/3}=0.4025\,\mathrm m\), so \(E_c=0.604\,\mathrm m\). The largest ideal bed rise that leaves the upstream state unchanged is

\[0.851-0.604=0.247\,\mathrm m.\]

For a smaller 0.15 m bed rise the crest must have \(E=0.7010\,\mathrm m\). Solving \(y+0.03262/y^2=0.7010\) on the deep branch gives approximately 0.614 m, which can be checked by substitution. Speed increases to about 1.30 m/s while the stream remains subcritical.

Raise the crest instead by 0.30 m. The unchanged upstream level would provide only 0.551 m specific energy, less than the minimum. If discharge stays fixed, the upstream surface must rise until at least \(E_1=0.30+0.604=0.904\,\mathrm m\) is available. The subcritical solution upstream is then approximately 0.860 m depth, a rise of about 60 mm. The physical limitation is the minimum section energy for the prescribed flow, not a loss of mass. Adding appreciable crest losses would require an even larger upstream head to sustain the same discharge.

A depth gauge right at a critical crest is relatively insensitive as an energy measurement: the first derivative \(dE/dy\) vanishes there. Conversely, inferring depth from a noisy measured head becomes sensitive near the minimum because the inverse slope becomes large. Measurements upstream and downstream help identify which of the two nearby depth branches is physically present.

Problem sheet 2 · Forces, flow work and losses

Use momentum to predict a support load and mechanical energy to predict pressures and pump power.

1. Calculate the load on a contraction

A horizontal water nozzle carries 0.020 m³/s. Its inlet area is 0.010 m², outlet area 0.0020 m², inlet gauge pressure 60 kPa and outlet atmospheric. Assume uniform profiles. (a) Find the momentum change per second. (b) Find the gauge force of the fluid on the nozzle. (c) Infer the head loss from the specified data.

Separate force and energy checks

The inlet and exit speeds are 2.0 and 10 m/s. Net downstream momentum flux is

\[20(10-2)=160\,\mathrm{N}.\]

Inlet pressure supplies 600 N, so the nozzle wall exerts −440 N on the fluid and the fluid exerts +440 N on the nozzle. The available pressure head is

\[60\,000/(1000g)=6.12\,\mathrm{m}.\]

The velocity head increases by \((100-4)/(2g)=4.89\) m, leaving a positive 1.22 m loss. A lossless calculation would yield a different inlet pressure for that flow.

2. Select pump duty for two reservoirs

A pump delivers 6.0 L/s to a vented reservoir 12 m above its source. The losses at this rate are 3.0 m. (a) Find hydraulic and shaft power at 75% efficiency. (b) If loss varies as \(Q^2\), repeat at 9.0 L/s. (c) Why is multiplying the first power by the flow ratio inadequate?

Add static lift and dissipative head

The original head is 15.0 m, so hydraulic power is \(1000(9.81)(0.006)(15)=883\) W and shaft power 1.18 kW. At 9 L/s the loss grows to \(3(1.5)^2=6.75\) m, giving 18.75 m total head. Hydraulic and shaft powers become 1.66 and 2.21 kW if efficiency remains unchanged. Flow enters directly in power and indirectly through the extra head needed to overcome losses.

3. Turn a free jet through 120 degrees

A 5.0 kg/s water jet travels horizontally at 8.0 m/s and leaves a fixed smooth guide at the same speed, turned 120° counterclockwise. (a) Find inlet and outlet velocity components. (b) Obtain the force of water on the guide. (c) Determine the guide’s mechanical power and reconcile it with its force.

Balance vector momentum

With the incoming jet along \(+x\), the outlet velocity is \((-4.0,6.93)\) m/s. The guide’s force on the water is \(5[(-4,6.93)-(8,0)]=(-60.0,34.6)\) N when gravity is negligible and both surfaces have atmospheric pressure. Water acts on the guide with \((60.0,-34.6)\) N. The stationary guide has zero velocity and its mechanical power is zero. It changes momentum direction without changing the jet’s speed or kinetic-energy rate.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Control-volume momentum and forces on fittings; Bernoulli head, losses and pump duty. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 5 · Viscous stresses, duct resistance and wall-driven flow

Derive a developed velocity profile from momentum balance and distinguish a moving-wall contribution from a pressure-driven contribution.

From first principles

Derive the parabolic velocity profile between stationary plates

Consider steady fully developed flow between wide parallel plates at \(y=0\) and \(y=H\). Assume incompressible Newtonian fluid, constant viscosity \(\mu\), no slip, no variation across the channel width and no streamwise body force. Let \(G=-dp/dx>0\) drive flow in the positive \(x\) direction. Since the axial velocity depends only on \(y\), the convective acceleration is zero. A thin fluid layer balances pressure force against the change of shear stress across its thickness.

\[0=-\frac{dp}{dx}+\frac{d\tau_{xy}}{dy},\]
\[\tau_{xy}=\mu\frac{du}{dy},\]
\[\mu u''=-G.\]

Integrate twice and apply zero velocity at both walls. The resulting velocity is largest halfway between the walls, where the shear stress vanishes. Integrating the profile gives discharge per unit width.

\[u(y)=\frac{G}{2\mu}y(H-y),\]
\[\frac Qb=\int_0^Hu(y)\,dy=\frac{GH^3}{12\mu}.\]

The cube of the gap arises because widening the channel increases both conducting area and the speed at a given pressure gradient. A moving wall adds a linear shear-driven profile through the boundary condition because the governing equation is linear.

Derivation and applications

Quantify the effect of wall slip on laminar pipe resistance

A pressure-flow calibration of a small tube can reveal a change in the assumed wall condition. Consider steady, fully developed, axisymmetric flow of a Newtonian liquid of viscosity \(\mu\) in a round tube of radius \(R\). Take the pressure gradient magnitude \(G=-dp/dz>0\) and neglect body forces along the tube. Balance pressure force against viscous drag on a coaxial fluid cylinder of radius \(r\). Positive axial speed decreases approaching the wall.

\[G\pi r^2=-\mu\frac{du}{dr}\,2\pi r,\]
\[\frac{du}{dr}=-\frac{Gr}{2\mu}.\]

Allow a Navier slip length \(b\geq0\) at the stationary wall: the wall speed extrapolates to zero a distance \(b\) beyond it in a local linear approximation. This gives

\[u(R)=-b\,u'(R).\]

Integrating the shear rate from the wall into the flow adds a uniform velocity offset to the usual parabola.

\[u(r)=\frac{G}{4\mu}(R^2-r^2+2bR).\]

Integrate the velocity over annuli to turn a local change of boundary condition into a measurable discharge. The contribution from the offset is proportional to the full area because the velocity addition is constant over the cross-section.

\[Q=2\pi\int_0^R u(r)r\,dr=\frac{\pi GR^4}{8\mu}\left(1+\frac{4b}{R}\right).\]

The shear stress needed to balance a fixed pressure gradient is unchanged. Effective slip lowers the required pressure gradient for a fixed flow. When \(b/R\) is small the fractional change is proportional to \(b/R\), making small tubes more sensitive at a given slip length.

Worked example

Calculate the extra discharge of a coated tube

Take \(R=100\) µm, \(b=5.00\) µm, \(\mu=1.00\) mPa s and \(G=10.0\) kPa/m. The no-slip flow is \(\pi(10^4)(10^{-4})^4/(8\times10^{-3})=0.393\) µL/s. Slip increases it by \(4b/R=0.200\), yielding 0.471 µL/s. The predicted wall speed \(GbR/(2\mu)=2.50\) mm/s could be examined by a near-wall tracer.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Infer a slip length from pressure reduction

A different tube of radius 40.0 µm requires only 80.0 percent of the reference no-slip pressure drop to sustain the same flow. Equal flow implies \(1+4b/R=1/0.800=1.25\), giving \(b=2.50\) µm. A radius calibration error can mimic this signal because the no-slip flow varies as \(R^4\). Independent bore measurements are therefore especially informative in this inverse experiment.

Open this live calculation

Transport momentum between neighbouring layers

Push two parallel plates past one another with liquid in the gap. Even at constant velocity the moving plate requires force. In a Newtonian fluid the tangential shear stress on a plane parallel to the motion is \(\tau_{xy}=\mu\,du/dy\). Dynamic viscosity \(\mu\) has units Pa·s. Divide it by density to obtain kinematic viscosity \(\nu\), an indicator of how rapidly momentum spreads through the fluid. Viscous resistance depends on a velocity gradient; uniform translation of the entire fluid would not create such stress.

Take two long horizontal plates separated by \(H\). Far from inlet and side walls assume constant density, constant viscosity and steady fully developed laminar motion \(u=u(y)\). The lower plate is fixed and the upper moves at speed \(U\). Represent the constant driving pressure gradient by \(G=-dp/dx\), positive for pressure falling downstream. A differential slab balances the downstream pressure force with the change in shear, giving \(0=G+d\tau_{xy}/dy=G+\mu u''\).

Impose the two wall velocities

The first integration gives

\[\mu u'=-Gy+C_1.\]

Integrating again and imposing no slip \(u(0)=0,\ u(H)=U\) determines the constants. The result is the sum of a straight wall-driven profile and a parabolic pressure-driven one:

\[u(y)=U\frac{y}{H}+\frac{G}{2\mu}y(H-y),\]
\[\frac{Q}{b}=\int_0^H u\,dy=\frac{UH}{2}+\frac{GH^3}{12\mu}.\]

Superposition here follows because the reduced equation and boundary conditions are linear in these inputs. It is not a universal property of the full nonlinear fluid equations. A negative \(G\) represents an adverse pressure rise and can produce a reverse-flow layer close to the stationary wall even when the moving wall drags fluid forward elsewhere.

Predict the pressure a moving surface can hold

An oil film has \(\mu=0.050\) Pa·s, \(H=1.0\) mm and \(U=0.20\) m/s. The wall-driven flux per unit width is \(UH/2=1.0\times10^{-4}\) m²/s. An adverse pressure gradient sufficient to reduce net flux to zero must satisfy \(G=-6\mu U/H^2=-60\,000\) Pa/m. Over 50 mm, pressure rises by 3.0 kPa. Zero net transport does not make the oil stationary.

Set \(s=y/H\) and insert the zero-flux gradient. The velocity is \(u/U=3s^2-2s\), which changes sign at \(s=2/3\) inside the film. The velocity gradient at the upper moving wall is \(4U/H=800\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\), so the wall supplies shear of magnitude 40 Pa. Even though the pressure-driven and wall-driven volume rates cancel, the moving surface still performs work, dissipated through the internal shear field.

Connect a local solution to a network

In a horizontal circular tube with fixed wall, axisymmetry changes the area that carries shear. Balancing forces on a cylinder of radius \(r\) gives

\[|\tau|=Gr/2.\]

Integrating the velocity gradient with no slip yields

\[u=G(R^2-r^2)/(4\mu),\]
\[Q=\pi R^4\Delta p/(8\mu L).\]

Thus a straight developed pipe behaves as a hydraulic resistance \(8\mu L/(\pi R^4)\) in Pa·s/m³. Series resistances carry the same \(Q\) and their pressure drops add. Parallel branches share pressure drop and their flows add. The analogy requires consistent units and a laminar regime; short entrance sections and strongly accelerating junctions may add losses outside the fully developed solution.

Express pipe resistance as a friction factor

For a circular pipe of diameter \(D=2R\), the developed laminar solution gives

\[\Delta p=32\mu L\bar u/D^2.\]

Define the Darcy friction factor by \(\Delta p=f_D(L/D)\rho\bar u^2/2\). Equating these expressions produces \(f_D=64/\mathrm{Re}_D\). For 2 m of 10 mm pipe with mean speed 0.10 m/s, water viscosity 0.001 Pa s and density 1000 kg/m³, the Reynolds number is 1000 and the pressure drop is 64 Pa. A friction factor listed in the Fanning convention is one quarter of the Darcy value, so identify the convention before substituting a tabulated number into a loss calculation.

Derive the profile of a shear-thinning liquid in a pipe

A Newtonian viscosity inferred in one duct may give poor predictions for a suspension or polymer solution at another rate. In a simple power-law model the shear stress magnitude is \(\tau=K|\dot\gamma|^n\), with consistency \(K\) in Pa sⁿ and index \(n\). A liquid with \(n<1\) has apparent viscosity decreasing with shear rate. Assume this law is supported over the shear range being sampled, flow is steady, axisymmetric, laminar and fully developed, and the circular pipe has no wall slip. The axial force balance is independent of rheology: at radius \(r\), \(\tau=Gr/2\) for driving gradient \(G=-dp/dx>0\).

Since the positive downstream velocity decreases towards the wall, write \(-du/dr=(Gr/2K)^{1/n}\). Integrate from radius \(r\) to the stationary wall at \(R\), then integrate the result over the circular cross-section to obtain

\[u(r)=\frac{n}{n+1}(G/2K)^{1/n}(R^{1+1/n}-r^{1+1/n}),\]
\[Q=\frac{\pi n}{3n+1}(G/2K)^{1/n}R^{3+1/n}.\]

Setting \(n=1,\ K=\mu\) gives the parabolic profile and \(Q=\pi GR^4/(8\mu)\). For \(n<1\) the velocity is flatter towards the centre and the relationship between pressure gradient and flow rate is nonlinear. The ideal power law can imply unrealistic apparent viscosity near zero shear at the axis; a fitted model with finite limiting viscosities is preferable when measurements resolve those regimes.

Infer a pressure demand from wall shear

A model resin follows \(n=0.50,\ K=0.80\,\mathrm{Pa\,s^{0.5}}\) in the operating range. A 2.0 m pipe of radius 5.0 mm experiences a pressure drop of 8.0 kPa, giving \(G=4000\,\mathrm{Pa/m}\) and wall shear \(\tau_w=GR/2=10.0\,\mathrm{Pa}\). The wall shear rate is

\[(10/0.80)^2=156.25\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}.\]

Writing the mean speed in terms of this wall value gives

\[\bar u=nR|\dot\gamma_w|/(3n+1)=0.15625\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

The corresponding flow is \(\pi(0.005)^2(0.15625)=1.227\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^3/s}\), about 0.736 L/min.

To double flow in this same pipe, the power-law relation requires the gradient to rise by \(2^n=\sqrt2\). The new pressure drop is 11.3 kPa, and wall shear rate doubles to 312.5 s⁻¹. A linear resistance model extrapolated from the original measurement would predict 16 kPa. Conversely at fixed gradient a 10% radius increase multiplies rate by \(1.1^{3+1/n}=1.61\), a stronger dependence than the Newtonian fourth power. Verify the resulting shear rate still falls in the tested rheological range, and check entrance length and temperature. Temperature changes produced by viscous dissipation can otherwise mimic a shear-dependent viscosity.

Two well-spaced steady pressure tests can independently infer the unknown exponent. At fixed pipe geometry,

\[\ln Q=(1/n)\ln\Delta p+\text{constant}.\]

If pressure drop rises from 8.0 to 12.0 kPa while flow rises from 0.736 to 1.66 L/min, the log slope is \(\ln(1.66/0.736)/\ln(12/8)\simeq2.00\), giving \(n\simeq0.50\). Repeating the higher-pressure point after cooling checks whether heating caused hysteresis between runs. Using an inlet pressure transducer alone would also include a changing downstream pressure; both end pressures or a differential gauge are needed to identify the flow law from this experiment.

The pressure taps should bracket the same developed length at every setting because entrance losses may change differently with flow.

How does a local pressure gradient accelerate a fluid particle?

A duct solution generally begins by cancelling terms in the momentum balance. To decide which terms can be cancelled, follow a small volume of fluid. Its acceleration includes the change in the velocity at its present location and the change as it moves into a different part of the velocity field. Applying the chain rule along its trajectory gives

\[D\mathbf u/Dt=\partial_t\mathbf u+(\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u.\]

Consider the two faces normal to the first coordinate axis. Their net surface force per unit volume in direction \(i\) approaches \(\partial_x\sigma_{ix}\) as the face separation tends to zero. Summing the three face pairs and adding weight gives

\[\rho Du_i/Dt=\sum_j\partial_j\sigma_{ij}+\rho g_i.\]

For an incompressible Newtonian liquid of constant viscosity \(\mu\), insert \(\sigma_{ij}=-p\delta_{ij}+\mu(\partial_j u_i+\partial_i u_j)\). The cross derivative sums to \(\partial_i(\nabla\cdot\mathbf u)=0\). The remaining terms are the local equations

\[\rho[\partial_t\mathbf u+(\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u]=-\nabla p+\mu\nabla^2\mathbf u+\rho\mathbf g,\]
\[\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0.\]

Take a horizontal slice through an ideal planar stagnation region, away from confining walls. The steady field \(u=ax,\ v=-ay\) satisfies continuity. Every velocity component is linear, so its Laplacian vanishes. Nevertheless the material acceleration is \((a^2x,a^2y)\). Integrating the two horizontal gradients gives

\[p(x,y)=p_0-\rho a^2(x^2+y^2)/2,\]

where \(p_0\) is the stagnation-point pressure at this elevation. For water at \(1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\), \(a=4\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\) and \(y=0.10\,\mathrm m,\ x=0\), pressure is 80 Pa below \(p_0\). Fluid entering along positive \(y\) moves towards the origin but accelerates in positive \(y\): it slows as pressure rises. At the stagnation point itself both velocity and acceleration vanish. Specifying \(p_0\) fixes the pressure datum. The velocity gradients determine the spatial change.

Chapter 6 · Dynamic similarity, boundary layers and model tests

Choose dimensionless groups for a fluid experiment and determine which full-scale predictions a scale model can support.

From first principles

Read similarity conditions from the nondimensional momentum balance

Two geometrically similar channels can behave differently when inertia, viscosity and gravity contribute in different proportions. Use incompressible Newtonian flow with density \(\rho\), kinematic viscosity \(\nu\) and constant gravitational acceleration \(\mathbf g\). Choose length \(L\), speed \(U\), elapsed time \(L/U\) and pressure scale \(\rho U^2\). The underlying balance is force per unit mass equals acceleration.

\[\partial_t\mathbf u+\mathbf u\cdot\nabla\mathbf u=-\rho^{-1}\nabla p+\nu\nabla^2\mathbf u+\mathbf g .\]

Insert \(\mathbf u=U\mathbf u^*\), \(\mathbf x=L\mathbf x^*\) and the other scales. Divide through by \(U^2/L\). The viscosity and gravity terms acquire coefficients that compare their acceleration scales with inertia.

\[\partial_{t^*}\mathbf u^*+\mathbf u^*\cdot\nabla^*\mathbf u^*=-\nabla^*p^*+\frac1{Re}\nabla^{*2}\mathbf u^*+\frac1{Fr^2}\mathbf e_g,\]
\[Re=\frac{UL}{\nu},\]
\[Fr=\frac U{\sqrt{gL}} .\]

Here \(\mathbf e_g\) points downwards. Matching the relevant coefficients and dimensionless initial and boundary conditions gives the same nondimensional problem. Choosing what to match therefore depends on which physical effects matter for the measured observable.

Background · Reynolds number
\[\mathrm{Re}=\rho UL/\mu\]

Choose speed and length scales appropriate to the geometry. Matching this ratio compares inertial and viscous terms under the stated nondimensionalisation.

Derivation and applications

Scale a rising interface with Reynolds and Froude numbers

A free-surface model can match gravitational wave motion and still misrepresent viscous drag. Consider two geometrically similar open-channel experiments with characteristic lengths \(L_m,L_p\), speeds \(U_m,U_p\), gravitational accelerations \(g_m,g_p\) and kinematic viscosities \(\nu_m,\nu_p\). Ignore surface tension only when the Bond number is sufficiently large in both channels. Compare the inertial acceleration \(U^2/L\) with gravity and viscous acceleration \(\nu U/L^2\). Their ratios define

\[Fr=\frac{U}{\sqrt{gL}},\]
\[Re=\frac{UL}{\nu}.\]

Let \(\lambda=L_m/L_p\) be the model-to-prototype length ratio. Froude similarity fixes the velocity ratio because the shape and timing of long free-surface disturbances depend on the competition with gravity. Substituting this speed into Reynolds similarity fixes the required viscosity ratio as a second independent condition.

\[\frac{U_m}{U_p}=\sqrt{\lambda\,\frac{g_m}{g_p}},\]
\[\frac{\nu_m}{\nu_p}=\lambda^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{g_m}{g_p}}.\]

A travel time scales as length divided by velocity, and discharge scales as velocity times geometrically similar area. The matching free-surface kinematics therefore predict two distinct dimensional scale factors.

\[\frac{t_m}{t_p}=\sqrt{\lambda\,\frac{g_p}{g_m}},\]
\[\frac{Q_m}{Q_p}=\lambda^{5/2}\sqrt{\frac{g_m}{g_p}} .\]

When the same liquid and gravity are used, exact equality of both dimensionless numbers is possible only at full scale. If boundary-layer details matter in a smaller channel, the calculated Reynolds mismatch quantifies what a Froude-scaled measurement leaves unresolved.

Worked example

Interpret a one-to-nine water channel

At equal gravity, \(\lambda=1/9\) gives a speed ratio of \(1/3\). A model flow of 0.400 m/s corresponds to 1.20 m/s at full scale. A 2.00 s wave passage scales to 6.00 s. With the same water, \(Re_m/Re_p=\lambda^{3/2}=1/27\), so a prototype Reynolds number of 270,000 becomes 10,000. Matching model surface-wave timing alone gives no guarantee of the same wall-shear coefficient.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Choose the viscosity for simultaneous matching

In a separate equal-gravity test, \(\lambda=1/4\) and the prototype liquid has \(\nu_p=8.00\) mm²/s. Simultaneous similarity requires \(\nu_m=(1/4)^{3/2}(8.00)=1.00\) mm²/s. A prototype discharge of 64.0 L/s maps to \(64.0(1/4)^{5/2}=2.00\) L/s. This pair of viscosity and discharge targets specifies two independently measurable conditions for commissioning the model.
