OneSimulation laboratory · Measure boundary-layer growth from a velocity profile

Compare competing mechanisms

A scale model can reproduce a shape exactly and still display a different flow pattern. A fast oil stream and a slow water stream may behave similarly if the relevant dimensionless groups match. To see which groups enter, choose a typical length \(L\), speed \(U\), time \(L/U\) and pressure change \(\rho U^2\) in the incompressible Newtonian momentum equation. Divide by the inertial scale \(\rho U^2/L\). The coefficient multiplying viscous diffusion becomes \(1/Re\), where \(Re=\rho UL/\mu\).

If gravity must be retained, its coefficient is \(1/Fr^2\), with \(Fr=U/\sqrt{gL}\). A free water surface makes this particularly important because gravity restores disturbances. For a fully submerged shape in a constant-density fluid, gravity can often be absorbed into hydrostatic pressure, leaving the Reynolds number dominant. Extra effects can introduce more groups, for example roughness divided by length or oscillation frequency times \(L/U\). Choose groups to match the phenomenon being measured.

Derive a near-wall length scale

Consider a smooth flat plate parallel to a uniform stream with a negligible pressure gradient. In a laminar boundary layer, momentum is advected downstream on a scale \(U^2/x\) and diffuses across the shear layer on a scale \(\nu U/\delta^2\). Balancing them gives \(\delta\sim\sqrt{\nu x/U}\). The ratio \(\delta/x\sim Re_x^{-1/2}\) may be small even though the tangential shear force from the layer matters to the machine. The similarity solution places the 99% speed level near \(5x/\sqrt{Re_x}\), and the local skin-friction coefficient \(\tau_w/(\rho U^2/2)\) near \(0.664/\sqrt{Re_x}\).

Doubling speed at a fixed station multiplies the local Reynolds number by two. The dimensionless friction coefficient falls by \(1/\sqrt2\), but dynamic pressure grows fourfold. The dimensional shear therefore rises by \(2^{3/2}\). This distinction matters when reading plots of nondimensional test results: a lower plotted coefficient can accompany a larger actual force.

Scale a submerged component

A streamlined full-size probe of length 0.60 m moves at 2.0 m/s in water with \(\nu=1.0\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\). A geometrically similar third-scale model in the same water needs 6.0 m/s for equal Reynolds number \(1.2\times10^6\). Assume matching surface finish in relative terms, sufficiently small blockage, the same flow regime and no free-surface effect. Then the dimensionless drag \(C_D=F_D/(\rho U^2A/2)\) should agree. Since the model’s reference area is one ninth and its speed squared is nine times as large, its drag is equal to the prototype drag in this example.

If only 2.0 m/s can be reached in the test tank, the model Reynolds number is one third of full size. An observed transition, separation point or drag coefficient may then shift. Document this mismatch rather than scaling the raw measured drag by area alone. Matching a single group does not fix every boundary condition, especially the upstream disturbance level that can trigger transition.

Scale a different experiment with the same geometry

For a one-ninth length model of a surface vessel, matching Froude number requires

\[U_m=U_p/3.\]

In the same water its Reynolds number relative to the prototype is

\[(1/3)(1/9)=1/27.\]

Exact Froude and Reynolds similarity cannot both hold at that length and speed with the same fluid. Gravity-wave resistance can be compared at matched Froude number, while viscous skin friction needs a separate correction. Report both dimensionless numbers with the measured force and its reference area. That allows a reader to distinguish the physical part of the scaling argument from assumptions used for extrapolation.

Recover a dimensional frequency from a model

Matching a dimensionless frequency can transfer an unsteady effect. For an oscillation of frequency \(f\) in a stream of speed \(U\) past a length \(L\), the Strouhal number is \(fL/U\). A 0.10 m model in a 3 m/s stream oscillating at 6 Hz gives 0.20. If relevant geometry and governing dimensionless parameters are matched, a 0.50 m prototype at 2 m/s would oscillate at \(0.20(2)/0.50=0.80\) Hz. Frequency alone cannot be rescaled by the geometric factor when the reference speeds differ.

Determine when surface tension prevents gravity-wave similarity

A reduced-scale flume can match gravity and inertia by matching Froude number. The air–water interface also has surface tension \(\sigma\). A disturbance with characteristic length \(L\) and speed \(U\) has inertial pressure scale \(\rho U^2\), gravity scale \(\rho gL\), and capillary pressure scale \(\sigma/L\). The ratios produce \(\mathrm{Fr}^2=U^2/(gL)\) and \(\mathrm{We}=\rho U^2L/\sigma\). Dividing the latter by the former gives the Bond number \(\mathrm{Bo}=\rho gL^2/\sigma\). Choosing model speed to match Froude number leaves Bond number determined by geometry and material properties. With the same liquid and gravity, reducing length by ten reduces Bond number by one hundred.

For a more precise test use the small-amplitude, inviscid deep-water dispersion relation \(\omega^2=gk+\sigma k^3/\rho\) for wave number \(k=2\pi/\lambda\). The gravity term arises from surface elevation, while the capillary pressure perturbation is proportional to surface curvature \(k^2\eta\). The ratio of capillary to gravity restoring contributions is \(\sigma k^2/(\rho g)\). Equal contributions occur at wavelength \(2\pi\sqrt{\sigma/(\rho g)}\). The appropriate length in this ratio is \(1/k\); omitting the \(2\pi\) when using wavelength changes the inferred correction substantially.

Check a wave-frequency prediction at two geometric scales

For water use \(\sigma=0.072\,\mathrm{N/m},\ \rho=1000\,\mathrm{kg/m^3}\) and \(g=9.81\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\). The crossover wavelength is

\[2\pi\sqrt{0.072/9810}=0.0170\,\mathrm m.\]

Consider a prototype wave of wavelength 0.40 m. Its wave number is 15.71 m⁻¹ and the capillary-to-gravity ratio is

\[0.072(15.71)^2/9810=0.00181.\]

Its frequency is approximately \(\sqrt{9.81(15.71)(1.00181)}/(2\pi)=1.98\,\mathrm{Hz}\), essentially the gravity prediction.

A geometrically similar one-tenth model uses wavelength 0.040 m, wave number 157.1 m⁻¹ and capillary-to-gravity ratio 0.181. Pure gravity scaling would predict frequency \(\sqrt{10}(1.98)=6.26\,\mathrm{Hz}\). Including capillarity gives \(\sqrt{9.81(157.1)(1.181)}/(2\pi)=6.79\,\mathrm{Hz}\), about 8.5% higher. A frequency discrepancy need not indicate a clock error or a viscosity error. The model has moved into a region where a restoring force previously negligible is perceptible. Deep water also requires \(kh\) large at both scales; shallow flume depth adds a separate \(\tanh(kh)\) factor. Enlarging the model or choosing a different measured observable may be preferable to enforcing incompatible similarity conditions.

A useful comparison can be made by timing wave packets as well as crests. The phase speed is

\[c_p=\omega/k.\]

The group speed of a narrow wave packet follows from differentiating the dispersion relation,

\[c_g=[g+3\sigma k^2/\rho]/(2\omega).\]

Their ratio is \([1+3B]/[2(1+B)]\), where \(B=\sigma k^2/(\rho g)\). In the prototype example it is 0.502, close to the deep-water gravity value of one half. In the short model it is 0.653. Thus scaling time to match one measured crest frequency still would not make packets travel relative to their crests in exactly the same way. A video showing both individual crest motion and arrival of a packet envelope provides experimental evidence for the altered mechanism.

Surface contamination is another experimental variable: an altered surface tension changes the capillary correction without changing either geometric length or gravity-wave Froude number.

Study the full derivation

Settling spheres, Stokes drag and a wall correction · Momentum, motion and transport in fluids

A falling-sphere viscometer infers viscosity from terminal speed. Derive the drag on a no-slip sphere, distinguish creeping flow from merely steady motion and use radius, vessel width and orientation checks to decide what the speed experiment actually measures.

Problem sheet 3 · Viscous transport and similarity

Derive resistance or a scaling law first, then test that its flow-regime assumptions still apply.

1. Split a laminar flow

Two long circular pipes of the same length connect common manifolds. Their radii are 2 mm and 3 mm and the fluid is the same. (a) Find the flow ratio. (b) Split a total of 6.0 mL/s. (c) If both radii are reduced by 10%, how does the required pressure drop change at fixed total flow?

Apply the common-pressure condition

At equal pressure drop the radius-fourth law gives

\[Q_2/Q_1=(3/2)^4=5.0625.\]

Thus \(Q_1=6/(1+5.0625)=0.990\) mL/s and \(Q_2=5.01\) mL/s. Reducing both radii scales each conductance by \(0.9^4\), so the common pressure drop must increase by \(1/0.9^4=1.52\). This assumes both flows remain developed and laminar and manifold losses remain negligible.

2. Compare pressure and wall driving

A 2 mm film of viscosity 0.10 Pa·s is bounded by a fixed lower plate and an upper plate moving at 0.40 m/s. A downstream pressure fall produces \(G=3000\) Pa/m. (a) Find the two contributions to \(Q/b\). (b) Find the midpoint speed. (c) What pressure gradient would produce zero net flux?

Superpose the profiles

The moving-wall and pressure terms are \(UH/2=4.0\times10^{-4}\) and \(GH^3/(12\mu)=2.0\times10^{-5}\) m²/s, totalling 0.00042 m²/s. At the middle, \(u=U/2+GH^2/(8\mu)=0.215\) m/s. To cancel wall transport one needs \(G=-6\mu U/H^2=-60\,000\) Pa/m. The negative sign represents a pressure rise in the direction of plate motion.

3. Design a reduced-scale tow test

A 2 m submerged object is towed at 1.5 m/s in water with \(\nu=10^{-6}\) m²/s. A geometrically similar 0.50 m model is available. (a) Find the required speed for equal Reynolds number in the same water. (b) For equal drag coefficient, compare model and prototype drag. (c) If surface waves dominate instead, find the speed for equal Froude number and the resulting Reynolds-number ratio.

Match a dimensionless balance

Prototype Reynolds number is \(3.0\times10^6\). At quarter length the required model speed is 6.0 m/s. Same density and drag coefficient imply \(F_m/F_p=(6/1.5)^2(0.5/2)^2=1\). Froude matching would require \(1.5\sqrt{0.25}=0.75\) m/s, giving \(Re_m/Re_p=(0.75/1.5)(0.5/2)=1/8\). The choice of matching rule changes the interpretation of the measured drag.

Which pressure difference establishes an accelerating uniform stream?

In the uniform core of a horizontal water test channel let \(u=0.60t\) in SI units for the first two seconds, with negligible transverse velocity and negligible viscous stresses in the core. Two pressure taps sampling the core at the same elevation are 1.50 m apart. Find the material acceleration and the pressure difference, then compare this result at \(t=2\) s with a steady core moving at the same instantaneous speed. Use \(\rho=998\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\).

Show the worked solution

Spatial gradients vanish, so the advective term is zero. During the ramp \(Du/Dt=0.60\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}\), hence

\[\partial_xp=-998(0.60)=-598.8\,\mathrm{Pa\,m^{-1}}.\]

Integrating downstream gives \(p_\mathrm{up}-p_\mathrm{down}=898.2\,\mathrm{Pa}\). Both cases have \(u=1.20\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\) at the instant of comparison. In the steady uniform ideal core the local derivative also vanishes, so the tap difference is zero. A calculation using speed alone could not distinguish them. Actual wall friction, if significant, would demand an additional pressure gradient.

Chapter 7 · Vorticity, circulation and swirling flow

Distinguish rotation of a fluid element from an orbiting streamline and use radial momentum balance to predict a free-surface shape.

From first principles

Derive vorticity from circulation around a small rectangle

In a smooth planar velocity field write velocity as \((u,v)\) and consider a fixed small rectangle of sides \(dx,dy\). Traverse it counterclockwise and add the component of velocity along each side times the side length. Uniform velocity cancels between opposite sides. A difference in vertical velocity across \(dx\) or horizontal velocity across \(dy\) can leave a net circulation.

\[d\Gamma=[v(x+dx,y)-v(x,y)]dy-[u(x,y+dy)-u(x,y)]dx .\]

Taylor expansion and division by the enclosed area give the local circulation density, or axial vorticity. Adding neighbouring rectangles cancels the internal boundaries and yields the finite-area formula.

\[\omega_z=\lim_{dA\to0}\frac{d\Gamma}{dA}=\partial_xv-\partial_yu,\]
\[\Gamma=\oint_C\mathbf u\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell=\int_A\omega_z\,dA .\]

For solid-body rotation \(u=-\Omega y\), \(v=\Omega x\), so \(\omega_z=2\Omega\). A fluid element's local angular velocity is half its vorticity. This local rate can vanish even on a curved streamline because the motion of a parcel centre and the deformation around it are different observations.

Background · Vorticity
\[\boldsymbol\omega=\nabla\times\mathbf u\]

Its units are reciprocal seconds. Circulation around a closed loop integrates the corresponding vorticity flux when the interior is regular.

Derivation and applications

Reconstruct the core of a vortex from circulation

A vortex in a stirred tank often rotates nearly as a solid body close to its axis and approximately as a potential vortex farther out. Model a steady planar axisymmetric flow with tangential speed \(v_\theta\), no radial velocity, and a circular core of radius \(a\). Choose uniform axial vorticity \(\omega_0>0\) in the core and zero vorticity outside. For a circle of radius \(r\), Stokes' theorem relates the tangential speed to the vorticity integrated over the enclosed disk.

\[2\pi r v_\theta(r)=\int_0^r\omega_z(s)2\pi s\,ds=\pi\omega_0\min(r^2,a^2).\]

Inside the core the circulation grows with area. Outside it, the entire core is already enclosed, so the same circulation is spread over a larger circumference. The speed is continuous and reaches its maximum at the core radius.

\[v_\theta(r)=\begin{cases}\omega_0 r/2,&r\leq a,\\ \omega_0 a^2/(2r),&r\geq a.\end{cases}\]

For a horizontal flow of constant density \(\rho\), radial momentum balance gives

\[dp/dr=\rho v_\theta^2/r.\]

Integrate first from the centre to the core radius and then from the core to a distant radius where the swirl has decayed. The two regions make equal contributions to the centre-to-infinity pressure deficit.

\[p(\infty)-p(0)=\rho\left[\int_0^a\frac{\omega_0^2r}{4}dr+\int_a^\infty\frac{\omega_0^2a^4}{4r^3}dr\right]=\frac{\rho\omega_0^2a^2}{4}.\]

The idealised sharp change of vorticity at \(a\) replaces a smooth viscous transition. A finite vessel truncates the exterior integral, so the pressure reference radius must be specified when it lies close to the core.

Worked example

Predict a core pressure deficit

A water vortex has \(a=30.0\) mm and \(\omega_0=40.0\) s⁻¹. The peak tangential speed is 0.600 m/s and its exterior circulation is \(\pi(40.0)(0.0300)^2=0.113\) m²/s. The predicted pressure difference from the centre to distant liquid at the same height is

\[1000(0.600)^2=360\,\mathrm{Pa}.\]

Half of this rise occurs within the core.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Infer the core radius from two speed measurements

A second vortex has speed 0.200 m/s at \(r=10.0\) mm inside the core and 0.250 m/s at \(r=50.0\) mm outside. The inner reading gives \(\omega_0=2(0.200)/0.0100=40.0\) s⁻¹. The outer reading gives \(a=\sqrt{2rv_\theta/\omega_0}=25.0\) mm, between the measuring radii as assumed. Its maximum speed should therefore be 0.500 m/s.

Open this live calculation

Distinguish a particle orbit from local spin

Drop a small paddle wheel into a swirling tank. Its centre can travel around the tank axis whether or not the wheel itself spins relative to distant reference axes. To measure local rotation, consider two short perpendicular material lines through the same fluid element. In a planar velocity field \((u,v)\), an initially horizontal line rotates at \(\partial v/\partial x\), and an initially vertical line rotates at \(-\partial u/\partial y\), both with counterclockwise positive. Their mean is the local angular velocity, one half the out-of-plane vorticity.

\[\omega_z=\partial v/\partial x-\partial u/\partial y,\]
\[\Gamma=\oint_C\mathbf v\cdot d\mathbf l=\iint_A\omega_z\,dA .\]

The second relation is Stokes’ theorem. The curve encloses \(A\) with consistent orientation and the velocity must be regular throughout that surface. Circulation is measured in square metres per second. In a region with zero vorticity a closed curve can still enclose a singular or otherwise excluded core; then the area needed for the usual zero-circulation argument does not lie entirely in that region.

Compare two angular velocity distributions

In axisymmetric swirl with only tangential velocity \(v_\theta(r)\), circulation on a circular path is \(2\pi r v_\theta\). Dividing its change between neighbouring rings by the annular area gives

\[\omega_z=(1/r)\,d(rv_\theta)/dr.\]

A forced vortex rotates like a rigid body, \(v_\theta=\Omega r\), with vorticity \(2\Omega\). At radius 0.10 m and angular speed 8 rad/s, its velocity is 0.80 m/s and the circular-path circulation is 0.503 m²/s.

In a free-vortex exterior \(v_\theta=K/r\); the vorticity is zero at every regular point there although the streamlines are circular. If its velocity at the same radius is also 0.80 m/s, then \(K=0.080\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\). At radius 0.20 m its speed falls to 0.40 m/s, while in the forced vortex the speed rises to 1.60 m/s. Both flows have the same circulation at the original radius. A second measuring circle distinguishes them without an instrument that directly senses local shear.

Recover pressure and free-surface rise

For steady axisymmetric circular streamlines without radial or vertical motion the radial acceleration points inward with magnitude \(v_\theta^2/r\). Radial momentum balance requires

\[dp/dr=\rho v_\theta^2/r.\]

Vertical equilibrium gives

\[\partial p/\partial z=-\rho g.\]

Along a free surface at uniform ambient pressure their contributions cancel, so \(dz/dr=v_\theta^2/(gr)\). For rigid rotation, integration gives

\[z(r)-z(0)=\Omega^2r^2/(2g).\]

A cylindrical open tank of radius 0.20 m rotating at 8 rad/s has a predicted wall-to-centre rise of \(64(0.20)^2/[2(9.81)]=0.1305\,\mathrm m\). If no water spills, volume conservation places its former flat level halfway between the new centre and rim elevations for this paraboloid. Each shifts by about 65 mm in opposite directions. A nearly full tank will spill instead, and the average-level constraint then changes. A free vortex produces a different surface profile and its singular axis must be replaced by a physical core.

Construct irrotational flow around a cylinder

Outside a vortex core or boundary layer, a two-dimensional incompressible flow can often be approximated as irrotational. In a simply connected region irrotationality permits a velocity potential with \(\mathbf v=\nabla\phi\), and continuity then requires

\[\nabla^2\phi=0.\]

For a circular cylinder of radius \(a\) in a uniform stream \(U\) along the positive \(x\) direction, try \(\phi=(Ar+B/r)\cos\theta\). The two radial factors solve Laplace’s equation for the angular first harmonic. Matching the distant uniform potential \(Ur\cos\theta\) gives \(A=U\). No penetration requires radial velocity \(\partial_r\phi=0\) at \(r=a\), giving \(B=Ua^2\).

\[v_r=U(1-a^2/r^2)\cos\theta,\]
\[v_\theta=-U(1+a^2/r^2)\sin\theta .\]

The boundary has zero normal speed but a nonzero tangential speed. This is consistent with an inviscid slip condition. It does not satisfy the no-slip condition for an actual viscous wall, so predicted surface pressure is meaningful only insofar as outer flow remains attached and nearly irrotational. In a real cylinder wake, separation breaks the fore–aft symmetry of this ideal solution.

Applying steady Bernoulli relative to the uniform upstream stream gives the surface pressure coefficient \(C_p=1-4\sin^2\theta\). Its values change considerably around the cylinder even though integrating the pressure forces yields zero net drag and lift by symmetry. Zero potential-flow drag does not imply uniform pressure.

Add circulation and predict unequal side pressures

A mathematical cylinder excludes its interior from the fluid. Superpose a free-vortex velocity \(\Gamma/(2\pi r)\) in the positive counterclockwise direction; this preserves the no-penetration condition while changing circulation around the body. Its sign adds to the negative surface velocity on one side and opposes it on the other. For a 50 mm radius cylinder in air at \(U=10\,\mathrm{m/s}\), choose \(\Gamma=0.50\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\) counterclockwise. The added surface speed is

\[0.50/[2\pi(0.050)]=1.59\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

At the top the signed tangential speed is −18.41 m/s, and at the bottom it is +21.59 m/s.

For air density 1.20 kg/m³ the dynamic pressure is 60 Pa. The top and bottom coefficients \(1-(v_\theta/U)^2\) are −2.39 and −3.66, respectively. The lower pressure is now at the bottom, producing downward lift. Integrate \(-p\,n_y a\,d\theta\) around the circumference: the terms even between top and bottom cancel, leaving signed lift per span \(L'=-\rho U\Gamma=-6.0\,\mathrm{N/m}\) for these axes. A rigid-body rotation of the entire fluid would give a different vorticity field; the superposed free vortex has zero local vorticity outside the excluded body. Physical circulation in an experiment depends on how the flow and surface boundary layers are established, and cannot be selected by the potential solution alone.

The stagnation points satisfy \(\sin\theta=\Gamma/(4\pi aU)\), or 0.0796 here. They shift by about 4.6° from their zero-circulation positions without changing the fact that pressure is highest where speed vanishes. This offers a second observable for estimating circulation, independent of integrating the measured lift, provided the observed surface points reasonably follow the attached outer-flow model.

Chapter 8 · Boundary layers and momentum thickness

Connect a measured velocity deficit to wall drag and distinguish the physical edge of a boundary layer from its integral thicknesses.

From first principles

Obtain wall drag from the streamwise momentum deficit

A thin boundary layer slows fluid next to a solid wall. Consider steady two-dimensional flow over a smooth flat plate with constant external speed \(U\) and pressure. Use unit span and constant density \(\rho\). Choose a control volume ending at location \(x\), with its upper surface a streamline outside the layer. Mass conservation relates the uniform inlet flow to the downstream velocity profile. Multiplying the discharged mass in a strip by the streamwise speed it has lost gives that strip's contribution to the momentum deficit.

\[dD=\rho\,u(y)[U-u(y)]\,dy,\]
\[D(x)=\rho U^2\theta(x),\]
\[\theta=\int_0^\infty\frac uU\left(1-\frac uU\right)dy .\]

The integrated deficit equals the drag per unit span exerted by the wall on the flow in magnitude. Extending the plate by a small \(dx\) adds wall drag \(\tau_w\,dx\), so differentiating yields

\[\tau_w=\rho U^2\frac{d\theta}{dx},\]
\[C_f=\frac{\tau_w}{\rho U^2/2}=2\frac{d\theta}{dx}.\]

These relations assume negligible pressure gradient. The thickness \(\theta\) weights lost velocity by the mass flux actually present in that part of the profile.

Derivation and applications

Relate displacement thickness to boundary-layer entrainment

Mass conservation requires a weak normal flow at the edge of a developing boundary layer. Consider steady, two-dimensional incompressible flow over an impermeable flat plate with constant external speed \(U\). Let \(u(x,y)\) be tangential velocity, \(v(x,y)\) normal velocity and \(y=0\) the wall. Integrate continuity from the wall to a fixed measurement height \(H\) chosen well outside the layer throughout the region of interest. Since \(v(x,0)=0\),

\[\partial_x u+\partial_y v=0,\]
\[v(x,H)=-\frac{d}{dx}\int_0^H u(x,y)\,dy .\]

The integral would equal \(UH\) for a uniform velocity profile. The missing volume flux per unit span defines displacement thickness \(\delta^*\) times \(U\). For constant \(H,U\), differentiating cancels the large uniform part and connects the missing flux directly to the weak external normal velocity.

\[U\delta^*=\int_0^H(U-u)\,dy,\]
\[v(x,H)=U\frac{d\delta^*}{dx}.\]

For an illustrative profile take \(u/U=2\eta-\eta^2\) up to \(\eta=y/\delta=1\) and \(u=U\) above it. Its deficit is \((1-\eta)^2\) and its integral is one third. When \(\delta\) grows as the square root of distance from the leading edge, the normal velocity follows by differentiation.

\[\delta^*=\frac{\delta}{3},\]
\[\delta=C\sqrt x\ \Longrightarrow\ v(x,H)=\frac{U\delta}{6x}.\]

The sign is positive because the low-speed layer occupies an increasing share of the fixed-height section. The value at fixed external height differs from the mass entering a control surface that itself follows the growing edge.

Worked example

Estimate normal flow above a plate

At \(x=0.500\) m, a measured polynomial layer has \(\delta=6.00\) mm and \(U=4.00\) m/s. If it grows locally as \(\sqrt x\), the predicted edge velocity is

\[4.00(0.00600)/(6\times0.500)=0.00800\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

Its ratio to the external tangential speed is only 0.00200. The displacement thickness is 2.00 mm, the height by which a uniform-speed stream would need to be displaced to produce the same missing flux.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Use two profiles to infer the average vertical flux

Independent surveys at \(x=0.200\) and 0.600 m find displacement thicknesses 1.20 and 2.00 mm. At \(U=3.00\) m/s the interval-average normal velocity is

\[3.00(0.000800)/0.400=0.00600\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

Over a plate span of 0.500 m this corresponds to outward volume flow \(0.00600(0.400)(0.500)=1.20\) L/s through the fixed upper face, equal to the growth of the streamwise flux deficit.

Start from this chapter. Balance streamwise advection against diffusion normal to the plate. Predict the thickness scaling, then measure both the 99% thickness and wall-friction coefficient at one specified station.

OneSimulation laboratory · Measure boundary-layer growth from a velocity profile

Viscosity matters where velocity changes rapidly

Flow past a smooth plate with a large length-based Reynolds number often has nearly uniform speed outside a thin layer next to the wall. No slip requires zero velocity at the stationary surface. Inside the thin region, transverse velocity gradients can be large enough for viscosity to balance streamwise inertia. If the free-stream speed is \(U\), the streamwise distance is \(x\) and the layer thickness is \(\delta\), the leading inertial and viscous scales are \(U^2/x\) and \(\nu U/\delta^2\). Their balance gives \(\delta/x\sim1/\sqrt{\mathrm{Re}_x}\), where \(\mathrm{Re}_x=Ux/\nu\).

This scaling predicts how a laminar layer grows. Its numerical factor depends on how the thickness is defined. A common measured edge is the position reaching 99% of the external speed. Two integral measures instead quantify flow displaced by the layer and its lost momentum, and are less sensitive to picking a particular edge point.

\[\delta^*=\int_0^\infty(1-u/U)\,dy,\]
\[\theta_m=\int_0^\infty(u/U)(1-u/U)\,dy .\]

The thickness \(\delta^*\) gives the equivalent missing volume flux per unit width divided by \(U\). Momentum thickness \(\theta_m\) weights that deficit by the actual local speed. Fluid right at the wall contributes strongly to displacement thickness but carries almost no streamwise momentum flux.

Relate the momentum deficit to wall shear

A steady control volume surrounding one face of a flat plate at zero incidence receives fluid from the uniform upstream stream. For constant external speed and pressure, the integrated wall force equals the deficit of streamwise momentum flux downstream after accounting for entrained fluid. Per unit plate width this gives

\[D'(x)=\rho U^2\theta_m(x).\]

Differentiating gives local wall shear \(\tau_w=\rho U^2\,d\theta_m/dx\). A growing deficit implies positive drag on the plate.

Suppose a velocity survey is represented by \(u/U=2\eta-\eta^2\) for \(0\le\eta=y/\delta\le1\), and uniform flow outside. Integrating yields

\[\delta^*/\delta=\int_0^1(1-\eta)^2d\eta=1/3.\]

Since \(\int_0^1(2\eta-\eta^2)d\eta=2/3\) and \(\int_0^1(2\eta-\eta^2)^2d\eta=8/15\), the momentum thickness is \(2\delta/15\).

Infer drag from a measured layer

At the end of a 0.60 m plate of width 0.40 m in air, let \(U=12\,\mathrm{m/s}\), \(\rho=1.20\,\mathrm{kg/m^3}\) and \(\delta=5.0\,\mathrm{mm}\) according to the fitted profile. Then \(\theta_m=0.667\,\mathrm{mm}\) and drag on the measured face is

\[1.2(12)^2(0.000667)(0.40)=0.0461\,\mathrm N.\]

The mean shear over that face is

\[0.0461/(0.60\times0.40)=0.192\,\mathrm{Pa}.\]

It need not equal the local shear at the measuring station. An adverse pressure gradient or separated flow changes the momentum balance; surface pressure forces and external acceleration must then be included before interpreting a wake or boundary-layer survey.

Predict a boundary-layer thickness before viewing a simulation

At the same measuring station the laminar scaling from the first section gives \(\delta\propto\sqrt{\nu/U}\). Starting from a measured 35.84 mm layer at \(x=0.50\) m, predict its thickness after separately doubling free-stream speed and doubling kinematic viscosity. Which change should raise the dimensional wall stress if density and viscosity are held fixed while speed increases? Compare your thickness ratios with the two computed runs and distinguish the measured skin-friction coefficient from wall stress in pascals. Compare the flat-plate runs.

Reduce a partial differential equation to one similarity profile

For a laminar zero-pressure-gradient boundary layer along a flat plate, the leading equations are \(u_x+v_y=0\) and \(u u_x+v u_y=\nu u_{yy}\). Assume external speed \(U\) constant, a stationary no-slip surface and a layer thin relative to streamwise distance. Introduce a streamfunction \(\psi\) through \(u=\psi_y,\ v=-\psi_x\) to satisfy continuity identically. The scaling \(\delta\sim\sqrt{\nu x/U}\) suggests \(\eta=y\sqrt{U/(\nu x)}\) and \(\psi=\sqrt{\nu Ux}\,f(\eta)\). Differentiation gives \(u=U f'\) and \(v=\tfrac12\sqrt{\nu U/x}(\eta f'-f)\).

When these forms enter the momentum equation, the terms containing \(\eta f'f''\) from longitudinal and transverse advection cancel. Both the remaining inertial and viscous terms carry \(U^2/x\). Dividing through gives the Blasius boundary-value problem

\[f'''+\tfrac12 f f''=0,\]
\[f(0)=f'(0)=0,\]
\[f'(\infty)=1 .\]

The two wall conditions impose zero normal and tangential velocity. The outer condition matches the uniform stream. The unknown wall slope of the velocity profile, \(f''(0)\), is determined by matching that distant condition; prescribing it independently would overdetermine the third-order equation. Numerical integration gives approximately 0.3321 for this definition of \(\eta\). Conventions that include a factor of two in the similarity coordinate assign a different numerical constant but the same physical wall stress.

Predict a force measurement from the similarity solution

The wall shear is \(\mu U f''(0)\sqrt{U/(\nu x)}\), giving \(c_f(x)=\tau_w/(\rho U^2/2)=0.664/\sqrt{\mathrm{Re}_x}\). Its integral over a plate of length \(L\) yields mean coefficient \(\bar c_f=1.328/\sqrt{\mathrm{Re}_L}\). The mean equals twice the local trailing-edge value because the integrable \(x^{-1/2}\) shear is highest near the leading edge. Using a single trailing-edge wall gauge for the whole area would therefore underestimate the force.

Take a smooth plate 0.50 m long and 0.25 m wide in air at \(U=6.0\,\mathrm{m/s},\ \nu=1.5\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^2/s},\ \rho=1.20\,\mathrm{kg/m^3}\). The trailing-edge Reynolds number is 200,000, and a nominally laminar calculation gives

\[c_f(L)=0.00149,\ \bar c_f=0.00297.\]

The one-face drag is

\[0.5(1.20)(6)^2(0.50)(0.25)(0.00297)=0.00802\,\mathrm N.\]

The trailing-edge wall shear is just 0.0321 Pa, and the 99% thickness estimate \(5L/\sqrt{\mathrm{Re}_L}\) is 5.59 mm. A support carrying both plate faces would register roughly twice the one-face drag if both flows are comparable, plus fixture and edge drag. A single net force measurement cannot by itself tell whether a discrepancy arose from transition, unequal face conditions or the support geometry.

The similarity solution also predicts how to collapse profiles measured at different stations. At \(x=0.125\) m the same value of \(\eta\) corresponds to half the wall-normal distance it does at 0.50 m, since the distance scales with \(\sqrt{x}\). Meanwhile wall shear doubles and local skin-friction coefficient doubles. A traverse resolving only whole millimetres may describe the outer velocity defect at both positions but badly estimate the near-wall slope at the earlier one. Testing both the profile collapse and the independent integral drag provides a stronger check of the assumed laminar flow than comparing a single velocity at the outer edge.

Measure the momentum transported by turbulent fluctuations

In a smooth plane channel driven by a constant pressure drop, time averaging can hide the dominant transport mechanism. Consider steady statistics and a fully developed mean flow between parallel walls. Let \(y=0\) denote the lower wall and \(h\) the distance to the centreline. Write \(u=U(y)+u'\), \(v=v'\). Impermeability and incompressibility give zero mean transverse velocity. The average of the instantaneous flux of streamwise momentum across a horizontal surface is nevertheless \(\rho\overline{uv}=\rho\overline{u'v'}\). Averaging the product retains the correlation between the two fluctuations.

For constant density and viscosity the mean streamwise balance becomes

\[0=-d\bar p/dx+d(\mu\,dU/dy-\rho\overline{u'v'})/dy.\]

Set \(G=-d\bar p/dx>0\). Integrating from \(y\) to the centreline, where symmetry sets the total shear to zero, gives

\[\tau_{\rm total}(y)=\mu\,\frac{dU}{dy}-\rho\overline{u'v'}=G(h-y),\]
\[\tau_w=Gh.\]

The right side is fixed by the driving pressure gradient. At a no-slip wall both fluctuating velocities vanish, so molecular viscosity carries the full shear there. Farther out a parcel moving rapidly downstream can move towards the lower wall (\(u'>0,\ v'<0\)). A slow parcel leaving that wall has the same negative product. Both transfer streamwise momentum towards the wall. With the signs above the turbulent contribution to the shear is positive.

For water with \(\rho=1000\) kg/m³ and \(\mu=0.0010\) Pa s, take \(h=0.020\) m and \(G=100\) Pa/m. The wall stress is 2.00 Pa. At \(y=0.0050\) m the total shear is 1.50 Pa. A measured slope of \(150\) s⁻¹ accounts for 0.150 Pa. The remaining 1.35 Pa implies \(\overline{u'v'}=-1.35\times10^{-3}\) m²/s². The exact force balance gives a quantitative consistency check for velocity measurements or a simulation. Predicting the split from \(G\) alone would require a relation for the velocity correlations.

Practise on sheet 4 · Test a turbulent channel measurement. Further reading · University of Iowa · ME:7268 Turbulent Flow · Chapter 8, part 1 · Channel flow, Reynolds stress and force balance.

Momentum thickness is \(\theta=\int(u/U)(1-u/U)\,dy\). Use three idealised trial profiles at the same outer thickness. Integrating the ordinate with respect to the plotted dimensionless height gives \(\theta/\delta\).

Estimate wall drag by integrating a boundary-layer profile · Momentum, motion and transport in fluids

A velocity probe can resolve the momentum missing from a boundary layer more easily than the wall shear itself. Derive an integral momentum relation, estimate drag using a polynomial profile and determine when a pressure gradient or a measurement too far from the wall changes the answer.

Problem sheet 4 · Swirling flows and wall drag

Use a velocity distribution to infer local vorticity, pressure differences and the momentum lost to wall drag.

A rigidly spinning tank

Water in an open cylindrical tank of radius 0.15 m rotates as a solid body at 6 rad/s. Determine the circulation around the rim, local vorticity and wall-to-centre free-surface rise. Compare the vorticity with that of an ideal free-vortex exterior carrying the same circulation.

Reveal the worked solution

The rim speed is \(6(0.15)=0.90\,\mathrm{m/s}\) and circulation is

\[2\pi(0.15)(0.90)=0.848\,\mathrm{m^2/s}.\]

The vorticity is \(2\Omega=12\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\). Integrating the radial and vertical pressure balance gives

\[\Delta z=6^2(0.15)^2/(2g)=0.0413\,\mathrm m.\]

A free vortex of the same circulation has zero local vorticity outside its core. The circulation measures what the loop encloses.

A linear layer profile

An idealised velocity survey gives \(u/U=y/\delta\) to the layer edge and unity above. Derive displacement and momentum thicknesses. For \(U=8\,\mathrm{m/s}\), water density 1000 kg/m³, \(\delta=2\,\mathrm{mm}\) and plate width 0.15 m, estimate drag on the surveyed face to this station.

Reveal the worked solution

Integrating \(1-\eta\) gives

\[\delta^*=\delta/2=1.0\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Integrating \(\eta(1-\eta)\) gives

\[\theta_m=\delta/6=0.333\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Under constant external pressure and speed,

\[D=\rho U^2\theta_m b=3.20\,\mathrm N.\]

Only the one face to which the survey applies has been counted.

Infer a local shear stress

A measured zero-pressure-gradient momentum thickness follows \(\theta_m=0.0010\sqrt{x/(1\,\mathrm m)}\,\mathrm m\). Air density is 1.2 kg/m³ and external speed 15 m/s. At \(x=0.25\,\mathrm m\), find local wall shear and integrated drag per unit width. Why do these two quantities have different units?

Reveal the worked solution

At the station \(\theta_m=0.00050\,\mathrm m\) and \(d\theta_m/dx=0.0010\). Local shear is

\[\rho U^2d\theta_m/dx=0.270\,\mathrm{Pa}.\]

Drag per width is

\[\rho U^2\theta_m=0.135\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

Local shear is force per area; integrating along plate length removes one inverse length.

Test a turbulent channel measurement

A water channel has half-height 15 mm and pressure gradient \(-80\) Pa/m. At a probe 5 mm from the lower wall, measurements give \(dU/dy=100\) s⁻¹ and \(\overline{u'v'}=-5.0\times10^{-4}\) m²/s². Use \(\rho=1000\) kg/m³ and \(\mu=0.0010\) Pa s. How well do the measurements close the momentum balance? What correlation would close it at the stated position?

Worked solution

The expected total stress is

\[80(0.015-0.005)=0.80\,\mathrm{Pa}.\]

The gradient gives 0.10 Pa and the measured covariance gives another 0.50 Pa. Together these account for 0.60 Pa. The shortfall is 0.20 Pa, or 25% of the pressure-based stress at the probe. The wall stress of 1.20 Pa is a different comparison value.

Holding the gradient fixed gives \(\overline{u'v'}=-(0.80-0.10)/1000=-7.0\times10^{-4}\) m²/s². Before changing a turbulence model, check the probe height, the averaging interval and whether the flow is fully developed. Changing the sign convention for just one term cannot resolve the shortfall.

Revisit chapter 8 · Measure the momentum transported by turbulent fluctuations.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Vorticity, circulation and swirling flow; Boundary layers and momentum thickness. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Infer viscosity from the edge of a boundary layer

Using the baseline 99% thickness 35.84 mm at \(x=0.50\) m and speed 1 m/s, calibrate the coefficient of \(\delta=C\sqrt{\nu x/U}\) at \(\nu=10^{-4}\) m²/s. At the same station and speed a different run gives 51.03 mm. Infer its kinematic viscosity without measuring a wall gradient. Compare it with the input and identify one reason for a discrepancy.

Work through the solution

The baseline gives

\[C=0.03584/\sqrt{10^{-4}(0.50)}=5.07.\]

At common \(x,U\), the coefficient cancels in the ratio, so \(\nu_2=10^{-4}(51.03/35.84)^2=2.03\times10^{-4}\) m²/s. The laboratory input was \(2.00\times10^{-4}\) m²/s. The similarity argument describes an ideal laminar layer. Estimating its edge from a discretised velocity profile also depends on interpolation at 99% of the free-stream velocity.

Chapter 9 · Pressure integration, lift and drag

Resolve pressure and shear into integrated forces and infer drag independently from a far-wake velocity survey.

From first principles

Resolve measured surface tractions into lift and drag

Choose a closed surface on a body and a unit normal \(\mathbf n\) pointing from solid into fluid. Pressure acts inward on the body. Viscous stress acts along or normal to the surface depending on the flow. For fluid stress tensor \(-pI+\boldsymbol\tau\), the total fluid force on the body is the sum of all local tractions times surface area.

\[\mathbf F=\int_A(-p\mathbf n+\boldsymbol\tau\mathbf n)\,dA,\]
\[D=\mathbf F\cdot\mathbf e_D,\]
\[L=\mathbf F\cdot\mathbf e_L .\]

Here \(\mathbf e_D\) points along the incident stream and \(\mathbf e_L\) is the chosen transverse lift direction. A constant pressure reference can be subtracted because the normal integrates to zero over a closed surface. For a thin wing strip at small slope, upper and lower pressure contributions to lift reduce to their difference integrated over projected area.

\[C_L=\frac L{q_\infty S}\simeq \frac1c\int_0^c(C_{p,l}-C_{p,u})\,dx,\]
\[C_p=\frac{p-p_\infty}{q_\infty},\]
\[q_\infty=\tfrac12\rho U_\infty^2 .\]

The last expression assumes uniform spanwise loading and negligible viscous contribution to lift. Estimating drag can require the small axial components of pressure and the wall shear that this lift approximation omits.

Derivation and applications

Determine the aerodynamic centre of pressure from surface data

A net lift measurement does not say where the load acts. Consider a thin two-dimensional panel of chord \(c\) and span \(b\) in steady flow of dynamic pressure \(q\). Let \(\xi=x/c\) increase from the leading edge and \(\Delta C_p(\xi)\) be lower minus upper pressure coefficient. Assume small surface slopes and neglect shear and end effects so that each strip exerts a vertical force. Define moment as positive for an upward load acting aft of the leading edge. The integrated force and moment are

\[L=qbc\int_0^1\Delta C_p\,d\xi,\]
\[M_{\rm LE}=qbc^2\int_0^1\xi\Delta C_p\,d\xi .\]

The centre of pressure \(x_{\rm cp}\) replaces the distributed forces by one force with the same moment. When the total force is nonzero, dividing the two integrals eliminates dynamic pressure and span.

\[\frac{x_{\rm cp}}c=\frac{M_{\rm LE}}{cL}=\frac{\int_0^1\xi\Delta C_p\,d\xi}{\int_0^1\Delta C_p\,d\xi}.\]

For a linear distribution \(\Delta C_p=A+B\xi\), perform both elementary integrations. A constant increment to the pressure distribution acts at mid-chord and can move the centre even as the original pressure gradient stays unchanged.

\[C_L=A+B/2,\]
\[\frac{M_{\rm LE}}{qbc^2}=\frac A2+\frac B3,\]
\[\frac{x_{\rm cp}}c=\frac{A/2+B/3}{A+B/2}.\]

Near zero net lift the position ratio can become very large. In that situation a force and a moment about a specified structural axis remain meaningful even when replacing them by one force is inconvenient.

Worked example

Place the resultant from a triangular pressure trace

Let \(A=1.20\), \(B=-1.20\), \(c=0.400\) m, \(b=1.00\) m and \(q=200\) Pa. Lift is \(200(1)(0.400)(0.600)=48.0\) N. Its moment is \(200(1)(0.400)^2(0.200)=6.40\) N m. Hence \(x_{\rm cp}=6.40/48.0=0.133\) m, exactly one third of the chord from the high-pressure-difference end.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Recognise a pure couple in a loading test

A second panel has \(A=1.00\), \(B=-2.00\), \(q=300\) Pa, \(b=0.800\) m and \(c=0.500\) m. Positive and negative contributions cancel in the lift integral, giving zero total lift. The moment coefficient is \(1/2-2/3=-1/6\) and the moment is \(-10.0\) N m. A force transducer alone would miss this load; two separated vertical reactions can balance the measured couple.

Start from this chapter. The local wall shear is proportional to dynamic pressure times the local friction coefficient. Test how doubling speed can increase shear even while the dimensionless coefficient falls.

OneSimulation laboratory · Measure boundary-layer growth from a velocity profile

Build the resultant from surface measurements

A wind tunnel balance reports force components along and across the incoming flow. A pressure tap reports only local pressure. To connect the two, draw the surface outward normal from the solid into the fluid. A pressure load then acts on the solid in direction \(-n\); fluid wall shear acts tangentially. Integrate pressure traction and shear traction over the body and project the result onto drag and lift axes. A uniform ambient pressure integrates to zero on a closed surface, so gauge pressure is sufficient for this calculation.

For a thin wing at small incidence the upper and lower surfaces are almost horizontal. To leading order the upward pressure force is the planform integral of \(p_{\rm lower}-p_{\rm upper}\). With dynamic pressure \(q_\infty=\rho U^2/2\), define \(C_p=(p-p_\infty)/q_\infty\). For rectangular planform and a pressure difference independent of span, the lift coefficient is approximately

\[C_L=\frac{L}{q_\infty S}\simeq \int_0^1(C_{p,l}-C_{p,u})\,d(x/c).\]

The reference area \(S\) must be stated. A coefficient based on wing planform and a bluff-body coefficient based on frontal area cannot be compared as if they used the same force scale.

Integrate a pressure distribution

A wing panel has chord 0.30 m and span 0.80 m. Take the pressure-coefficient difference as a triangle, rising linearly from zero at the leading edge to 1.20 at \(x/c=0.25\) and decreasing linearly to zero at the trailing edge. The coefficient integral is the area under this triangle, \(C_L=0.60\). At \(U=20\,\mathrm{m/s}\) in air of density 1.20 kg/m³, \(q_\infty=240\,\mathrm{Pa}\). The estimated lift is \(240(0.24)(0.60)=34.56\,\mathrm N\).

For this spanwise-uniform distribution the centre of pressure follows from the first moment of the triangle. Its chord fraction is the mean of the three vertex abscissae, \((0+0.25+1)/3=0.4167\). The resultant acts about 0.125 m aft of the leading edge. The moment magnitude about that edge is \(34.56(0.125)=4.32\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). Two panels with the same total lift can therefore transmit different support moments if their pressure distributions differ.

Recover a drag force without surface taps

For a survey far enough downstream that static pressure has returned to the external value, integrate the streamwise momentum deficit across the wake. With essentially parallel flow, constant density and negligible outer boundary shear, drag per unit span is \(D'=\rho\int u(U-u)\,dy\). This momentum survey includes the effect of both body pressure and surface shear.

For a simplified wake 40 mm thick with uniform \(u=16\,\mathrm{m/s}\) inside it and \(U=20\,\mathrm{m/s}\) outside, the inferred drag per span is \(1.20(16)(4)(0.040)=3.07\,\mathrm{N/m}\). Replacing the product by \(\rho U(U-u)\) overestimates the deficit because it assigns the undisturbed mass flux to the wake. A survey close to the trailing edge often also needs a static pressure correction; insufficient lateral coverage can miss part of the deficit.

Relate finite-span lift to a second contribution to drag

A pressure integration on one two-dimensional section neglects a feature of every finite lifting surface. Bound circulation changes along the span and trailing vorticity produces downwash behind the wing. For attached lifting-line flow at small angles, downwash tilts the effective incoming stream downward by induced angle \(\alpha_i\). The section’s aerodynamic force is approximately perpendicular to that local stream, so its projection along the original incoming direction creates induced drag \(D_i\simeq L\alpha_i\). This contribution can remain even for an inviscid outer flow because momentum is carried away in a three-dimensional trailing wake.

For elliptical span loading the ideal lifting-line result is

\[\alpha_i=C_L/(\pi AR),\]

where aspect ratio \(AR=b^2/S\), span \(b\) and planform area \(S\). A span efficiency factor \(e\) accounts approximately for a non-elliptic distribution at a given lift, giving \(C_{D,i}=C_L^2/(\pi e AR)\). With a locally constant parasite coefficient \(C_{D,0}\), total drag at fixed required lift \(W\) becomes

\[D=qS C_{D,0}+\frac{W^2}{\pi e AR\,qS},\]
\[q=\rho U^2/2.\]

At low speed the necessary lift coefficient rises and induced drag dominates; at high speed parasite drag rises with dynamic pressure. Differentiating \(D\) with respect to \(qS\) gives minimum drag where the two terms are equal. Thus \(C_{L,\rm opt}=\sqrt{\pi e AR C_{D,0}}\). This optimum maximises lift-to-drag ratio for the adopted polar. Minimum power \(DU\) is a different operating point.

Select a glide speed and check the angle assumptions

A small unpowered survey glider has weight 120 N, area 1.20 m² and span 3.0 m. Use density 1.20 kg/m³, \(e=0.80\) and \(C_{D,0}=0.025\). Assume shallow steady glide so lift is approximately weight. Aspect ratio is

\[3^2/1.2=7.50,\]
\[\pi e AR=18.85.\]

The optimum lift coefficient is

\[\sqrt{18.85(0.025)}=0.686.\]

From \(W=qSC_L\) the dynamic pressure is 145.8 Pa and the airspeed is

\[\sqrt{2(145.8)/1.20}=15.6\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

At this condition total drag coefficient is

\[0.025+0.686^2/18.85=0.0500.\]

Drag is 8.75 N, giving \(L/D=13.7\) and glide angle approximately \(\arctan(1/13.7)=4.17^\circ\). The assumption \(L\simeq W\) is consistent to better than one percent. Doubling speed at unchanged weight reduces \(C_L\) by four, so induced drag falls by four while parasite drag rises by four. The original 4.38 N contributions become about 1.09 and 17.5 N. If an estimated optimum lies at or beyond the measured stall coefficient, the attached-flow polar and lifting-line argument no longer support that speed decision.

Banking the same glider changes the required lift. In a coordinated near-level turn with bank angle \(\phi\), the vertical lift component must support the weight, so \(L=W/\cos\phi\). At 45° bank the required lift rises by \(\sqrt2\). At unchanged speed the induced portion of drag then doubles, while the parasite portion is approximately unchanged under the same polar. This transfer calculation explains why a straight-flight drag estimate cannot be inserted unchanged into a turning-flight performance prediction.

The required lift coefficient at the higher bank angle must still remain inside the measured unstalled polar.

Settling in the creeping-flow limit

A small tracer bead in a viscous liquid can reach a regime in which inertial forces on the surrounding liquid are negligible. Estimate their ratio to viscous forces using \(Re_d=\rho U(2a)/\mu\), with radius \(a\). For \(Re_d\ll1\), the steady incompressible equations outside a sphere reduce to \(\nabla p=\mu\nabla^2\mathbf u\) and \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0\). Apply no slip at the sphere and uniform speed \(U\) far away. Linearity then requires drag proportional to \(U\).

The coefficient comes from surface traction. Fix the sphere in flow directed along positive \(z\), and measure polar angle \(\theta\) from that axis. The Stokes solution gives surface pressure relative to the far field \(-3\mu U\cos\theta/(2a)\) and tangential shear \(-3\mu U\sin\theta/(2a)\). The normal viscous stress vanishes there. Project pressure and shear onto the flow direction and use the area of a ring, \(2\pi a^2\sin\theta\,d\theta\). The pressure integral uses \(\int_0^\pi\cos^2\theta\sin\theta\,d\theta=2/3\), while the shear integral uses \(\int_0^\pi\sin^3\theta\,d\theta=4/3\). The two contributions are \(2\pi\mu aU\) and \(4\pi\mu aU\). Even in this highly viscous regime, pressure accounts for one third of the drag.

A spherical calibration bead settles far from the container walls. At terminal speed its apparent weight equals \(6\pi\mu aU_t\), so

\[U_t=\frac{2(\rho_s-\rho)ga^2}{9\mu}.\]

For \(a=0.200\,\mathrm{mm}\), \(\rho_s=1250\), \(\rho=1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\), and \(\mu=0.100\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}\),

\[U_t=0.218\,\mathrm{mm\,s^{-1}}.\]

The resulting \(Re_d=0.000872\) justifies dropping convective inertia. After terminal speed is reached, 10 mm takes about 45.9 s. Doubling the radius at the same density difference quadruples speed and multiplies \(Re_d\) by eight. A size change therefore merits a fresh check on the approximation.

Practise on sheet 5 · Measure viscosity with a falling calibration bead. Further reading · Georgia Institute of Technology · PHYS 4421 course reading, B. Lautrup, Physics of Continuous Matter · Chapter 20, §20.2 Stokes’ solution for a sphere.

Study the full derivation

Settling spheres, Stokes drag and a wall correction · Momentum, motion and transport in fluids

A falling-sphere viscometer infers viscosity from terminal speed. Derive the drag on a no-slip sphere, distinguish creeping flow from merely steady motion and use radius, vessel width and orientation checks to decide what the speed experiment actually measures.

Chapter 10 · Pump curves, pipe networks and operating points

Construct a hydraulic system curve and solve the operating point of a pump connected to serial or parallel flow paths.

From first principles

Find pump discharge by matching added and required head

A pump supplies head that depends on its discharge and speed. The connected pipework requires head to overcome both terminal elevation or pressure differences and losses. Consider steady incompressible flow between large reservoirs with negligible endpoint velocities. For a fixed pump speed, conservation of mechanical energy at the operating point gives

\[H_p(Q)=H_{\rm static}+h_L(Q),\]
\[H_{\rm static}=z_2-z_1+(p_2-p_1)/(\rho g).\]

For fittings and pipe sections with approximately constant loss coefficients, each loss varies as \(V^2/(2g)\). In a single series path all sections carry the same \(Q\), with \(V_j=Q/A_j\). Adding the individual losses gives a system coefficient \(K\).

\[h_L=\sum_j\frac{\zeta_j}{2gA_j^2}Q^2=KQ^2 .\]

If the measured pump characteristic is approximated by \(H_p=H_0-aQ^2\), solve the intersection explicitly on the positive-flow branch.

\[Q=\sqrt{\frac{H_0-H_{\rm static}}{a+K}} .\]

This solution needs \(H_0>H_{\rm static}\). Branches in parallel share an endpoint head difference and their discharges add; those constraints must be imposed before using an equivalent system curve.

Derivation and applications

Find the sensitivity of a pump operating point to a valve

A pump curve and a system curve determine their intersection. Their local slopes also determine how far that intersection moves when a valve setting changes. Model a fixed-speed pump by \(H_p=H_0-aQ^2\) and a network by \(H_s=H_z+bQ^2\) in the intended flow direction. Here \(H_z\) is a static elevation or pressure head, \(a,b>0\) have units of head per squared discharge, and \(H_0>H_z\). The loss model assumes an approximately constant turbulent loss coefficient in the examined flow range.

\[H_0-aQ^2=H_z+bQ^2,\]
\[Q=\sqrt{\frac{H_0-H_z}{a+b}} .\]

Differentiate implicitly with respect to the network coefficient at fixed speed and static head. A larger loss coefficient raises the system curve and reduces discharge. The fractional sensitivity is weaker when the pump itself has a steep characteristic.

\[\frac{dQ}{db}=-\frac{Q}{2(a+b)},\]
\[\frac{d\ln Q}{d\ln b}=-\frac{b}{2(a+b)} .\]

The actual head delivered by the pump changes as the flow moves along its curve. Differentiating the pump head with the result just obtained gives a positive slope with respect to \(b\), an effect distinguishable by measuring both flow and pressure.

\[\frac{dH_p}{db}=-2aQ\frac{dQ}{db}=\frac{aQ^2}{a+b}.\]

These derivatives apply to small changes. Near zero available head a fractional uncertainty in the difference \(H_0-H_z\) can dominate, even if the separate head measurements are quite accurate.

Worked example

Predict the effect of throttling a circulation line

Measure \(Q\) in L/s and head in metres. Take \(H_0=20.0\), \(H_z=4.00\), \(a=0.250\) and \(b=0.750\) in consistent units. The flow is 4.00 L/s and pump head is 16.0 m. A ten-percent increase in \(b\) predicts \(-3.75\) percent flow by the derivative, or 3.85 L/s. The exact new flow is \(\sqrt{16/1.075}=3.86\) L/s, close to the linear estimate.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Infer which curve limits adjustability

At a separate operating point the measured logarithmic flow sensitivity to the total network coefficient is \(-0.100\). This implies \(b/(a+b)=0.200\) and therefore \(a=4b\). A reduction of \(b\) by 20.0 percent at fixed available head changes flow by the exact factor \(\sqrt{5/4.8}=1.0206\). The small 2.06 percent increase quantifies why a noticeable valve change can have little effect on discharge.

Open this live calculation

Why a pump does not prescribe a unique flow

A centrifugal pump at fixed speed can deliver different combinations of flow and head. The connected network selects an operating point by requiring the head gained through the machine to match the static lift and losses elsewhere. For two large reservoirs, surface speeds are negligible and equal surface pressures cancel. A steady mechanical energy balance then gives

\[H_{\rm pump}=\Delta z+h_L.\]

With turbulent pipe resistance represented locally by constant loss coefficients, the losses can be written \(h_L=KQ^2\) for positive flow.

This quadratic law is an approximation over a specified operating range. It follows because fittings and pipe friction have head losses proportional to velocity squared when the relevant coefficients vary little. In laminar pipe flow, the pressure loss at fixed viscosity is instead proportional to flow. A system curve extrapolated far outside its underlying Reynolds-number range can give a misleading operating point.

OneSimulation · When does reducing pump speed close the check valve?

An ideal check valve connects a pump to an open reservoir above its inlet. Let \(n\) denote the speed divided by its reference value and let \(Q\) be measured in litres per second. The pump supplies \(20n^2-0.025Q^2\) metres of head. The pipe and reservoir require \(H_s+0.075Q^2\) metres. Forward flow requires a positive intersection.

\[0.100Q^2=20n^2-H_s.\]

OneSimulation inverts the total dynamic loss over the forward-flow interval. With a 5 m static lift, half speed just balances gravity at zero delivery. At reference speed the pump delivers 12.25 L/s. At three-quarter speed it delivers 7.91 L/s, considerably below three quarters of the reference delivery. Beneath the threshold the closed valve carries the pressure difference; the forward-flow equation no longer applies.

"""Intersect a pump curve with a pipe-system head curve at two lifts."""
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.numerics.nonlinear.kernels.local_solvers.inversion.bracketed_inverse import bisect_increasing

speed = np.linspace(0.25, 1.2, 181)  # fraction of reference speed
shutoff = 20. * speed**2             # pump shutoff head in m
pump_loss, pipe_loss = 0.025, 0.075   # m per (L/s)²
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 4), constrained_layout=True)
for lift, colour in [(0., "#19596a"), (5., "#a5442e")]:
    available = shutoff - lift
    delivery = np.zeros_like(speed)
    active = available > 0
    def total_loss(flow):
        return (pump_loss + pipe_loss) * flow**2
    delivery[active] = bisect_increasing(total_loss, available[active], 0., 30.)
    assert np.allclose(shutoff[active] - pump_loss*delivery[active]**2,
                       lift + pipe_loss*delivery[active]**2)
    ax.plot(speed, delivery, color=colour, label=f"Static lift {lift:g} m")

ax.axvline(0.5, color="0.45", linestyle=":", label="5 m lift threshold")
ax.set(xlabel="Pump speed / reference speed", ylabel="Forward delivery (L/s)")
ax.legend()
fig.savefig("fluid-mechanics.png", dpi=170)

Change the inputs of this exact example and compare the recalculated curves with the recorded OneSimulation output above. The browser evaluates the stated analytical or modal reference.

Combine resistances by their physical constraints

In series the same flow passes through each component and head losses add. Thus two quadratic loss coefficients combine as \(K_s=K_1+K_2\). Parallel branches between the same nodes have equal head loss while their flow rates add. For two branches with forward flow, \(Q_i=\sqrt{h/K_i}\), giving

\[Q=\sqrt h\,(K_1^{-1/2}+K_2^{-1/2}),\]
\[K_{\parallel}=(K_1^{-1/2}+K_2^{-1/2})^{-2}.\]

A second identical branch divides the flow equally and reduces the quadratic coefficient of that section to one quarter of the single-branch value. Any upstream or downstream common pipe is still in series with the equivalent section and carries total flow.

Open a parallel return line

Use \(Q\) in litres per second and head in metres. A pump has \(H_p=30-0.20Q^2\); two open reservoirs differ in elevation by 6 m. Initially one pipe connects them with loss \(0.40Q^2\). Equating the heads gives \(30-0.20Q^2=6+0.40Q^2\), so \(Q=\sqrt{40}=6.32\,\mathrm{L/s}\) and \(H_p=22.0\,\mathrm m\). Hydraulic power delivered to water is

\[\rho gQ H_p=1000(9.81)(0.00632)(22)=1.36\,\mathrm{kW}.\]

Now add a second identical parallel pipe over the whole route. The system curve becomes \(6+0.10Q^2\). The new flow is \(\sqrt{80}=8.94\,\mathrm{L/s}\) and pump head 14.0 m. Each pipe takes only 4.47 L/s, less than the original single-pipe flow. Total delivery increases by about 41% while hydraulic power falls to 1.23 kW. The input shaft power also depends on the efficiency at each operating point. If a flow controller holds the original delivery after the second branch opens, its valve or speed controller changes the operating curve and the free-running comparison no longer predicts the duty.

Determine which side of a branch controls the flow

A check valve on a return line permits only positive branch flow. If an added elevated reservoir imposes a static threshold of 5 m on that branch, the forward branch law becomes \(Q_b=\sqrt{(H-5)/K_b}\) only for driving head \(H>5\) m. Below that threshold the flow is zero. The resulting system curve changes form as the valve opens, so a single constant equivalent coefficient cannot describe both regimes.

Derive the first pressure change in a rapidly closed line

The operating point of a pump and pipe network is a steady state. A valve closure can generate a much higher transient pressure while the flow far upstream has barely changed. Consider a straight horizontal liquid-filled pipe of area \(A\) and initially uniform forward speed \(V\). A rapid downstream closure sends a compression wave upstream at wave speed \(a\). During a short interval \(dt\), the wave reaches approximately \(a\,dt\) of pipe, bringing the fluid behind it to rest. Neglect friction over this short wavefront and take \(a\gg V\).

The stopped mass is \(\rho A a\,dt\), losing momentum \(\rho A a\,dt\,V\). The pressure difference across the front supplies an impulse \(A\Delta p\,dt\). Balancing magnitudes yields the Joukowsky rise \(\Delta p=\rho a V\); for a partial rapid speed change use its magnitude \(\rho a|\Delta V|\) with sign determined by whether the wave compresses or relieves the pipe. Small fluid compressibility and wall compliance both enter the actual wave speed. In a rigid tube \(a\simeq\sqrt{K/\rho}\), but an expanding elastic pipe stores some of the volume change and lowers that speed.

Reflections matter after the wave has travelled to a boundary and back. For a reservoir a distance \(L\) away the first round-trip time is approximately \(2L/a\). A valve that completes closure before the returning wave acts can produce the full first rise. A much slower closure allows incoming reflections to alter the pressure during the manoeuvre and requires a time-dependent network calculation.

Compare closure schedules against a pressure allowance

A water line is 300 m long with measured wave speed \(a=1000\,\mathrm{m/s}\), initial speed 1.5 m/s, density 1000 kg/m³ and initial valve gauge pressure 0.40 MPa. Rapid full closure predicts \(\Delta p=1000(1000)(1.5)=1.50\,\mathrm{MPa}\), bringing the first local peak to about 1.90 MPa gauge. A valve closing in 0.15 s completes its action well inside the 0.60 s round-trip interval. A static energy estimate based only on \(\rho V^2/2=1.13\,\mathrm{kPa}\) would miss the pipe-length momentum transient by orders of magnitude.

Suppose an operating specification allows a first-wave rise of 0.45 MPa. The maximum abrupt decrement in line speed consistent with it is

\[450000/[1000(1000)]=0.45\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

This provides a useful bound on a single rapid speed change. Dividing the full stop into several identical steps does not automatically satisfy the total pressure limit: reflected waves can add if the steps are poorly timed. A proposed 3 s controlled closure is slow compared with \(2L/a\), so the full instantaneous formula no longer estimates its actual peak. Model the valve characteristic, boundary reflections, pump coast-down and possible sub-atmospheric pressure before choosing a schedule. Measuring pressure at the valve and a second station allows wave speed to be identified from the arrival-time difference.

Calculate a pipe resistance from roughness and flow rate

For fully developed turbulent flow in a round pipe the pressure gradient depends on both the roughness height and the viscous scale of the near-wall flow. Force balance on a length \(L\) gives

\[\Delta p\,\pi D^2/4=\tau_w\pi DL.\]

Define the Darcy factor by \(f_D=8\tau_w/(\rho U^2)\), where \(U\) is the cross-sectional mean velocity. Combining the equations gives

\[h_f=f_D(L/D)U^2/(2g).\]

Use the same Darcy convention throughout. The Fanning factor is \(f_D/4\).

For a straight, uniform, turbulent pipe the empirical Colebrook equation relates \(f_D\) to \(Re=UD/\nu\) and equivalent sand roughness \(\epsilon/D\). Setting \(x=1/\sqrt{f_D}\) turns the implicit equation into a simple iteration:

\[x_{n+1}=-2\log_{10}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{3.7D}+\frac{2.51x_n}{Re}\right).\]

When roughness dominates the second term, the friction factor becomes nearly independent of Reynolds number. The smooth limit retains a Reynolds-number dependence. For fully developed laminar flow the separate law \(f_D=64/Re\) applies. Near transition a roughness estimate and a single turbulent curve alone cannot resolve the flow regime.

A building cooling loop carries \(Q=4.00\,\mathrm{L\,s^{-1}}\) of water through \(L=40.0\,\mathrm m\) of straight pipe with \(D=50.0\,\mathrm{mm}\), \(\nu=1.00\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}\) and \(\epsilon=0.050\,\mathrm{mm}\). Continuity gives \(U=4Q/(\pi D^2)=2.037\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\) and \(Re=1.019\times10^5\). Starting at \(x_0=7\), iteration converges to \(x=6.721\), hence \(f_D=0.02214\) and \(h_f=3.75\,\mathrm m\). If deposits raise equivalent roughness to \(0.200\,\mathrm{mm}\) at unchanged bore and flow, the same calculation gives \(f_D=0.02948\) and \(h_f=4.99\,\mathrm m\). Thus the pump needs 1.24 m additional head for this pipe segment alone. At fixed pump speed the operating flow would generally change. That requires solving the pump and system curves together.

Practise on sheet 5 · Infer an equivalent roughness from two pressure taps. Further reading · Purdue University · ME 30900 Introduction to Fluid Mechanics · Pipe Flows – Pipe Systems, “Colebrook Formula” and the Moody plot.

Problem sheet 5 · Surface forces and system curves

Determine loads from a pressure or wake survey and find the operating point after a network change.

Move the point of application

A rectangular 0.40 m chord, 1.0 m span panel has pressure-coefficient difference falling linearly from 1.4 at the leading edge to zero at the trailing edge. The dynamic pressure is 300 Pa. Estimate lift, centre of pressure and moment magnitude about the quarter-chord position.

Reveal the worked solution

The average coefficient difference is 0.70, so

\[L=300(0.4)(0.70)=84\,\mathrm N.\]

The triangular load acts one third of the chord from the larger end, at 0.1333 m. The quarter-chord is 0.100 m from the leading edge, giving moment magnitude \(84(0.0333)=2.80\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). This is a leading-order thin-panel calculation.

Integrate a two-zone survey

Behind a long cylinder in air at density 1.25 kg/m³, a far-wake survey finds velocity 7 m/s in a central strip 20 mm wide and 9 m/s in two neighbouring strips each 15 mm wide. The undisturbed speed is 10 m/s. Estimate drag per span and the drag coefficient based on diameter 25 mm.

Reveal the worked solution

The pressure-recovered momentum deficit is

\[D'=1.25[7(10-7)0.020+9(10-9)0.030]=0.8625\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

The reference force per width is \(0.5(1.25)(10)^2(0.025)=1.5625\,\mathrm{N/m}\), giving \(C_D=0.552\). Counting only the central strip misses a substantial share of the deficit.

Open unequal branches

A pump delivers head \(24-0.25Q^2\) metres with Q in litres per second. Static lift is 4 m. Parallel delivery branches have losses \(1.0Q_1^2\) and \(4.0Q_2^2\) metres. Find equivalent resistance, total delivery and flow split. Verify equal branch losses.

Reveal the worked solution

The equivalent coefficient is

\[(1+1/2)^{-2}=4/9.\]

The head balance gives \(Q^2=20/(0.25+4/9)=28.8\), hence \(Q=5.367\,\mathrm{L/s}\). Equal branch losses imply \(Q_1=2Q_2\), giving 3.578 and 1.789 L/s. Either branch loses 12.8 m, so the pump supplies 16.8 m including static lift.

Infer an equivalent roughness from two pressure taps

A level, constant-bore pipe has \(D=60.0\,\mathrm{mm}\). At mean speed \(2.00\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\), a 60.0 m fully developed length loses 5.00 m of water head. Take \(\nu=1.00\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}\). Determine the Darcy factor and infer the equivalent roughness. Explain how an unnoticed valve loss between the taps would affect the inferred number.

Show the worked solution
\[f_D=\frac{2g h_fD}{LU^2}=0.024525,\]
\[Re=1.20\times10^5.\]

Exponentiate the Colebrook relation to obtain

\[\epsilon=3.7D[10^{-1/(2\sqrt{f_D})}-2.51/(Re\sqrt{f_D})].\]

With the measured factor this gives

\[1.128\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm m=0.113\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The hidden valve would raise the measured loss at this flow, raising both the assigned pipe factor and its inferred roughness. The result would then describe the installation at one operating point.

Revisit chapter 10 · Calculate a pipe resistance from roughness and flow rate.

Measure viscosity with a falling calibration bead

A spherical bead of radius 0.300 mm and density \(1500\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\) falls 12.0 mm in 20.0 s after its speed settles. The liquid density is \(1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\). Walls are distant. Infer dynamic viscosity, check the creeping-flow criterion, and estimate how a 2% overestimate in radius affects the reported viscosity if speed is measured independently.

Show the worked solution

The measured terminal speed is

\[0.0120/20.0=6.00\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}.\]

Solving the buoyancy–drag balance gives

\[\mu=2(500)(9.81)(0.000300)^2/[9(0.000600)]=0.1635\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}.\]

From the inferred viscosity \(Re_d=1000(0.000600)(0.000600)/0.1635=0.00220\), consistent with the assumed limit. Since \(\mu\propto a^2\), the inferred viscosity is higher by \(1.02^2-1=4.04\%\). Timing more precisely does not correct a radius calibration error.

Chapter 11 · Angular momentum and work in a rotor

Use absolute and blade-relative velocities to derive the ideal head developed by a rotating impeller.

From first principles

Obtain rotor work from angular momentum and shaft power

A rotating impeller transmits work by changing the angular momentum carried by fluid. Choose a control volume around the rotor, with axisymmetric inlet and outlet sections. Let \(V_\theta\) be absolute tangential velocity, \(r\) the representative radius, \(\dot m\) steady mass flow and \(\Omega\) rotor angular speed. With negligible torque on other boundaries, the torque exerted by the blades on the fluid is outgoing minus incoming angular momentum flux.

\[M=\dot m(r_2V_{\theta2}-r_1V_{\theta1}).\]

Mechanical power from a rotating shaft is torque times angular speed. Divide by mass flow and write \(U_j=\Omega r_j\) for local blade speed. This gives Euler's turbomachinery work and its corresponding ideal head.

\[w_E=\frac{\Omega M}{\dot m}=U_2V_{\theta2}-U_1V_{\theta1},\]
\[H_E=w_E/g .\]

A velocity triangle relates the absolute velocity to the moving blade through \(\mathbf V=\mathbf U+\mathbf W\), where \(\mathbf W\) is relative velocity. A positive \(w_E\) adds energy to the fluid. Measured hydraulic head can be smaller owing to losses, even when the angular-momentum accounting itself is exact.

Derivation and applications

Extract reaction torque from stationary guide vanes

A stator can carry torque while delivering no shaft power. Consider a steady incompressible annular flow passing through stationary guide vanes. Assume the inlet and outlet flow each have a representative radius, reasonably uniform tangential speed and negligible externally applied torques other than the vanes. Axisymmetric pressure on inlet and outlet planes contributes no torque about the axis. For mass flow \(\dot m\) the instantaneous angular momentum per unit mass is \(rV_\theta\), so its outgoing minus incoming flux fixes the torque applied to the fluid.

\[M_{\rm vane\to fluid}=\dot m(r_2V_{\theta2}-r_1V_{\theta1}),\]
\[M_{\rm fluid\to vane}=-M_{\rm vane\to fluid}.\]

The torque on the casing and the work on the stream are different measurements. A row rotating at angular velocity \(\Omega\) performs shaft work per unit mass proportional to the change in angular momentum. The stationary row follows by setting \(\Omega=0\) even when the change in swirl is substantial.

\[w_{\rm shaft}=\frac{\Omega M_{\rm vane\to fluid}}{\dot m}=\Omega(r_2V_{\theta2}-r_1V_{\theta1}).\]

For a stator at constant height with equal axial velocities at inlet and outlet, reduction of swirl releases kinetic energy. In an ideal lossless row with zero shaft work, Bernoulli's balance transforms that kinetic energy into static pressure.

\[p_2-p_1=\frac{\rho}{2}(V_{\theta1}^2-V_{\theta2}^2).\]

Viscous dissipation lowers the actual recovered pressure. It does not alter the angular-momentum balance when fluxes and all external torques are measured correctly. Residual outlet swirl therefore affects both casing torque and the pressure available downstream.

Worked example

Predict the reaction of a water straightener

Water flows at 2.00 kg/s through vanes at representative radius 0.100 m. Its tangential speed decreases from 4.00 to 1.00 m/s in the same direction. The fluid pushes the casing with \(2.00(0.100)(4.00-1.00)=0.600\) N m. At \(\rho=1000\) kg/m³ the maximum ideal pressure gain is

\[1000(16-1)/2=7.50\,\mathrm{kPa}.\]

The stationary casing absorbs the moment while transmitting zero mechanical power.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Recover an exit angle from casing torque

A second stator takes in air with \(V_{\theta1}=12.0\) m/s at \(r_1=0.150\) m. A 0.900 N m positive casing torque at \(\dot m=0.750\) kg/s leaves \(r_2 V_{\theta2}=1.80-0.900/0.750=0.600\) m²/s. With \(r_2=0.120\) m, outlet swirl is 5.00 m/s. At axial speed 20.0 m/s, the absolute flow direction is \(\arctan(5/20)=14.0^\circ\) from the axis.
