Open this live calculation

Convert a change of swirl into shaft torque

A fluid element can gain energy in a rotating impeller even if its inlet and outlet are at the same elevation. The rotor exerts a torque that changes the angular momentum carried by the flow. Draw a fixed control volume around the rotor. In a steady, single-inlet, single-outlet approximation, with representative mass-flow-weighted tangential velocities \(V_{\theta1},V_{\theta2}\), the axial torque on the fluid is \(\dot m(r_2V_{\theta2}-r_1V_{\theta1})\). Positive tangential velocity points in the direction of shaft rotation.

Multiplying by shaft angular speed converts torque to power. Divide by the mass flow to obtain the Euler specific work. Writing blade speeds \(U_i=\omega r_i\) avoids confusion between the angular velocity of the hardware and the velocity components of the fluid.

\[w_E=U_2V_{\theta2}-U_1V_{\theta1},\]
\[H_E=w_E/g .\]

A pump adding energy has positive \(w_E\) under this convention. A turbine removes energy from the fluid and develops shaft output corresponding to a decrease in the fluid’s angular momentum. A stationary guide vane may change swirl while doing no shaft work because its angular speed is zero.

Relate the blade angle to the exit flow

At each station absolute velocity is the vector sum of local blade velocity \(U\) and velocity \(W\) relative to the moving blade. The throughflow component of \(V\) equals that of \(W\) when the blade motion is purely tangential. For an ideal radial impeller with backward-curved blades, define outlet angle \(\beta_2\) from the backward tangent towards the radial throughflow. Then \(W_{\theta2}=-V_{r2}\cot\beta_2\) and \(V_{\theta2}=U_2-V_{r2}\cot\beta_2\). State this angle convention before reading a drawing; other conventions change the algebraic appearance.

Predict work and distinguish hydraulic delivery

Let the outer radius be 0.10 m, rotor speed 1800 rpm, outlet radial velocity 3.0 m/s, exit blade angle 30°, and inlet swirl zero. The tip speed is 18.85 m/s; the ideal absolute exit swirl is

\[18.85-3\cot30^\circ=13.65\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

Hence specific work is 257.4 J/kg and head \(257.4/9.81=26.24\,\mathrm m\). For water at 8.0 L/s the mass flow is about 8.0 kg/s. Rotor torque on the fluid is \(8(0.10)(13.65)=10.92\,\mathrm{N\,m}\), and work rate is 2.06 kW.

If measurements show an actual delivered head of 21 m at this flow, hydraulic output is \(8(9.81)(21)=1.65\,\mathrm{kW}\). The ratio of actual head to the ideal blade-following estimate is 0.800. It combines departures from the ideal exit triangle with hydraulic losses in this simplified comparison. Mechanical bearing loss would additionally enter a comparison with measured shaft input power. If inlet guide vanes add positive inlet swirl, the subtracted \(U_1V_{\theta1}\) term reduces specific work for the same exit conditions.

Connect the triangle to pump affinity

For geometrically similar operation at unchanged impeller diameter, a fixed ratio \(V_r/U\) holds the ideal outlet triangle shape constant. Flow then scales with shaft speed, head with speed squared and hydraulic power with speed cubed. A real installation with static lift need not move between similar operating points when speed changes.

Derive work from a jet in a moving bucket

A radial pump uses an impeller to add angular momentum to a confined flow. An impulse turbine reverses the sequence: a stationary nozzle first converts pressure head to a high-speed free jet, which transfers momentum to buckets at nearly ambient pressure. Let jet speed \(V_j\) be tangential at the runner and bucket speed \(U\) positive in that direction. Relative inlet speed is \(V_j-U\). Suppose the bucket turns this relative stream through angle \(\pi-\beta\) and its exit relative speed magnitude is \(k(V_j-U)\), with \(k\le1\) accounting for relative-flow loss. The exit tangential absolute speed is

\[V_{\theta2}=U-k(V_j-U)\cos\beta.\]

A succession of buckets intercepts the full continuous nozzle mass flow \(\dot m=\rho Q\), unlike one isolated vane travelling away from a jet. Neglect leakage, windage and mechanical losses. Tangential runner force is \(\dot m(V_j-V_{\theta2})\), hence shaft work per kilogram and hydraulic efficiency relative to incoming jet kinetic energy are

\[w=U(V_j-U)(1+k\cos\beta),\]
\[\eta_j=\frac{2U(V_j-U)(1+k\cos\beta)}{V_j^2}.\]

Differentiating with respect to speed at fixed inlet jet and exit geometry gives \(U=V_j/2\) for maximum power. At zero bucket speed force can be large but shaft power is zero. At bucket speed equal to jet speed there is no relative inlet stream to turn and therefore no useful momentum transfer. These two limits expose an incorrectly drawn exit velocity triangle.

Compare torque at the design and off-design speeds

A laboratory nozzle delivers 12 L/s of water at 30 m/s to a runner of pitch radius 0.20 m. Adopt \(k=0.85\) and \(\beta=15^\circ\), so

\[1+k\cos\beta=1.821.\]

At optimum speed \(U=15\,\mathrm{m/s}\) and \(\omega=75\,\mathrm{rad/s}\), about 716 rpm. The runner force is \(12(30-15)(1.821)=328\,\mathrm N\), torque 65.6 N m, and power \(15(328)=4.92\,\mathrm{kW}\). The jet supplied \(12(30^2)/2=5.40\,\mathrm{kW}\), giving efficiency 91.0% under these idealised runner assumptions.

Load the shaft until bucket speed drops to 9 m/s. The force rises to \(12(21)(1.821)=459\,\mathrm N\) and torque rises to 91.8 N m, but power drops to \(9(459)=4.13\,\mathrm{kW}\). Increased measured torque therefore does not demonstrate improved conversion without the speed measurement. The exit tangential velocity becomes \(9-0.85(21)\cos15^\circ=-8.24\,\mathrm{m/s}\), indicating the bucket now reverses some absolute tangential momentum. Maintaining the assumed jet flow requires sufficient nozzle head independently of shaft loading; a restricted supply may change both jet velocity and flow rate during the test.

At ideal design speed the leaving water still has a radial or transverse velocity because \(\beta\) is nonzero. Its relative exit transverse component is \(k(V_j-U)\sin\beta=3.30\,\mathrm{m/s}\). The absolute tangential component is \(15-0.85(15)\cos15^\circ=2.69\,\mathrm{m/s}\), giving residual jet kinetic energy about 9.1 J/kg. The rest of the gap between inlet kinetic energy and shaft work includes dissipation associated with \(k<1\). It would be incorrect to attribute the entire efficiency shortfall to measured tangential exit velocity alone. Traversing velocity components or collecting the discharge separately provides a diagnostic of losses within the bucket.

Chapter 12 · Compressible nozzles and choking

Relate area change to Mach number and calculate the limiting mass flow through a converging nozzle.

From first principles

Derive the area–velocity relation for an isentropic stream

Consider a steady, quasi-one-dimensional flow through a smooth duct of area \(A\). Assume negligible viscosity, heat exchange and elevation change, and no shocks. Density \(\rho\) and speed \(V\) vary between stations. Mass conservation gives constant \(\rho VA\), so taking logarithmic differentials relates three fractional changes.

\[\frac{d\rho}{\rho}+\frac{dV}{V}+\frac{dA}{A}=0 .\]

Streamwise momentum gives

\[dp+\rho V\,dV=0.\]

For an isentropic change, sound speed satisfies \(a^2=(\partial p/\partial\rho)_s\) so the pressure differential can be eliminated in favour of density. With \(M=V/a\) this yields

\[\frac{d\rho}{\rho}=-\frac{V\,dV}{a^2}=-M^2\frac{dV}{V},\]
\[\frac{dA}{A}=(M^2-1)\frac{dV}{V}.\]

Subsonic flow accelerates in a contraction because its density variation is comparatively small. A supersonic stream accelerates in a diverging duct because density falls more rapidly. A smooth transition through \(M=1\) with nonzero velocity gradient requires a stationary area, usually the minimum area of a nozzle. Reservoir state and downstream pressure determine whether that sonic condition is actually reached.

Derivation and applications

Recover subsonic Mach number from stagnation pressure

A forward-facing pressure probe brings a small sample of moving gas nearly to rest. Relating the resulting pressure to speed requires accounting for compressibility. Consider a calorically perfect ideal gas, constant heat-capacity ratio \(\gamma>1\), no heat input or shaft work, and subsonic flow brought reversibly to rest. Neglect the height change. Use \(a^2=\gamma R T\), Mach number \(M=V/a\) and \(c_p=\gamma R/(\gamma-1)\). The steady energy balance gives

\[c_pT_0=c_pT+\tfrac12 V^2,\]
\[\frac{T_0}{T}=1+\frac{\gamma-1}{2}M^2.\]

For an isentropic ideal gas, \(p/\rho^\gamma\) is constant. Using the gas law to eliminate density yields a pressure ratio as a power of the temperature ratio. This provides a relation between a stagnation tap and a separate static tap at the same upstream state.

\[\frac{p_0}{p}=\left(\frac{T_0}{T}\right)^{\gamma/(\gamma-1)}=\left(1+\frac{\gamma-1}{2}M^2\right)^{\gamma/(\gamma-1)}.\]

Solve for the nonnegative speed magnitude. A static temperature measurement is needed to convert dimensionless Mach number into dimensional velocity. Expanding the pressure law for small Mach number recovers the familiar dynamic pressure as its leading term.

\[M=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\left[\left(\frac{p_0}{p}\right)^{(\gamma-1)/\gamma}-1\right]},\]
\[p_0-p=\tfrac12\rho V^2+O(pM^4).\]

Both pressures in the ratio must be absolute. A supersonic probe can form a shock and its recovered stagnation pressure then differs from the reversible upstream value.

Worked example

Estimate air speed from two pressure readings

For air take \(\gamma=1.40\), \(R=287\) J/(kg K), \(T=290\) K, \(p=90.0\) kPa and \(p_0=106\) kPa. Their ratio is 1.178, giving \(M=\sqrt{5[(106/90)^{2/7}-1]}=0.489\). The sound speed is 341 m/s, so the flow speed is about 167 m/s. The incompressible pressure estimate using static density would be higher because the gas compresses while it decelerates.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Predict the pressure ratio of a calibration stream

A second air stream has measured speed 102 m/s and static temperature 288 K, yielding \(a=340\) m/s and \(M=0.300\). At static pressure 100 kPa, the stagnation rise is

\[100[(1+0.2(0.300)^2)^{3.5}-1]=6.44\,\mathrm{kPa}.\]

The leading incompressible expression predicts

\[0.5\gamma pM^2=6.30\,\mathrm{kPa}.\]

Their 0.14 kPa difference sets a scale for the pressure resolution needed to detect compressibility here.

Keep the fixed assumptions stated in the readout when comparing with the example values.

Open this live calculation

Incorporate expansion work and mass conservation

Gas accelerates through a nozzle by converting enthalpy into kinetic energy. At sufficiently high speeds, density changes appreciably and a constant-density volume balance fails. For steady one-dimensional adiabatic flow without shaft work or appreciable elevation change, \(h+V^2/2=h_0\). A calorically perfect gas has \(h=c_pT\); its local sound speed is \(a=\sqrt{\gamma RT}\), and Mach number is \(M=V/a\). Evaluate \(a\) at local static temperature, rather than at the upstream reservoir temperature.

Assume the acceleration is reversible, so \(dp=a^2d\rho\) along the isentrope. Differential momentum balance gives \(dp=-\rho VdV\), hence \(d\rho/\rho=-M^2dV/V\). Inserting this in continuity \(d\rho/\rho+dV/V+dA/A=0\) gives the area–velocity relation

\[\frac{dA}{A}=(M^2-1)\frac{dV}{V}.\]

A subsonic stream accelerates through a converging passage. A supersonic stream accelerates through a diverging one. A smooth transition through sonic conditions requires a stationary area, conventionally a minimum-area throat for acceleration from subsonic to supersonic speed. A converging nozzle supplied from rest can first reach Mach one at its smallest area, the exit.

Obtain the throat state from the reservoir

In the upstream reservoir the flow is essentially at rest, so its static pressure and temperature are stagnation values \(p_0,T_0\). Combining \(c_p(T_0-T)=V^2/2\) with \(c_p=\gamma R/(\gamma-1)\) gives

\[T/T_0=[1+(\gamma-1)M^2/2]^{-1}.\]

Apply the ideal-gas isentropic pressure–temperature relation to find the critical pressure and temperature ratios at \(M=1\).

\[T^*/T_0=2/(\gamma+1),\]
\[p^*/p_0=[2/(\gamma+1)]^{\gamma/(\gamma-1)} .\]

With exit area fixed, lowering back pressure increases mass flow until the exit becomes sonic. Further reduction in back pressure does not change the ideal throat state for fixed reservoir conditions. Expansion downstream can then adjust the emerging jet to its surroundings.

Estimate a choked discharge

An insulated converging air nozzle has throat area \(50\,\mathrm{mm^2}\) and reservoir conditions 300 kPa absolute and 300 K. Use \(R=287\,\mathrm{J/(kg\,K)}\) and \(\gamma=1.40\). The critical temperature is \(300(2/2.4)=250\,\mathrm K\), critical pressure \(300(2/2.4)^{3.5}=158.5\,\mathrm{kPa}\), density \(158500/(287\times250)=2.21\,\mathrm{kg/m^3}\) and critical speed \(\sqrt{1.4(287)(250)}=317\,\mathrm{m/s}\).

Atmospheric back pressure of 100 kPa lies below the critical pressure, so the ideal mass flow is

\[\rho^*a^*A^*=2.21(317)(50\times10^{-6})=0.0350\,\mathrm{kg/s}.\]

A back pressure of 210 kPa would leave this converging nozzle unchoked, and the exit state must then be calculated from that back pressure. Real contraction and boundary-layer losses can reduce the flow through a calibrated orifice compared with the ideal nozzle prediction; a measured discharge coefficient accounts for those effects within its tested range.

Predict changes at a choked throat

At fixed reservoir pressure, the critical temperature is proportional to \(T_0\), critical density to \(1/T_0\) and critical speed to \(\sqrt{T_0}\). Their product shows that the choked mass flow scales as \(p_0A^*/\sqrt{T_0}\) for the same gas. Warming the original reservoir from 300 to 360 K at unchanged absolute pressure therefore reduces the ideal mass flow by \(\sqrt{300/360}=0.913\), to about 0.0320 kg/s. Increasing throat area or reservoir pressure has a direct linear effect while the downstream pressure stays low enough to maintain choking.

Derive the admissible jump from conservation laws

Supersonic gas in a duct can decelerate abruptly through a normal shock. The shock is thin compared with the duct dimensions, so treat it as a stationary discontinuity of unchanged area. Take a calorically perfect gas, no heat or shaft work and negligible elevation change. Mass, streamwise momentum and stagnation enthalpy are conserved:

\[\rho_1V_1=\rho_2V_2,\]
\[p_1+\rho_1V_1^2=p_2+\rho_2V_2^2,\]
\[c_pT_1+V_1^2/2=c_pT_2+V_2^2/2 .\]

Entropy increases through the shock, so the two end states cannot be joined with the isentropic pressure–temperature relation. Set \(r=\rho_2/\rho_1=V_1/V_2\). Momentum gives \(p_2/p_1=1+\gamma M_1^2(1-1/r)\) and the gas law gives

\[T_2/T_1=(p_2/p_1)/r.\]

Substitution into the energy equation yields two roots, one for unchanged flow and the compression solution

\[r=\frac{(\gamma+1)M_1^2}{2+(\gamma-1)M_1^2},\]
\[M_2^2=\frac{1+(\gamma-1)M_1^2/2}{\gamma M_1^2-(\gamma-1)/2}.\]

For \(M_1>1\) this second state has higher density and lower Mach number. As \(M_1\to1^+\), every state ratio tends to unity. For very large upstream Mach number the compression ratio approaches \((\gamma+1)/(\gamma-1)\) in the constant-specific-heat approximation, although real-gas changes eventually limit that approximation.

Calculate a diffuser’s stagnation-pressure loss

Air upstream of a normal shock has \(M_1=2.0,\ p_1=80\,\mathrm{kPa}\) absolute and \(T_1=250\,\mathrm K\). Use \(\gamma=1.4,\ R=287\,\mathrm{J/(kg\,K)}\). The density ratio is

\[2.4(4)/(2+0.4(4))=2.667,\]
\[p_2/p_1=1+1.4(4)(1-1/2.667)=4.50.\]

Hence \(p_2=360\,\mathrm{kPa}\) and \(T_2=250(4.50/2.667)=421.9\,\mathrm K\). The downstream Mach number is

\[\sqrt{1.8/(5.6-0.2)}=0.577.\]

Upstream speed is approximately 634 m/s and mass conservation gives about 238 m/s downstream.

The stagnation temperature upstream is \(250[1+0.2(4)]=450\,\mathrm K\); downstream it is \(421.9[1+0.2(0.577)^2]=450\,\mathrm K\) as required by energy conservation. Evaluate stagnation pressure separately from each local state by a hypothetical isentropic deceleration on its own side. Upstream \(p_{01}=80(1.8)^{3.5}=626\,\mathrm{kPa}\), whereas downstream \(p_{02}=360(1.0667)^{3.5}=451\,\mathrm{kPa}\). Recovery \(p_{02}/p_{01}=0.721\) shows that a large static pressure rise can accompany a 28% loss of stagnation pressure. For the same stagnation temperature the entropy rise is \(-R\ln(0.721)=94\,\mathrm{J/(kg\,K)}\), a positive value consistent with the irreversible deceleration.

A pitot tube facing the upstream supersonic flow forms its own shock ahead of the tip. The final stagnation pressure measured there corresponds ideally to isentropic deceleration after the probe’s shock, about 451 kPa here, rather than to the upstream isentropic stagnation pressure of 626 kPa. A conventional subsonic pitot reduction applied to the reading would therefore infer an incorrect upstream speed. By contrast, a static pressure tap placed sufficiently far upstream can still report the 80 kPa static state. The ratio \(451/80=5.64\) illustrates why the probe configuration must be stated when comparing a pressure ratio with a compressible-flow prediction.

The downstream temperature calculation can also be checked using mass flux. The upstream density is \(80000/[287(250)]=1.12\,\mathrm{kg/m^3}\) and the measured downstream static values imply \(360000/[287(421.9)]=2.97\,\mathrm{kg/m^3}\). Multiplication by the corresponding velocities gives roughly \(707\,\mathrm{kg/(m^2s)}\) on both sides. A calculation conserving stagnation pressure instead would generally fail one of these basic balances even if its pressure trend looked plausible.

The shock also changes the area needed to choke a downstream passage. For a given gas the choked mass flow scales as \(A^*p_0/\sqrt{T_0}\). Stagnation temperature is unchanged but stagnation pressure drops to 72.1% of its upstream value. Passing the same mass flow therefore requires a downstream critical area larger by \(1/0.721=1.39\). This does not mean the physical duct jumps in area at the normal shock. The critical area is the sonic reference area for an isentropic stream tube drawn from each side, and the two hypothetical reference conditions differ because entropy has increased.

A converging test section downstream that was sized from the upstream stagnation pressure may consequently choke earlier than expected. The entropy and critical-area tests point to the same lost capacity from different directions: one uses the gas state, the other its maximum deliverable mass flux. Together with conservation of actual mass flux at the shock, they make it possible to distinguish a real shock loss from a simple mismatch between static and total pressure instrumentation.

Find the two states that share a nozzle area ratio

For steady isentropic flow of a calorically perfect gas, combine continuity with stagnation enthalpy and the isentropic pressure law. The critical area \(A^*\) is the area giving Mach one for the same mass flow and stagnation state.

Problem sheet 6 · Rotor work and compressible discharge

Close angular momentum and nozzle balances while distinguishing measured performance from ideal reference states.

Add inlet pre-swirl

An impeller has \(U_1=9,\ U_2=18\) m/s, exit swirl 12 m/s and positive inlet swirl 3 m/s. Fluid mass flow is 4 kg/s. Find Euler work, power transfer and head. What changes if inlet swirl is reversed at unchanged exit conditions?

Reveal the worked solution

Specific work is \(18(12)-9(3)=189\,\mathrm{J/kg}\); ideal power transfer is 756 W and head 19.27 m. With reversed inlet swirl the term becomes positive in the work difference, giving 243 J/kg, 972 W and 24.77 m. The change reflects a larger increase in the angular momentum of the fluid.

Reconstruct the velocity triangle

At an ideal radial impeller exit the blade speed is 24 m/s, radial fluid velocity 4 m/s and backward blade angle 45°. Calculate exit swirl, absolute speed magnitude and work at zero inlet swirl. If delivered head is 40 m, find the ratio of measured to ideal head.

Reveal the worked solution

The blade-relative tangential component is \(-4\cot45^\circ=-4\,\mathrm{m/s}\), giving \(V_{\theta2}=20\,\mathrm{m/s}\). The absolute speed is

\[\sqrt{20^2+4^2}=20.40\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

Work is \(24(20)=480\,\mathrm{J/kg}\), or 48.93 m ideal head. The measured head ratio is

\[40/48.93=0.818.\]

Compare two back pressures

Air at 400 kPa absolute and 360 K discharges through an ideal converging nozzle of exit area 30 mm². Use \(\gamma=1.4\), \(R=287\,\mathrm{J/(kg\,K)}\). Are back pressures 180 and 250 kPa choked? Find mass flow in the choked case and exit speed in the unchoked case.

Reveal the worked solution

Critical pressure is \(400(2/2.4)^{3.5}=211.3\,\mathrm{kPa}\); 180 kPa chokes, 250 kPa does not. At choking \(T^*=300\,\mathrm K\), \(\rho^*=2.45\,\mathrm{kg/m^3}\), \(a^*=347.2\,\mathrm{m/s}\), hence \(\dot m=0.0256\,\mathrm{kg/s}\). At 250 kPa,

\[T=360(250/400)^{0.4/1.4}=314.8\,\mathrm K.\]

The energy balance with \(c_p=1004.5\,\mathrm{J/(kg\,K)}\) gives

\[V=\sqrt{2c_p(360-314.8)}=301\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

Find the operating point and account for the lost head

A pump between two open tanks supplies head Hₚ = 24 − 0.020q² metres, where q is measured in litres per second. The upper surface is 4.0 m higher, and pipe losses give hL = 0.030q² metres. With water density 1000 kg/m³ and pump efficiency 0.75, find volume flow, electrical input assuming an ideal motor, and the share used to raise the water. Then throttle the discharge by adding 0.030q² of valve loss.

Worked solution

Equalising pump and system head gives 0.050q² = 20, or q = 20.0 L/s. The head is 16.0 m, split into 4.0 m elevation and 12.0 m pipe loss. The fluid receives ρgQH = 3.14 kW and the assumed motor supplies 4.19 kW. Only 0.785 kW raises gravitational potential. It is one quarter of the fluid power and about 18.8% of the input.

With the valve, 0.080q² = 20 gives 15.81 L/s and a pump head of 19.0 m. Pipe and valve each dissipate 7.50 m. The new fluid power is 2.95 kW and useful lift power is 0.620 kW. Increasing the system resistance reduces flow even as the operating head rises.

An energy check sums elevation, pipe and valve contributions at the new flow. Extrapolating a fixed 75% efficiency beyond this example would need a measured efficiency curve. Also, tank surface velocities are negligible here; a discharge jet carrying appreciable kinetic energy would add an endpoint term.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Angular momentum and work in a rotor; Compressible nozzles and choking. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 13 · How a moving wall sends momentum into a liquid

A plate oscillating horizontally in a deep bath has no average velocity. It nevertheless dissipates energy continuously. A velocity measurement at two heights can reveal the fluid’s viscosity.

Keep the unsteady term in a parallel flow

Let \(y\) increase upward from a very broad, horizontal plate. The liquid is Newtonian and incompressible with constant kinematic viscosity \(\nu\); the plate moves parallel to itself with speed \(U\cos\omega t\). Ignore edges, any pressure gradient along the plate and transients left from starting the motion. By symmetry the velocity has a single component \(u(y,t)\). A parcel has no vertical speed and meets no streamwise velocity variation, so convective acceleration vanishes even though the motion is time dependent. Horizontal momentum balance on a slice of thickness \(dy\) compares its acceleration with the shear-stress difference on its two faces.

\[\rho\,dy\,\partial_t u=\tau(y+dy)-\tau(y),\]
\[\tau=\mu\,\partial_yu,\]
\[\partial_tu=\nu\partial_y^2u .\]

Velocity is prescribed at the plate by no slip and tends to zero far away. The same equation describes thermal diffusion with temperature in place of velocity. Here the quantity transferred is horizontal momentum, and a velocity gradient sets its transfer rate.

Read decay and phase from one complex root

Represent periodic velocity by the real part of \(\widehat u(y)e^{i\omega t}\). Inserting it into the equation gives

\[\widehat u''=(i\omega/\nu)\widehat u.\]

Since \((1+i)^2=2i\), the decaying branch of the solution has an exponent with equal real and imaginary parts.

\[\delta=\sqrt{2\nu/\omega},\]
\[u(y,t)=Ue^{-y/\delta}\cos(\omega t-y/\delta).\]

Every increase in height by \(\delta\) divides the amplitude by \(e\) and delays the phase by one radian. This \(\delta\) is a scale rather than a physical edge. The unbounded solution requires the top and sidewalls to be far enough away for the amplitude there to be negligible. At five penetration depths it is less than one percent of its value at the plate.

Worked example · interpret a submerged velocity probe

In an oil with \(\nu=5.0\times10^{-5}\) m²/s, set \(f=2.00\) Hz and \(U=0.080\) m/s. Using \(\omega=2\pi f\) gives \(\delta=2.82\) mm. At 3.00 mm the predicted amplitude is

\[0.080e^{-3/2.82}=0.0276\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

The phase lag is 1.06 rad or 0.0846 s. A synchronized velocity snapshot can therefore show nearly stationary liquid while the wall speed is large. A single instantaneous ratio generally differs from the ratio of fitted amplitudes.

Worked example · power delivered to the wall layer

The wall exerts on the fluid a tangential traction \(-\mu u_y(0,t)\). Its complex amplitude is \(\mu U(1+i)/\delta\). Only the in-phase part contributes to power averaged over a cycle, since averaging a product of two cosines gives one half of the cosine of their phase difference. Thus

\[\overline P/A=\mu U^2/(2\delta).\]

For the same oil at density 900 kg/m³, \(\mu=0.045\) Pa s and the mean power is 0.0510 W/m². The positive value is consistent with viscous dissipation even though mean shear and mean speed separately vanish.

Freeze the phase at a quarter cycle and find the depth at which the velocity first changes sign away from the wall. Sweep the phase to distinguish that moving zero from the stationary amplitude envelope, then double the frequency and predict the new penetration depth.

Open this experiment online

A related OneSimulation calculation

The course flat-plate calculation provides a second momentum-diffusion example. Predict what doubling viscosity does to the measured layer thickness at a fixed downstream station, then compare the square-root scaling across the two geometries.

Tutorial · estimate a material property without a force sensor

At 1.00 Hz two probes spaced 2.00 mm vertically report fitted amplitudes in the ratio upper/lower = 0.500. Estimate \(\delta,\nu\), and the phase difference. Identify a measurement that would falsify this model even if the amplitude ratio agreed.

Use both the magnitude and phase predictions

The spacing divided by \(\delta\) is \(\ln2\), giving \(\delta=2.89\) mm. Then \(\nu=\omega\delta^2/2=2.62\times10^{-5}\) m²/s. The upper signal must lag by 0.693 rad, equivalent to 0.110 s. A substantial phase discrepancy suggests confinement, an unremoved transient or motion beyond a one-dimensional shear layer. Fitting both observables is a stronger test than assigning a viscosity to every amplitude pair.

Further reading · MIT 2.25, Stokes’ second problem (PDF)

Chapter 14 · A cylinder in potential flow and the absent drag

A pressure field can vary by several dynamic-pressure heads around a body while producing exactly zero resultant drag in an ideal flow. Derive the field and identify what a real measurement must add.

Satisfy two boundary conditions with a potential

Consider steady two-dimensional flow of constant density far from a circular cylinder of radius \(a\). Assume irrotational motion in the exterior, an inviscid fluid and zero circulation. Then \(\mathbf u=\nabla\phi\) and incompressibility gives

\[\nabla^2\phi=0.\]

The incoming uniform velocity \(U\mathbf e_x\) has potential \(Ur\cos\theta\). The cylinder cannot absorb fluid, so the radial velocity must vanish at \(r=a\). Seek a correction with the same angular dependence and write \(\phi=f(r)\cos\theta\). The polar Laplacian reduces to

\[r^2f''+rf'-f=0,\]
\[f=Ar+B/r .\]

The coefficient of the growing term must be \(U\) for the far field. The derivative \(f'=U-B/r^2\) vanishes at \(a\) if \(B=Ua^2\). Taking radial and tangential derivatives then gives the complete external velocity field.

\[u_r=U(1-a^2/r^2)\cos\theta,\]
\[u_\theta=-U(1+a^2/r^2)\sin\theta .\]

The surface speed is zero at the upstream and downstream points. At either side it reaches \(2U\). The tangency condition constrains the normal component alone. At a real viscous wall liquid immediately adjacent to the solid also satisfies zero tangential speed. The ideal surface speed should be interpreted as an outer-flow prediction.

From speed to pressure and from pressure to force

Along streamlines Bernoulli relates static pressure to speed at the same height. Since the incoming flow is uniform and irrotational the same Bernoulli constant applies throughout this ideal solution. Normalize the pressure change by \(q=\rho U^2/2\). On the body,

\[C_p=(p-p_\infty)/q=1-4\sin^2\theta .\]

Pressure force on an arc per unit span is \(-p(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)a\,d\theta\). Points at \(\theta\) and \(\pi-\theta\) have identical pressures and opposite horizontal normals. The corresponding drag contributions cancel exactly. Top and bottom symmetry cancels lift. A real cylinder can have a separated wake with low rear pressure. Substituting the attached-flow pressure at the rear is the assumption that then fails.

Worked example · read three ports

Water of density 1000 kg/m³ flows at 0.80 m/s, so \(q=320\) Pa. At the upstream stagnation point the model predicts \(p-p_\infty=+320\) Pa. At a point where the normal is 45° to the stream axis, \(\sin^2\theta=1/2\), so \(C_p=-1\) and the reading is −320 Pa. On the side the predicted reading is −960 Pa. The front-to-side difference is 1280 Pa, four times the incident dynamic pressure. A pressure transducer connected to these two ports requires the correct geometric factor before being interpreted as a speed meter.

Worked example · convert a wake discrepancy into a drag

Approximate a measured departure from the ideal pressure by reducing pressure uniformly by 150 Pa on the downstream semicircle and leaving the upstream half unchanged. For a radius of 50 mm, the added downstream force per span is

\[150a\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos\theta\,d\theta=15.0\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

A span of 0.40 m gives 6.00 N before end effects. This integration illustrates why even a modest rear pressure error changes the net drag materially. Local low pressure on the sides contributes little horizontal force where the outward normals point almost vertically.

Begin with zero rear pressure deficit and check the pressure symmetry about the transverse axis. Introduce a 160 Pa downstream deficit at 0.80 m/s. Predict the pressure-drag coefficient from the projected area before reading the result.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · estimate speed and test the symmetry hypothesis

An ideal front-to-side differential is 720 Pa in water. Infer the incoming speed. Two further ports mounted at 60° and 120° report a difference of 90 Pa. Can a different incoming speed make that second difference consistent with the assumed model?

Separate calibration from model failure

The front-to-side difference is \(4q=2\rho U^2\), giving \(U=0.600\) m/s. Both other ports have the same \(\sin^2\theta=3/4\). Their ideal pressure difference vanishes at every speed. A discrepancy at these ports therefore tests symmetry and the assumed attached field independently of the first calibration.

Further reading · MIT 2.25, Potential Flow Theory

Chapter 15 · Curvature pressure, wetting and capillary rise

The water height in a narrow glass tube is a measurement of an interface force. Starting from a surface energy explains both its direction and its dependence on tube radius.

Convert a surface-energy change into a pressure difference

A clean liquid–gas interface of fixed composition and temperature requires reversible work \(\gamma\,dA\) to increase its area by \(dA\). The surface tension \(\gamma\) therefore has equivalent units J/m² and N/m. Imagine a spherical drop changing radius by \(dR\). Pressure inside does work while the outside pressure resists. Equating net pressure work to the increase in interface energy gives

\[(p_i-p_o)\,dV=\gamma\,dA,\]
\[p_i-p_o=\gamma\frac{8\pi R}{4\pi R^2}=\frac{2\gamma}{R}.\]

For a local patch with two principal radii the same argument gives \(\Delta p=\gamma(1/R_1+1/R_2)\) with signed curvature and consistently chosen normal. A cylinder has only one nonzero curvature, so the spherical factor of two is inappropriate there. A thin soap bubble has two nearly equal interfaces and doubles the pressure jump for a given radius.

Background · Capillary pressure
\[\Delta p=\gamma(1/R_1+1/R_2)\]

Principal curvatures carry signs set by the chosen normal. A cylindrical interface contributes only one finite principal curvature.

Close the balance at the wall

Put a vertical tube of inner radius \(r\) into a bath and let \(\theta\) be the contact angle measured through the liquid. Surface tension along the circular contact line has upward component \(2\pi r\gamma\cos\theta\). The excess column over the external free surface has effective weight \((\rho_l-\rho_g)g\pi r^2 h\). Neglect meniscus volume relative to the column and assume equilibrium, a broad bath, a uniform tube and a well-defined contact angle.

\[h=\frac{2\gamma\cos\theta}{(\rho_l-\rho_g)gr}.\]

The sign of \(h\) changes when \(\theta\) exceeds 90°. A smaller tube gives more contact-line length per unit cross-sectional area and therefore supports a taller column. The same equation follows from the pressure jump for a nearly spherical cap of radius \(r/\cos\theta\) in magnitude and a hydrostatic pressure change. If gravity distorts the local cap considerably, the full interface shape must be solved. The capillary length \(\sqrt{\gamma/(\Delta\rho g)}\) indicates when tube radius is small on the relevant scale.

Worked example · inspect a level reading

Take water with \(\gamma=0.072\) N/m, density difference 1000 kg/m³, radius 0.400 mm and contact angle 30°. The rise is

\[2(0.072)\cos30^\circ/[1000(9.81)(0.0004)]=31.8\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The hydrostatic pressure difference is 312 Pa. The capillary length is about 2.71 mm, comfortably larger than the radius. A reader who assumes perfect wetting would infer 36.7 mm. The uncertainty in surface condition can therefore exceed a millimetre-scale ruler uncertainty.

Worked example · use a second liquid column as a test

In a tube of radius 0.500 mm a liquid of density 850 kg/m³ rises 10.0 mm. The product determined by this experiment is

\[\gamma\cos\theta=\Delta\rho g r h/2=0.02085\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

If an independent measurement gives \(\gamma=0.0300\) N/m, then \(\cos\theta=0.695\), or \(\theta=46.0^\circ\). A second tube of half the radius should approximately double the height on identically prepared walls. It does not by itself separate contact angle from surface tension because the same product appears in both balances.

At a fixed radius rotate the contact angle through 90 degrees. Estimate the sign and magnitude just on either side of the crossing. Compare the effect of halving surface tension with doubling radius in the complete-wetting comparison.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · compare a drop and a bubble

A clean water drop of radius 0.60 mm has \(\gamma=0.072\) N/m. Find its excess pressure. A spherical soap film of the same radius has tension 0.030 N/m at each of its two interfaces. Which object has the larger excess pressure? State a radius at which the soap bubble would match the drop.

Count each interface

The drop has one interface and excess pressure \(2(0.072)/0.00060=240\) Pa. The bubble has two interfaces and \(4(0.030)/0.00060=200\) Pa. To match 240 Pa its radius must be 0.500 mm. The liquid volume between the bubble interfaces is treated as small enough for both interface radii to coincide.

Further reading · J. Bush, MIT 18.357, Lecture 5, stress boundary conditions and the static meniscus (PDF)

Study the full derivation

Gravity, capillarity and instability at a moving interface · Momentum, motion and transport in fluids

A flat interface can carry waves restored by gravity and surface tension. If its two liquids or gases move at different speeds, a band of those waves can grow. Derive the ideal threshold, identify the most vulnerable wavelength at onset and distinguish growth of an instability from advection of an otherwise stable disturbance.

Chapter 16 · Critical depth and the hydraulic jump

In a wide shallow channel the same discharge can pass as a shallow fast stream or as a deeper slow stream. A gradual hump and an abrupt jump connect depths through different conservation laws.

Minimise the energy needed to carry a discharge

Use a broad rectangular channel and let \(q=Q/b\) be its volume flow per unit width. At a section with approximately uniform speed \(u\) and water depth \(h\), mass conservation gives \(u=q/h\). Take pressure to be hydrostatic over the depth and neglect losses between adjacent smooth sections. Measured relative to the local channel bottom, specific energy in metres of head is

\[E(h)=h+\frac{q^2}{2gh^2},\]
\[\frac{dE}{dh}=1-\frac{q^2}{gh^3}=1-\mathrm{Fr}^2 .\]

The derivative vanishes at \(h_c=(q^2/g)^{1/3}\), where \(\mathrm{Fr}=u/\sqrt{gh}=1\). The second derivative \(3q^2/(gh^4)\) is positive. Critical depth is the unique minimum, with \(E_c=3h_c/2\). The speed \(\sqrt{gh}\) is the speed of small long gravity waves relative to shallow water. When \(u\) exceeds it, even a disturbance travelling upstream relative to the liquid is carried downstream relative to the bed.

Background · Froude number
\[\mathrm{Fr}=U/\sqrt{gh}\]

For shallow rectangular flow with gravity waves this compares material speed to wave speed. Critical depth occurs when the two are equal.

Worked example · decide if a smooth rise can pass the flow

A channel carries \(q=0.40\) m²/s at depth 0.50 m. Speed is 0.80 m/s and specific energy is

\[0.50+0.80^2/(2g)=0.5326\,\mathrm{m}.\]

Critical depth is 0.2536 m and the minimum specific energy is 0.3804 m. Since total head includes bed elevation, a local rise of up to about 0.152 m can be accommodated in the ideal fixed-upstream-state model. A higher rise requires the upstream water level or discharge to adjust. For a small rise on the subcritical branch the available specific energy decreases and depth decreases toward the critical value.

Keep momentum across a short rapid transition

A hydraulic jump is turbulent and strongly dissipative. Place control surfaces far enough upstream and downstream for pressure to be hydrostatic again. Across a short horizontal reach, neglect net bed shear compared with the momentum and end-pressure forces. The hydrostatic force on an end section is \(\rho g h^2/2\) per unit width, obtained by integrating a triangular pressure profile. Momentum flux is \(\rho q^2/h\). Requiring equal momentum functions gives

\[\frac{h_1^2}{2}+\frac{q^2}{g h_1}=\frac{h_2^2}{2}+\frac{q^2}{g h_2}.\]

Factor out \(h_2-h_1\) and take the distinct-depth root. With \(r=h_2/h_1\), the remaining relation is

\[r(r+1)=2\mathrm{Fr}_1^2.\]

The positive solution and associated energy drop are

\[r=\frac{\sqrt{1+8\mathrm{Fr}_1^2}-1}{2},\]
\[E_1-E_2=\frac{(h_2-h_1)^3}{4h_1h_2}.\]

The loss is positive for a jump from shallow to deep flow. Reversing the depths would require a gain in mechanical energy without a source, which excludes that continuation of the same algebraic equation.

Worked example · locate the conjugate depth

At a flume section let \(h_1=0.100\) m and \(u_1=2.00\) m/s. Then \(q=0.200\) m²/s and \(\mathrm{Fr}_1=2.02\). The depth ratio is 2.40, giving \(h_2\simeq0.240\) m and \(u_2\simeq0.835\) m/s. Upstream energy is 0.304 m and downstream energy about 0.275 m. Momentum balance predicts a head drop near 0.0285 m. Its location along the channel also depends on the imposed tailwater level; the two-section balance itself contains no position variable.

Follow the head over the bump until its horizontal line just touches the energy curve. Estimate the maximum admissible bed rise from the two vertical readings. Predict how changing discharge moves that tangency before changing the input.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · infer discharge from two depth gauges

Two gauges straddle a stationary jump and report 0.12 and 0.30 m. Use the momentum relation to recover the discharge per unit width, determine which side is upstream and calculate the specific mechanical-energy loss.

Recover the flow and check its direction

Factoring the momentum equality gives \(q^2=g h_1 h_2(h_1+h_2)/2\), hence \(q=0.2724\) m²/s. The shallow side has speed 2.27 m/s and Froude number 2.09. The deeper side has speed 0.908 m/s and Froude number 0.529. Taking the shallow side upstream yields energy loss \(0.18^3/[4(0.12)(0.30)]=0.0405\) m. Both discharge and the sign of dissipation support this ordering.

Further reading · W. Smyth, All Things Flow, §9.3, hydraulic jumps and bores

Chapter 17 · A viscous squeeze film as a rate-dependent support

Bring two smooth circular disks slowly together with a thin layer of liquid between them. The force needed depends strongly on gap size even when neither surface is elastic.

Decide which motion the gap permits

Take rigid parallel disks of radius \(R\) separated by \(h(t)\ll R\), filled with an incompressible Newtonian liquid of viscosity \(\mu\). The lower disk is fixed and the upper disk approaches at speed \(V=-\dot h>0\). Assume an axisymmetric film discharging to ambient pressure at the edge, no slip on both disks and negligible inertia. The latter requires viscous stress to dominate transient and convective inertia, including the scale \(\rho Vh/\mu\ll1\). Fluid displaced from the centre must travel sideways through the thin film. Across-gap derivatives dominate radial derivatives of velocity, and the leading pressure is uniform through the thickness.

\[\mu\,\partial_z^2 u_r=\frac{dp}{dr},\]
\[u_r(0)=u_r(h)=0 .\]

Two integrations give

\[u_r=(dp/dr)z(z-h)/(2\mu).\]

The pressure gradient must be negative for outward flow. Integrating velocity over the gap gives radial volume flow per circumferential length \(q_r=-h^3(dp/dr)/(12\mu)\). The cubic power comes from a velocity scale proportional to \(h^2\) multiplied by the available gap \(h\).

Balance the displaced volume inside every circle

The film inside radius \(r\) loses volume at rate \(\pi r^2 V\). With no compressibility or leakage through the disks this must equal \(2\pi r q_r\). Hence \(q_r=rV/2\). Match to the viscous flow relation, integrate inward from \(p(R)=p_a\), and sum the excess pressure over the wetted disk:

\[\frac{dp}{dr}=-\frac{6\mu Vr}{h^3},\]
\[p(r)-p_a=\frac{3\mu V}{h^3}(R^2-r^2),\]
\[F=\int_0^R [p(r)-p_a]\,2\pi r\,dr=\frac{3\pi\mu V R^4}{2h^3}.\]

The pressure distribution is parabolic; its area average is half its centre value. A fixed-speed actuator sees a rapidly increasing load as the film narrows. Conversely a constant load makes the approach slower. An experiment must state which of speed or force is controlled before two records can be compared.

Worked example · halve a film thickness

Let \(R=20\) mm, \(h=0.20\) mm, \(\mu=0.10\) Pa s and \(V=0.10\) mm/s. The centre excess is

\[3(0.10)(10^{-4})(0.020)^2/(0.00020)^3=1500\,\mathrm{Pa}.\]

The net force is 0.942 N. Reducing the gap to 0.10 mm at unchanged speed raises pressure and force by eight, giving 12.0 kPa and 7.54 N. Reducing speed by eight at the thinner gap would recover the original force. The model consequently distinguishes a viscous support from a spring by repeating the same gap at different rates.

Worked example · integrate the approach at constant load

Rearrange the force law to \(-dh/dt=2Fh^3/(3\pi\mu R^4)\). Separation of variables gives

\[t=[3\pi\mu R^4/(4F)](h_f^{-2}-h_i^{-2}).\]

Under a constant 1.00 N load the example disk takes 2.83 s to go from 0.20 to 0.10 mm. The ideal time to reach zero mathematical thickness diverges. Real surfaces eventually develop solid contact at roughness peaks, and departures from parallel alignment can redirect much of the pressure distribution.

First halve the gap and then independently halve the approach speed. Predict how the centre pressure changes in each trial. Check the area-average line against the radial curve and explain why an unweighted average over radius gives a different value.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · identify a viscosity and test the boundary assumption

At gap 0.15 mm, disks of radius 15 mm give 2.00 N at 0.25 mm/s. Infer the viscosity. Predict the load if a vented outer annulus reduces the effective wetted radius to 12 mm while the gap and velocity stay fixed.

Integrate over the wetted radius

The inverse is \(\mu=2Fh^3/(3\pi V R^4)=0.113\) Pa s. With the edge now open to ambient at 12 mm, force scales by \((12/15)^4=0.4096\), giving 0.819 N. Simply multiplying the original force by the area ratio would miss the change in peak pressure caused by the shorter flow path.

Chapter 18 · How turbulent momentum transport produces a logarithmic velocity profile

The time-averaged velocity next to a wall looks smooth, even when individual velocity records fluctuate rapidly. Decompose the momentum flux, derive the wall scaling, and recover a shear stress from two measurements in the logarithmic region.

Where the turbulent shear stress comes from

For an incompressible constant-density fluid write \(u=U+u'\) and \(v=V+v'\), with uppercase velocities time averages and mean fluctuations zero. The flux of streamwise momentum carried in the wall-normal direction contains \(\rho\,\overline{uv}=\rho(UV+\overline{u'v'})\). In a statistically steady parallel mean flow with \(V=0\), the apparent shear transporting streamwise momentum toward the wall becomes

\[\tau_{\rm total}=\mu {dU\over dy}-\rho\,\overline{u'v'} .\]

Upward moving near-wall fluid often carries lower-than-average streamwise momentum and descending fluid carries higher-than-average momentum. Both events have negative \(u'v'\) in this sign convention, producing a positive turbulent shear. At a stationary impermeable no-slip wall fluctuations vanish and the entire wall stress is viscous, even when turbulence dominates a short distance away.

Match the inner scales and a constant-stress layer

Define \(u_\tau=\sqrt{\tau_w/\rho}\), \(y^+=yu_\tau/\nu\) and \(U^+=U/u_\tau\). Close enough to the wall \(dU/dy\simeq\tau_w/\mu\), so \(U^+\simeq y^+\). Farther out, provided y is still small compared with the outer flow thickness, take \(\tau_{\rm total}\simeq\tau_w\) and viscous stress small. Prandtl's mixing-length closure approximates the turbulent shear by \(\rho \ell_m^2|U'|U'\). The near-wall dimensional assumption \(\ell_m=\kappa y\) gives

\[{dU\over dy}\simeq{u_\tau\over\kappa y},\]
\[U^+={1\over\kappa}\ln y^+ + B .\]

The constant B must come from matching or measurements. Use illustrative smooth-wall values \(\kappa=0.41,\ B=5.0\). The line \(U^+=y^+\) applies in the viscous sublayer and the log line only in an overlap region (often beyond about \(y^+=30\) and well inside the outer layer). In between lies a buffer region. Extending a logarithm to the wall would violate the no-slip condition. Roughness and adverse pressure gradients alter these comparisons.

Predict a difference, then use it as a stress measurement

Take water with \(\rho=1000\) kg/m³, \(\nu=10^{-6}\) m²/s and \(u_\tau=0.040\) m/s. Wall stress is 1.60 Pa. At \(y=0.05\) mm, \(y^+=2\), so the viscous prediction is \(U=0.080\) m/s. At 1.0 mm and 4.0 mm, \(y^+=40,160\). If both points are in the overlap region, their velocity difference is \((0.040/0.41)\ln4=0.1353\) m/s; B cancels. The individual predicted speeds are about 0.560 and 0.695 m/s. A log-linear plot over many positions is a stronger test than fitting a line to just two values.

Suppose two probes 0.80 and 3.20 mm from a similar wall instead read a difference of 0.100 m/s. The inferred friction velocity is \(0.41(0.100)/\ln4=0.0296\) m/s and the inferred stress is 0.875 Pa. The lower probe then has \(y^+=23.7\), which makes the logarithmic assumption questionable there. This check feeds the inferred parameter back into the condition for the inference. Repositioning both probes at larger wall distances, while staying away from the outer flow, supplies an independent test.

Increase viscosity while holding friction velocity fixed. Predict how the two displayed validity intervals move in millimetres. Use the reported wall distances in viscous units to decide whether a logarithmic fit through fixed probes is still plausible.

A fully developed round pipe of radius 25 mm has \(-dp/dx=160\) Pa/m. Derive its wall shear by balancing axial pressure force and perimeter friction. Predict the increase between two overlap-layer points whose distances have a ratio of three.

Derive the wall force and velocity increment

For pipe length L the pressure force is \((-dp/dx)L\pi R^2\), balanced by \(\tau_w2\pi RL\), hence

\[\tau_w=(-dp/dx)R/2=2.0\,\mathrm{Pa}.\]

In water \(u_\tau=0.0447\) m/s. The expected speed increase is

\[u_\tau\ln3/0.41=0.120\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

The pressure-force result remains an exact mean balance under the fully developed assumptions. The profile comparison additionally depends on the closure and the placement of both probes.

Chapter 19 · How gravity and surface tension select different wave speeds

A long crest and a millimetre-scale ripple on the same deep liquid can travel at different speeds. Derive their dispersion relation from two surface boundary conditions and distinguish the motion of a crest from the motion of a narrow packet.

Continue the surface motion down into the liquid

Use z positive upward, liquid occupying \(z<0\), and an undisturbed planar free surface at zero. Neglect viscosity and consider small-amplitude irrotational motion. The velocity is \(\nabla\phi\); incompressibility requires

\[\nabla^2\phi=0.\]

A horizontal Fourier component \(e^{i(kx-\omega t)}\) with \(k>0\) therefore has vertical dependence \(e^{kz}\) in water deep compared with \(1/k\). The other Laplace solution grows without bound as \(z\to-\infty\) and is discarded. Let \(\eta\) denote surface elevation. Linearising the kinematic condition at z=0 gives \(\eta_t=\phi_z\), since the surface moves with the normal fluid velocity.

Translate curvature and elevation into surface pressure

For small slope the liquid surface pressure above atmospheric is \(-\gamma\eta_{xx}\), positive beneath a crest. Linear Bernoulli with the hydrostatic shift at the rising surface gives \(\phi_t+g\eta-(\gamma/\rho)\eta_{xx}=0\) at z=0. For amplitudes \(\Phi,\hat\eta\), the kinematic condition gives

\[-i\omega\hat\eta=k\Phi.\]

Insert this in the dynamic condition to eliminate \(\Phi\):

\[\omega^2=gk+{\gamma\over\rho}k^3,\]
\[c_p={\omega\over k}=\sqrt{g/k+\gamma k/\rho},\]
\[c_g={d\omega\over dk}={g+3\gamma k^2/\rho\over2\omega}.\]

For long deep-water gravity waves \(c_g\simeq c_p/2\). In the opposite capillary limit \(c_g\simeq3c_p/2\). Thus tracking the envelope of a short group and timing an individual crest can give different speeds even with accurate instruments. Finite depth would multiply the right-hand side for \(\omega^2\) by \(\tanh(kH)\); deep-water long-wave asymptotics still require \(kH\gg1\).

Find the slowest crests and time a wave packet

For water take \(\rho=1000\) kg/m³ and \(\gamma=0.072\) N/m. Differentiating \(c_p^2\) with respect to k gives a stationary point at \(k_*=\sqrt{\rho g/\gamma}\), so

\[\lambda_*=2\pi\sqrt{\gamma/(\rho g)}=17.0\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Here gravity and capillarity contribute equally to \(\omega^2\); the minimum phase speed is 0.231 m/s. Also \(d(kc_p)/dk=c_p+k\,dc_p/dk=c_p\) at this point. Thus group and phase velocities coincide locally.

At 100 mm wavelength the predicted phase and group speeds are 0.401 and 0.212 m/s. At 10 mm they are 0.247 and 0.307 m/s. Over a 0.50 m path a narrow 10 mm packet envelope takes about 1.63 s, whereas timing a crest across the same distance would give 2.03 s if it stayed observable. Surface contamination changes effective restoring and damping behaviour, and dispersion broadens a packet with appreciable bandwidth.

Find where phase and group speed meet, then halve the surface tension and predict the new wavelength of that crossing. Time an individual short crest and its packet over half a metre. Which arrives first on either side of the crossing?

Tutorial · recover surface tension from a measured frequency

A sinusoidal mode of wavelength 12.0 mm oscillates at 20.0 Hz in deep water of density 1000 kg/m³. Infer its surface tension using the full relation and estimate the error caused by discarding gravity. Which depth would make the deep-water approximation credible?

Solve for the constitutive parameter

With \(k=523.6\) m⁻¹ and \(\omega=125.66\) rad/s, \(\gamma=\rho(\omega^2-gk)/k^3\simeq0.0742\) N/m. Omitting gravity gives about 0.110 N/m, roughly 48% high. At depth 10 mm, \(kH=5.24\) and \(\tanh(kH)\) exceeds 0.9999, making the finite-depth correction very small in this model. Frequency, wavelength and depth should all be measured before attributing a discrepancy to surface chemistry.

A flat interface can carry waves restored by gravity and surface tension. If its two liquids or gases move at different speeds, a band of those waves can grow. Derive the ideal threshold, identify the most vulnerable wavelength at onset and distinguish growth of an instability from advection of an otherwise stable disturbance.

Chapter 20 · Watch a wetting front approach the capillary height

Dip two clean glass tubes of different inner radii into the same wetting liquid. A microscope following only the first few millimetres of rise can give a different impression of which tube fills more readily than a photograph taken at equilibrium. Start with the force on one meniscus, derive a transient relation including gravity, and learn which measurements can distinguish a change of viscosity from a change of surface condition.

Balance the surface force against a pressure difference

Let \(a\) be the tube radius and \(\theta\) the advancing contact angle measured through the liquid. The vertical component of surface tension along a circular contact line is \(2\pi a\sigma\cos\theta\). Dividing by the cross-sectional area equates this force to a capillary pressure magnitude \(p_c=2\sigma\cos\theta/a\). The liquid pressure immediately below a wetting meniscus is below the gas pressure. An equivalent derivation uses a spherical cap and the Young–Laplace pressure jump with radius of curvature \(a/\cos\theta\). Both derivations require a meniscus whose transverse shape is set mainly by capillarity, so \(a\) should be small compared with the capillary length \(\sqrt{\sigma/(\rho g)}\).

Take \(z\) to be the height of the meniscus above an effectively infinite open reservoir. The bottom and the gas above the meniscus share the same external atmospheric pressure. A static column can sustain the hydrostatic head \(\rho g z\). Equating that head with \(p_c\) gives the equilibrium height \(H=2\sigma\cos\theta/(\rho g a)\). A wider tube has a smaller equilibrium rise. Viscosity does not enter this statement: after flow stops, there is no viscous pressure drop.

This equilibrium construction implicitly neglects the small volume in the curved meniscus, any entrance flow below the reservoir surface, and changes in the contact angle as the wall wets. These corrections matter when the rise is only a few tube radii. They can also matter in comparing two nominally identical capillaries cleaned in different ways. A practical measurement should define whether the microscope tracks the meniscus centre, a contact line or an optical threshold. An inconsistent definition looks like an offset in the inferred height.

Relate the instantaneous capillary force to wall shear

Farther inside a long circular tube the local pressure gradient sets a parabolic velocity profile for steady, fully developed laminar flow. Applying this relation quasi-steadily to a column of length \(z\), the pressure loss is \(8\mu z\,\bar u/a^2\). Conservation of liquid volume in a tube of uniform cross-section identifies \(\bar u=\dot z\). The pressure remaining to drive viscous motion is the capillary jump less the hydrostatic head. Therefore

\[\frac{8\mu z}{a^2}\frac{dz}{dt}=p_c-\rho g z=\rho g(H-z).\]

The driving pressure falls as the column grows, and its hydraulic resistance grows at the same time. These two effects account for the long final approach to equilibrium. Introduce \(B=\rho g a^2/(8\mu)\), which has dimensions of velocity. The resulting first-order equation is

\[\dot z=B(H-z)/z.\]

Close to the entrance it predicts an arbitrarily large speed when \(z\) tends to zero. The approximation has discarded fluid inertia, entrance losses and moving contact-line dynamics. Treat the early viscous law as an intermediate asymptotic once a wetted column exists. A good fit late in the record is no evidence for that divergent initial prediction.

To integrate the differential equation it is simplest to regard time as a function of height. Set \(s=z/H\) and \(\tau=H/B\). Then \(dt/\tau=s\,ds/(1-s)\). Integrate from zero to \(s\), choosing the nominal clock origin at vanishing length. The result is

\[\frac{t}{\tau}=-s-\ln(1-s),\qquad 0\le s<1.\]

The right side is increasing. The notebook uses bisection to invert it for height at a specified laboratory time. The two bracket endpoints have clear physical meanings, zero rise and the equilibrium rise. Near \(s=0\), direct subtraction of \(s\) from \(-\ln(1-s)\) can discard significant digits. Evaluating the logarithm with log1p and using the power series for very small \(s\) preserve the small positive elapsed time.

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Read two distinct time laws from one expression

Expanding the logarithm gives

\[t/\tau=s^2/2+s^3/3+\cdots.\]

To leading order \(z^2=2HBt=a\sigma\cos\theta\,t/(2\mu)\). At a common early time, a wider tube therefore has a larger height proportional to \(\sqrt a\). It has less capillary pressure but its resistance per unit length falls rapidly with radius. An experiment that accelerates the initial uptake by increasing radius can simultaneously reduce the final available lifting height.

Near equilibrium write \(s=1-\epsilon\). The implicit solution reads \(t/\tau=-1+\epsilon-\ln\epsilon\). To leading order \(\epsilon\simeq\exp[-1-t/\tau]\). The final height is approached exponentially and is never reached in finite time by this idealisation. Fitting a straight line in \(z^2\) against \(t\) across this late part of the record will overestimate the viscosity if gravity is omitted, because it attributes the slower rise entirely to viscous resistance. A semilog plot of \(H-z\) is a more appropriate late-time diagnostic, although uncertainty in \(H\) becomes more important as the difference shrinks.

Interactive calculation · Capillary rise

Change the physical and numerical inputs of the notebook model. Compare the updated curves and balance checks with the recorded notebook figures. Each plot uses the same equations and assumptions documented in that notebook; its readout names any analytical or reduced-model reference.

Download this example’s editable notebook

For \(a=100\) µm, \(\mu=0.012\) Pa s, \(\rho=1000\) kg/m³, \(\sigma=0.060\) N/m and \(\theta=30^\circ\), the predicted static height is 105.94 mm. The time scale is about 103.7 s. Reaching 25%, 50% and 90% of that height takes approximately 3.91, 20.02 and 145.40 s. The time to half height is not half the time to a specified near-equilibrium height. At half height the exact dimensionless time is

\[\ln2-1/2=0.19315.\]

The leading square-root estimate gives only 0.125 and underestimates the time by about 35% relative to the exact result.

Use a static observation and a moving observation together

In a high-viscosity sample a single short-time image constrains only the combination \(\sigma\cos\theta/\mu\) if all you have is the early growth rate. A change of surface chemistry and a change of viscosity can produce the same slope. A static-height measurement independently fixes \(\sigma\cos\theta\) for a known radius, density and gravity. Combining it with the time to half height gives a viscosity estimate \(\mu=\rho ga^2t_{1/2}/[8H(\ln 2-1/2)]\). The two measurements have different parameter dependences, which is what makes the inverse calculation possible.

Consider the limitations of this estimate. A slowly falling reservoir shifts the elevation origin and changes the effective head. Evaporation from the front can drive a persistent flow even with an apparently stationary meniscus. A wall with contact-angle hysteresis can hold a range of static heights. Repeating the experiment after prewetting the tube changes which contact angle is relevant. Observing both the growing profile and the final plateau helps locate these effects. Reducing the residual of a single two-parameter fit can otherwise conceal them.

Estimate viscosity using only passage times at two marks

A camera need not capture the instant of immersion if the tube carries two calibrated height marks. Select marks at \(z_1=0.25H\) and \(z_2=0.50H\) using an independent long-time reading of \(H\). If the camera clock has an unknown constant offset, subtract the recorded times. The offset cancels exactly. With \(F(s)=-s-\ln(1-s)\) the model gives

\[t_2-t_1=\tau[F(0.50)-F(0.25)].\]

The bracket is about 0.15547. A measured interval of 16.11 s therefore gives \(\tau=103.6\) s. Substituting this value into \(\mu=\rho g a^2\tau/(8H)\) gives approximately 0.0120 Pa s for the stated dimensions. The two-mark construction does not cancel a time-varying camera rate or an error in the static height.

Where should the marks be placed? Two marks very close together make the absolute timing uncertainty large relative to the measured interval. Setting the second mark extremely near equilibrium can also be troublesome because a small height uncertainty then produces a large uncertainty in passage time. Differentiate the dimensionless time to obtain

\[F'(s)=s/(1-s).\]

At half height its slope is one, while at ninety percent it is nine. The same one-percent error in fractional height produces approximately nine times as much time uncertainty at the latter mark as at half height. Use the movie to estimate a passage-time uncertainty from the meniscus location itself, alongside the uncertainty of one video frame.

The notebook also feeds three specified camera times into OneSimulation’s vectorised bracketed inverse in a local installation. After identifying the corresponding heights, substitute them back into the time law. This checks the inversion independently of a visual match between two trajectories.

Exercises on scaling and interpretation

1. Enlarge the tube radius by 50% while holding all material parameters fixed. Predict the ratios of final height, initial square-root coefficient and time to half of the new height.

Work through the solution

The static height is inversely proportional to radius, so the height ratio is \(2/3\). The coefficient in \(z=C\sqrt t\) scales as \(\sqrt a\), giving \(C_{\mathrm{new}}/C_{\mathrm{old}}=\sqrt{1.5}=1.225\). The time scale behaves as \(\tau\propto a^{-3}\). Reaching half of the smaller final height now takes about \(20.02/(1.5)^3=5.93\) s. The two experiments reach different absolute heights at their respective half-height times.

2. In a tube of known radius the static rise is unchanged after a fluid is replaced, but the time to 50% height doubles. What quantity changes in this model?

Work through the solution

At fixed density, gravity and radius the observed \(H\) fixes \(\sigma\cos\theta\). The dimensionless time at 50% is fixed, so a doubling of \(t_{1/2}\) doubles \(\tau\) and hence doubles the viscosity. If density also changes, the measurements constrain the kinematic combination \(\mu/\rho\) and the capillary combination \(\sigma\cos\theta/\rho\), so an independent density measurement is needed to identify dynamic viscosity.

3. A plot of \(z^2\) versus time is linear over the first recorded interval. Does that identify the contact angle uniquely?

Work through the solution

Its slope is \(a\sigma\cos\theta/(2\mu)\) in the gravity-free viscous regime. Knowing only the radius leaves several unknown factors. Independent measurements of viscosity and surface tension would give \(\cos\theta\). Even then, a dynamic advancing angle could differ from the static angle, so compare with the plateau and inspect whether the inferred angle varies with the chosen fit interval.

Read the original capillary-flow argument

Washburn’s paper The Dynamics of Capillary Flow develops the connection between viscous motion and capillary pressure. In comparing radii, first decide whether the experimental observable is a penetration length, a transported volume or a fraction of equilibrium height.

Problem sheet 7 · A capillary that fills horizontally and climbs vertically

A transparent tube draws a wetting liquid from a large reservoir. Combine a static meniscus balance with a viscous pressure-drop law to predict a transient, recover a property from timing and determine where the simplest startup model fails.

1 · Predict the final height before timing the rise

The inner radius is 0.200 mm, surface tension 0.072 N/m, contact angle 30°, density 1000 kg/m³ and dynamic viscosity 0.010 Pa s. Neglect gas viscosity and assume the radius is small relative to the capillary length. Calculate the pressure head supplied by the meniscus and the final vertical rise.

Calculate pressure and static elevation

A spherical-cap meniscus gives

\[\Delta p=2\gamma\cos\theta/r=624\,\mathrm{Pa}.\]

Hydrostatic pressure balances it at \(H=\Delta p/(\rho g)=63.6\) mm. Viscosity does not enter this equilibrium balance. A liquid with the same density and surface properties but twice the viscosity therefore arrives at the same final level under these assumptions.

Read the 0.20 mm capillary radius on the equilibrium curve

2 · Turn a pressure drop into a filling-time law

First lay the tube horizontally. Denote the filled length by x. In developed laminar flow the mean speed is \(r^2\Delta p/(8\mu x)\). With constant tube area, the speed of the meniscus is that mean speed. Predict the time to fill 50 mm and the effect of doubling viscosity.

Integrate the resistance that grows with the filled length

Equating \(\dot x\) to the mean liquid speed gives

\[x\dot x=r^2\Delta p/(8\mu).\]

Integrating from negligible initial length gives

\[x^2=\gamma r\cos\theta\,t/(2\mu).\]

The time for 50 mm is

\[2(0.010)(0.050)^2/[0.072(0.00020)\cos30^\circ]=4.01\,\mathrm{s}.\]

Doubling viscosity doubles the filling time. At x=25 mm the prediction gives about 0.0125 m/s, with Reynolds number based on diameter about 0.50. Laminar resistance is then plausible. Extrapolating its infinite startup speed to zero length would neglect entrance inertia and the formation of the velocity profile.

3 · Predict a measurable fraction of the vertical rise

Turn the tube upright. Only \(\Delta p-\rho g x\) remains available for viscous losses. Derive a time formula for \(0<x<H\). Find the time to H/2 and compare with the time needed to reach that same length horizontally.

Solve the separable moving-front equation

Write \(K=\rho gr^2/(8\mu)=0.004905\) m/s, which gives

\[\dot x=K(H/x-1).\]

Integration of \(dt=x\,dx/[K(H-x)]\) yields

\[t(x)={1\over K}\left[H\ln{H\over H-x}-x\right].\]

At half height this becomes

\[(H/K)(\ln2-1/2)=2.50\,\mathrm{s}.\]

Horizontal filling over the same 31.8 mm takes 1.62 s. The logarithm diverges as \(x\to H\), so a laboratory definition such as “within 1 mm of the final level” is necessary when reporting an arrival time.

4 · Recover viscosity independently of contact angle

Suppose a fresh tube of the same radius gives static rise 60.0 mm and fills 40.0 mm horizontally in 3.00 s. Infer the viscosity without fitting a contact angle. Which pair of measurements lets you test whether static and dynamic wetting agree?

Eliminate the unknown capillary pressure

The vertical rise yields

\[\Delta p=\rho gH=588.6\,\mathrm{Pa}.\]

Horizontal integration gives \(\mu=r^2\Delta p\,t/(4x^2)=0.0110\) Pa s. Compare this inferred value with independent viscometry and record the meniscus angle during travel. A moving contact angle different from the static value changes the pressure available during the horizontal run. A time trace at several lengths also tests the assumed \(x^2\propto t\) scaling.

Problem sheet 8 · Calibrate a rotational viscometer without a thin-gap assumption

An inner cylinder rotates inside a stationary cylindrical cup filled with a Newtonian liquid. Instruments often convert torque to viscosity with a gap approximation. Solve the actual cylindrical velocity field, use the measured torque to infer viscosity and determine how end drag or secondary flow would appear in a calibration series.

Find the velocity field from stress balance

Take inner radius \(a=15\) mm, outer radius \(b=20\) mm and active length \(L=80\) mm. The inner cylinder rotates at \(\Omega=5.0\) rad/s; the outer one is fixed. Assume steady axisymmetric flow with no radial or axial velocity, no slip and negligible end effects. Why is the shear rate not simply the derivative of tangential velocity? Derive the velocity between the two walls.

Compare neighbouring angular velocities

A fluid rotating as a rigid body with \(u_\theta=\Omega r\) has nonzero radial velocity gradient but zero shear deformation. The cylindrical shear rate that respects this fact is \(r\,d(u_\theta/r)/dr\). The torque transmitted through a coaxial cylinder is \(2\pi Lr^2\tau_{r\theta}\). In a steady purely azimuthal flow with no body torque this is independent of radius. For Newtonian viscosity \(\mu\), integrate \(r^3\,d(u_\theta/r)/dr=\text{constant}\) to find

\[u_\theta=Ar+B/r.\]

Applying both wall speeds gives

\[u_\theta(r)=\frac{\Omega a^2}{b^2-a^2}\left(\frac{b^2}{r}-r\right).\]

It returns \(a\Omega=0.075\) m/s at the inner wall and zero at the outer wall. Mid-gap speed is approximately 0.0344 m/s rather than the 0.0375 m/s of a straight line. A small-looking velocity discrepancy can have a larger effect on stress because stress contains a derivative and a curvature correction.

Infer viscosity from the measured torque

The measured sidewall torque after bearing drag subtraction is 0.646 mN m. Determine the viscosity, the magnitude of inner wall shear stress and the result a laboratory would get using a planar gap model. Identify which direction the resulting viscosity bias takes.

Integrate shear traction with the lever arm

Differentiating the velocity solution yields

\[\tau_{r\theta}=-2\mu\Omega a^2b^2/[(b^2-a^2)r^2].\]

The liquid resists the inner cylinder, so the applied steady drive torque is positive and has magnitude

\[T=\frac{4\pi\mu L\Omega a^2b^2}{b^2-a^2}.\]

The measurement implies \(\mu=0.250\) Pa s. Inner stress is

\[T/(2\pi La^2)=5.71\,\mathrm{Pa}.\]

A planar gap estimate uses wall speed \(a\Omega\) divided by \(b-a\) as the shear rate. It predicts 0.424 mN m at the true viscosity and would infer 0.381 Pa s from the actual reading. The sign of bias follows from the cylindrical shear being larger than the assumed planar rate at the inner surface.

Reducing gap while keeping inner radius fixed makes both approximations approach one another. The trade-off is that fractional uncertainty in the actual clearance can become large relative to machining tolerances or eccentricity. Measuring the two radii independently to good absolute precision need not yield an accurately known very small difference.

Check the power balance and use an active-length sweep

Predict the mechanical power entering the liquid. Show that it equals the viscous dissipation integrated over the annulus. How could torque at two lengths separate the intended sidewall response from an approximately length-independent end contribution?

Integrate power and isolate the intercept

Driving power is \(T\Omega=3.23\) mW. Local dissipation density is \(\mu[r\,d(u_\theta/r)/dr]^2\). Multiplying by \(2\pi rL\,dr\) leaves an integral proportional to \(\int_a^b dr/r^3\), giving exactly the drive power after substitution of both radii. This check tests both the factor of two and the sign convention in the torque derivation. It also predicts a small but continuous heat load whose importance depends on the sample's thermal control.

If lengths 40 and 80 mm yield measured torques 0.373 and 0.696 mN m, the slope is 8.08 mN m per metre and extrapolated end intercept 0.050 mN m. The inferred 80 mm sidewall contribution is 0.646 mN m as assumed. This correction relies on similar end geometry and sidewall flow at both lengths. A varying free surface, slip, secondary recirculation or contact-line torque can change that intercept with immersion.

Open this experiment online

Distinguish non-Newtonian behaviour from a failing flow geometry

The graph lets you change viscosity while monitoring the profile shape and computed torque. Explain what an observed nonlinear relationship between torque and angular speed would mean and what it would fail to identify by itself.

Test independent model assumptions

In the assumed Newtonian steady regime, wall speeds set the entire velocity shape independently of viscosity, while torque scales linearly with viscosity and rotation rate. At fixed geometry, a nonlinear torque curve can suggest a shear-dependent viscosity. It can also result from heating, a moving contact line, wall slip, inertia-driven secondary circulation or a changing parasitic bearing contribution. Repeat at controlled temperature and multiple active lengths, acquire upward and downward speed sweeps and measure reversibility after full equilibration.

The inner rotating configuration can develop Taylor vortices above an onset that depends on geometry and fluid properties. A torque departure accompanied by axial cellular motion violates the assumed single azimuthal component. A velocity profile measured near the cylinder centre and away from its ends supplies information unavailable in one total torque. In the benign low-speed calibration one can also use a reference liquid of known viscosity and repeat for different gaps; collapse onto the finite-gap prediction is a stronger test than fitting a single instrument factor.
